#10583. How Healthcare AI's Early Wins Can Have A Lasting Impact

Dr. Paige Kilian, Chief Medical Officer at Inovalon, bridges clinical medicine and data analytics to improve real-world healthcare outcomes.

Everyone is trying to forecast AI’s effects on healthcare. Some believe it will finally free medical professionals from mundane administrative tasks so they can spend more time with patients.

Others worry it will erode the hands-on expertise required to deliver comprehensive, compassionate medical care. As AI creates the illusion of an easy approach to diagnosis, physicians will have fewer opportunities to learn by experience, potentially causing overreliance on technology at the expense of patient outcomes. Autopilot could not have landed Captain Sully’s plane.

Neither reflects what’s happening in most encounters today. Right now, physicians are overloaded with nonclinical tasks, patients are waiting too long for care and the playing field continues shifting as more regulations are announced and new technologies enter this space.

Fortunately, there are clear examples of AI catalyzing the change healthcare has long needed. I’ve observed them in my own day-to-day work. Models have produced exciting wins in taking on time-consuming, tedious tasks to help skilled experts make patient encounters more meaningful and, in some areas, produce better outcomes. AI scribes and other ambient documentation tools are giving physicians back time to focus on patients, and AI-based alerts are catching patient safety risks earlier.

The challenge is embracing innovation while preserving the art of taking the patient’s history, factoring in the nuance of expression and inflection and performing a solid physical exam; AI disruption has to happen in parallel to continued medical learning.

Forty-two percent of physicians report experiencing burnout, with administrative work as a primary contributor, a top reason why many physicians, nurses and pharmacists are leaving their professions.

Ambient AI can help ease documentation burden and give physicians time back to focus on patients. Doctors can turn away from the computer, look patients in the eye and still capture notes for later. For non-English-speaking patients, ambient AI could also be a valuable tool in improving communication with their providers.

The result is a more meaningful interaction without sacrificing the documentation accuracy needed to inform billing, quality measurement reporting and risk adjustment programs. In one study, clinician burnout fell 13% after 30 days of ambient use, and another study found that ambient scribes reduced cognitive load for physicians.

If agentic AI is added to providers’ toolkit, it can further offset administrative burden by helping appeal denials or complete prior authorizations. This is one area where early wins are signaling for healthcare teams to move faster. Thoughtful increased use of agentic AI can cut the costly inefficiencies taking up time and resources, allowing clinical teams to see their next patient sooner without sacrificing documentation needs for clinical care, quality reporting and prior authorizations.

In one study comparing AI-based recommendations for empiric antibiotics against what clinicians prescribed, the model chose the correct antibiotic as well as, or better than, the clinicians’ choices—and accounted for resistance profiles and chose narrower-spectrum antibiotics, reducing the risk of antibiotic-resistant infections.

In a cardiovascular health clinical trial, AI-enabled electrocardiogram (ECG) alerts reduced all-cause mortality rates within 90 days. AI alerts sent physicians a report of patients predicted to be at high risk of mortality, producing a 3.6% mortality rate in the intervention group compared to 4.3% in the control group. Separately, a systematic review of 11 randomized cardiovascular AI clinical trials found that five contributed to improvements in clinical events, six produced enhanced diagnostic accuracy and early detection and three yielded better resource utilization.

As AI is increasingly used in patient care, healthy skepticism must remain to prevent it from curtailing the experience on either side. Clinical experts still have to build their knowledge and apply best practices to each patient’s unique needs while also protecting patient comfort. AI might influence how they express themselves, making the combination of skilled medical practice and attuned bedside manner even more important.

For example, a patient who knows AI is transcribing their conversation may be less confident that the encounter is confidential. They might hold back or answer questions less honestly, and physicians need to catch these subtleties. AI can’t pick up if a patient isn’t looking their doctor in the eye or fidgeting while talking, yet these are the signals that create opportunity to foster connection and uncover important details about a patient’s wellness.

Clinicians must also prevent a diagnostic cascade. If doctors order tests based on AI recommendations, without performing a physical exam as well, one set of imaging could beget another test, more follow-up visits and potentially more invasive testing, which may not be warranted. Tests must be ordered based on sound clinical assessment, including an experienced physical examination that considers the patient’s unique history.

AI can’t replace decision-making in a particular encounter or in the years of practice required to build a personal medical perspective. Physicians must remain dedicated to their craft, with AI as a tool informing them in delivering care—and they should also consider how a patient’s behavior may change when they’re aware that AI is part of their care team.

The more AI takes on healthcare’s operational challenges, the more imperative it is for physicians to reinvest administrative time in continuing to sharpen their clinical skills. AI and human expertise need to progress together, with professionals staying committed to medical principles.

We’re seeing promising examples in lowering burnout and improving patient safety largely because the technology in these instances is taking on the complicated, task-heavy side of care and empowering clinical experts to focus on what the person in front of them actually needs. That’s the ultimate goal of AI use throughout the care journey: building highly skilled AI to collaborate with highly skilled humans to deliver the highest caliber, most compassionate healthcare possible.

Humans must remain at the controls of healthcare delivery, capitalizing on all the benefits technology offers while keeping it in an appropriate, clinical and caring context. There is no role for autopilot in the healthcare journey.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#10582. What happens to your online accounts when you die? Here’s how to prepare

What happens to all of your online accounts—credit cards, merchants, news subscriptions, social media, streaming services—after you die? How will your heirs know how to access these accounts or understand what you want done with them? Most people haven’t considered these questions, and this lack of planning can create significant headaches for next of kin.

We studied challenges older adults encounter when thinking about what should happen to their online accounts after they die. We interviewed 21 American adults over age 60 to learn about their concerns, needs, and practices for their online accounts, as well as what would make planning for these accounts easier.

Most of the people we interviewed had hired lawyers or estate planners to draft wills specifying how their money and physical property should be handled after their deaths. They wanted to make sure that heirs had easy access to the money and assets left behind.

People also wanted their relatives to have easy access to their digital estates, but they had not done any formal planning and didn’t know how to plan for transitioning or shutting down online accounts, such as email, social media, and accounts for online bills. Some told us they hadn’t thought about their online accounts at all. Others said they assumed heirs would know what to do, or they assumed that giving heirs the passwords to accounts would be enough to grant access to their digital estates.

Unfortunately, more planning is typically necessary to smoothly transition a digital estate.

Passing down a list of passwords

The older adults we interviewed mainly kept track of their passwords by hand: They wrote their passwords down on paper, or they stored them in a digital file. Almost everyone had already shared some of their passwords with family members who help them manage their accounts. Everyone also believed that their heirs would be able to find their list of passwords after their death.

The people we interviewed weren’t always happy with their current systems for keeping track of their passwords. Some people complained that when accounts required frequent password changes, it was difficult to keep their password lists up to date. As a result, they weren’t sure their heirs would get the latest version of all their passwords.

Password managers can be a good way to make sure passwords are up to date and, if set up appropriately, accessible to a designated heir. While most of the people we interviewed used password managers, some said they didn’t save important passwords in them because they didn’t know whether they were secure. However, using a password manager is the most frequent security recommendation from experts, who typically use them to store passwords for all of their accounts.

By using a password manager, you can make sure your designated heir has access to all your latest passwords. Some password managers provide access for survivors by default, such as through time-delayed emergency access features. You can also write the master password down somewhere safe to pass to your heir. Instead of making sure dozens of account passwords are up to date, you only have to manage one.

Passwords alone cannot unlock all accounts

Unfortunately, just handing someone your passwords doesn’t always mean they can log in and manage your account. If the account asks for two-factor authentication, such as through a code sent to a phone or email, a password alone won’t grant access. And if the email account is closed or the phone number is no longer active after you die, then they might be out of luck.

To make sure your heirs aren’t locked out, identify the devices and accounts that can act as keys for heirs to unlock the rest of your online accounts, and direct your heirs to keep them open until other accounts have been settled. This not only makes sure that heirs can get authentication codes, but also lets heirs reset passwords to your other accounts if they can’t find the password.

In some cases, passwords might not be necessary. Some online services, such as Facebook and Google, have features that let you choose someone to manage your account or get your data after you die. However, not all platforms offer this option, and few of the older adults we interviewed were aware of these features.

Online accounts don’t automatically die with you

Like many of the people we interviewed, you might also assume that heirs will properly deal with accounts with money and important data, while unimportant accounts can be ignored. But even if there is nothing important in an account, it can still be used by hackers for identity theft, spam, or fraudulent activities. Automatic monthly charges, to cover the cost of streaming services or annual billing for magazine subscriptions, for example, might also continue for a long time.

Without instructions, the people managing your online accounts after you die might not think to close those accounts. They might also find it hard to guess your intentions.

You can leave instructions for your heirs by going through your accounts and noting which ones can be closed outright, which ones require final payments before closure, and which ones might require other actions. You can also periodically tidy up and close accounts you are no longer using to keep your list of accounts more manageable.

Planning ahead for you and your heirs

While it seems simple to just let your heirs handle your important online accounts and let the others slowly fade into nonexistence after you die, the reality is often more complex.

Your online accounts will live on after you die. Planning ahead for what happens to those accounts can reduce the stress on your heirs and ensure your digital accounts are handled according to your wishes.

Jenny Tang is a PhD student in societal computing at Carnegie Mellon University.

Xiaoyuan (Owen) Wu is a PhD student in societal computing at Carnegie Mellon University.

Lujo Bauer, Nicolas Christin, and Lorrie Cranor contributed to this article.

This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.

#10579. The Next Cyber Insurance Claim May Start With Authorized AI

Jay Bavisi is the Founder, Chairman, and Group CEO of EC-Council, a global leader in AI governance and cybersecurity education.

​In the current insurance landscape, underwriters know how to ask about stolen credentials, ransomware, unauthorized access, data theft and the controls that reduce those risks. That work is still needed. In fact, the cyber insurance industry has spent decades getting better at measuring compromise.

In a world where IBM’s “Cost of a Data Breach Report 2026” put the global average breach cost at $4.99 million, up 12% from the prior year, AI agents create a different level of exposure because a system can create material loss while using valid credentials, following an approved workflow and operating inside the authority the business gave it.​

Gartner predicts that by 2028, one-third of interactions with generative AI services will use action models and autonomous agents to complete tasks. As more of those agents move into business workflows, cyber risk will include what authorized systems are allowed to do when they are operating normally.​

Two companies can use the same model and carry very different levels of risk. One may only read information and recommend a next step. Another may send emails, approve transactions, modify customer records, change identity policies or call another automated system. For insurers, model choice is only part of the assessment. The authority attached to the system determines how a mistake becomes a loss.​

Every AI agent should have an authority budget, meaning the maximum set of consequential actions it can take without human approval. That budget should be visible to the business, the security team and, where the use case is material, the insurer. Nonhuman identities, OAuth tokens, API keys, service accounts and privileged access management will become financially relevant because they determine where the agent can go and what it can execute. More privilege means a larger potential blast radius, but more autonomy is not automatically more valuable.​

The question should be whether the marginal utility of giving an agent more authority justifies the additional exposure. If a recommendation produces most of the business value, allowing autonomous execution may add little utility while significantly increasing the consequence of failure.​

Recent cybersecurity evaluations show why this matters. OpenAI disclosed that models circumvented controls and reached external systems, including Hugging Face. Anthropic has separately reported Claude models gaining unauthorized access to real third-party systems during evaluations. These were not ordinary enterprise deployments, so they should not be treated as forecasts of future claims. They do, however, show how quickly capability, connectivity and permissions can combine once autonomous systems are given room to act.​

That creates a problem for static underwriting. An agent can gain new permissions, connect to additional systems or reveal new behaviors after a policy is bound. The annual policy may not have changed, but the authority budget has.​

An AI policy cannot show whether an agent’s authority is actually bounded. Before deployment, the approval process should connect the business case to the access being granted, the controls being tested and the human review that remains in place for higher-risk actions. This is where responsible AI has to move from principle to practice.​

The value of any framework depends on whether it helps teams document those choices in a way security leaders, boards and insurers can review. For insurers, that evidence may become as important as traditional security controls. A company should be able to show how machine authority was requested, approved, tested, logged and revoked as the agent’s role changes. Without that record, an AI policy says little about the risk created when an authorized system makes the wrong call.​

There are two questions I would urge all leaders to ask: What is the most consequential action our AI can take without human approval? Has that authority expanded since the risk was assessed?​

If the authority budget has expanded, the financial exposure has expanded with it. That is the point cyber insurance will have to absorb. In the agentic AI space, security posture is no longer the whole story. The ability to prove that machine authority is intentional, bounded, observable and reversible may become just as important to insurability as proving that the network itself is secure. That leads me to the main question we should ask: Can you tell me what your AI agent did last week?​

#10578. Am I going to be fired by AI? How corporate America is being seduced by AI and algorithms in the ‘forever layoffs’ era

For five years, the former Meta employee had been feted as a high performer by her managers. She had even received a rare equity award in recognition of her performance, which had never fallen below an “exceeds expectations” rating. 

Imagine her surprise, then, when she was unceremoniously laid off this past spring.

Perhaps she should have seen the writing on the wall. After she took a medical leave in late 2025, her performance rating slipped. It turned out she’d initially been given an “exceeds expectations” rating, but her manager had been forced to change her rating to fit a bell curve. Her job responsibilities also changed substantially. She had always ranked high on the internal dashboards Meta used to track AI adoption, which categorized its most avid practitioners as “AI first” or “AI native.” But after returning, she noticed her scores had dropped and was startled to realize the dashboards had not taken into account that she was on protected leave. 

Another employee impacted by the layoffs, a former director at Meta, had come to a similar conclusion. He monitored his team’s usage of artificial intelligence closely, almost on a daily basis, and noticed the dashboards had “failed to distinguish employees’ leave from lack of engagement,” as he said in recent legal filings. In other words, there was no way to detect if someone’s ranking had been compromised simply because they were on leave.

In an era of forever layoffs, some tech workers have suspected that an algorithm or AI could be responsible for picking off employees. Now, it seems, those fears may be realized, as captured by a novel lawsuit that the Meta employees brought in July, alongside a number of former colleagues who were also laid off this year. The 26 anonymous plaintiffs made a bold claim: They accused Meta of using AI-enabled systems to determine which employees would be laid off in a major round of cuts this year—and that, as a result, the company had disproportionately targeted people who needed accommodations for disabilities or took parental leave.

Job displacement isn’t the only consequence of widespread AI adoption. A Fast Company LinkedIn poll reveals . . .

that the fears of being ousted by AI are palpable, and not just for disgruntled tech workers. More than two-thirds of people surveyed said they were worried about their company deploying AI to select employees for layoffs. Of more than 480 respondents, 37% said they were “very” concerned about the prospect of AI being used to that end.

For months, Meta has strenuously denied the claims laid out in the lawsuit, insisting humans were involved throughout the layoff selection process. In one declaration, Linh Doan, an HR leader, stressed that “AI did not select anyone for termination. AI did not assist anyone in making selection decisions.”

But those kinds of proclamations may be too little, too late. Workers know their employers already use automated tools to collect all kinds of data that informs hiring and promotion decisions. Major companies like JPMorgan Chase, Disney, and, of course, Meta are now enlisting AI to draft self-assessments and performance reviews. People analytics tools often track absenteeism and sick days, an exercise in productivity theater that employers nonetheless use to evaluate worker output—even though relying on that kind of data could harm people who take leave. 

So it’s not hard to imagine that all this data could influence layoff decisions, as companies are armed with more information on their employees than ever before. Some managers and HR leaders have said as much in anonymous surveys, where they admit to using AI to trim head count. A handful of states have already passed legislation that will force companies to be more transparent about how AI factors into termination decisions, in anticipation of the potential impact on workers. Meanwhile, high-profile lawsuits against HR tech companies like Workday and Eightfold indicate that we are just beginning to understand how AI use could perpetuate discrimination in other stages of the employment cycle. 

As AI becomes more pervasive across corporate America, it is increasingly difficult for workers to assess the extent to which it may be used in layoff decisions and how all the data companies are hoovering up could be wielded against them. Perhaps the real concern is not whether AI is conducting layoffs—but that there is so little visibility into how AI is radically transforming the way companies make decisions about their workforce. 

The precedent for automating layoffs

As companies ramp up pressure on employees to adopt AI, and layoffs become a regular feature across workplaces, it seems only natural that the technology may start to influence how companies approach some of the most difficult decisions they have to make about their workforce. 

“Making layoff decisions is an impossible problem because you are not only getting rid of people, but you’re also changing the structure of the work itself in ways that are very hard to predict,” says Richard Landers, a professor of industrial and organizational psychology at the University of Minnesota. “It makes using external tools very attractive [because] some of that responsibility is offloaded.”

While there is limited evidence that large employers are explicitly using AI tools to generate lists of employees to lay off, the idea of productivity metrics or automated systems driving employment decisions is hardly new. 

“There has always been to a degree some form of measurement, explicit or implicit, that goes into layoffs,” says Joseph Fuller, a Harvard Business School professor of management. He points to the layoffs Ford conducted in the 1970s by asking supervisors which employees were most productive or spent more hours in the office—the type of metrics companies can now easily monitor through swipe card data or email traffic analysis. “Using AI to make those decisions is really in its infancy,” he says. 

Warehouse workers are already subject to more monitoring and can automatically lose their jobs for not meeting productivity quotas at companies like Amazon. Gig workers at Uber and DoorDash can be automatically suspended or find their accounts deactivated with little human oversight; in fact, Uber is facing a fine of nearly $1 billion in the Netherlands for suspending drivers (though the company plans to appeal).

White-collar workplaces track such data points as sick days and other absences to measure absenteeism, even keeping tabs on disengaged employees who might be a “flight risk.” The COVID-19 pandemic and rise of remote work prompted some companies—among them JPMorgan Chase—to more closely monitor how their employees spent their time, tracking keyboard activity and meeting cadence to evaluate productivity. A New York Times report from 2022 found that eight of the 10 largest private employers in the country collected productivity data on their workers. 

In 2025, the Department of Government Efficiency sought to automate layoffs across the government while making sweeping cuts to the federal workforce. A Reuters report revealed that DOGE revived an old piece of software called AutoRIF, which the government had developed decades before in an attempt to automate layoffs. The tool was deemed too “clunky” to actually use, according to Reuters.

But DOGE reportedly tapped a Tesla engineer to redesign the software to identify federal workers for layoffs, renaming it the “Workforce Reshaping Tool” and disseminating it to federal agencies for testing. (It’s not clear whether the software was involved in making cuts to the federal workforce—or whether AI was used for layoffs in any capacity.)

Multiple experts told Fast Company there are no tools on the market that are expressly designed to conduct layoffs, and in states like California, there are laws in place to govern how companies use AI or automated systems for employment decisions. But that could change soon.

A VC funding flood

In the first quarter of 2026, AI companies reportedly received more than 80% of overall venture capital funding—something like $242 billion—which means plenty of new tools could emerge on the scene. And some companies may simply position their tools in a manner that evades scrutiny. One researcher told Fast Company they had recently approached a people analytics provider to partner on a study examining AI-driven layoffs. The company—which the researcher described as a market leader—ultimately declined to participate because its software and AI-driven products were being branded as workforce management tools, with the implicit acknowledgement that “workforce management is code for layoffs.” 

Once AI becomes embedded in virtually every workflow, it is perhaps unavoidable that termination decisions will draw on inputs from automated systems. In the near future, asking whether AI was involved in the process, as Fuller puts it, “will be the equivalent of saying: ‘Did you use any data in this decision?’”

As workforce management tools grow more sophisticated, companies will likely turn to AI and automation to evaluate individual employees in the context of layoffs. One way employers are already using AI to make cuts, however, is in the context of broader changes to their workforce as they eliminate job functions “in one fell swoop,” according to Fuller.

“Companies are beginning to understand what the first generation of agents can do, and that’s causing them to start adjusting workforces,” he adds. “It’s not so much that they’re doing the layoff because they’ve analyzed individual workers’ performance using AI. It’s because they’ve made a judgment about the durability of an existing job description in light of AI.”

The influence of ‘work intelligence tools’

Some employers are more and more interested in what the Gartner analyst Emily Rose McRae describes as “work intelligence tools,” which are effectively designed to meet this current moment. McRae says these tools claim they can identify which jobs will be most exposed to AI at a given company. “The phrase usually used is ‘impacted,’ or where there is ‘opportunity,’” she explains, “by which they mean where they think you could replace head count.”

In practice, however, these tools—which McRae says some companies are using to determine which jobs should be cut—are not very good at taking into account all the variables that influence whether a job can truly be supplanted with AI, echoing the concerns many economists have expressed.

“It’s appealing to have an external party say, ‘Here’s what the impact will be,’” she says. “It doesn’t feel as personal.”

Large employers tend to have access to more robust tools—whether developed internally or acquired through third-party vendors—as well as legal teams that should, in theory, ensure layoffs don’t inadvertently target specific groups of employees. The concern at smaller or midsize companies is that managers may get limited guidance on layoff decisions or lack access to enterprise grade tools, leaving them to seek out informal help from chatbots like Claude or Copilot.

‘A veneer of objectivity’

Hatim Rahman, a management and organizations professor at Northwestern University, is not surprised to hear that managers are using AI or related tools to make difficult termination decisions. “AI and algorithms give a veneer of objectivity,” he says. “Numbers can be very seductive in providing justification for why certain people can be laid off. The problem is that many times the people who are making decisions about layoffs don’t have an understanding of how the AI tools or workforce analytics that are being used are generating those recommendations or that data.”

If a hiring manager inputs performance review data and asks for layoff recommendations, for example, the chatbot may not consider whether the reviews are capturing the right metrics in the first place. “The thing that’s new is that anybody can do this, which means they don’t have to have any understanding of human resources principles or the relevant laws,” says Tara Behrend, a professor at Michigan State University’s School of Human Resources and Labor Relations.

“The question is: Do you have the right data?” she adds. “Are you making the decisions in a fair way based on things that actually matter, or are you picking up on noise and just sort of spurious nonsense? The recent advances in AI just make it even less apparent whether the data are meaningful—whether they’re picking up on signal or noise.”

‘Some red flags’

When researchers at Rutgers University in New Brunswick, New Jersey, started looking into how AI can shape layoffs at small and midsize companies, they hoped to better understand how reliably chatbots like ChatGPT and Gemini acted when selecting people for layoffs. But even with an engineered prompt, the results were not consistent, according to TJ Raineri, a PhD candidate and one of the researchers working on the study. “There were some red flags that we saw, including overindexing on laying off women and people of color,” he says.

In a subsequent study, Raineri and another researcher, Yixiao Shen, are looking into why this gap exists, and how those results compare to when a human oversees the selection process. “Big companies are getting all the attention, but midsize companies are doing layoffs,” Raineri says. “They might not be on the same scale as Amazon or Meta, but they’re doing layoffs, and I think we need to understand [that].”

Using chatbots or other AI tools could be especially appealing to managers who have never overseen job cuts and might not trust their own instincts. But an overreliance on AI can end up reshaping how they approach those decisions—a concept that Landers, the University of Minnesota professor, describes as abdication to AI. “Over time, a person who is accustomed to making decisions with AI input gradually is likely to find that their decision-making processes have not just been influenced but potentially replaced with that of AI without their knowledge,” says Landers. “And the biases that [those systems] exhibit will essentially creep their way into their judgment without them realizing it.”

The workers who pay the price

The recent lawsuit has forced Meta to disclose an unusual amount of information about how the company conducts layoffs and what selection criteria are taken into consideration. Among those criteria were performance ratings and job profiles, both of which are now being influenced by AI’s growing incursion into workforce management.

The suit also invites uncomfortable questions about how the growing adoption of AI could unintentionally target certain employees, especially as companies become leaner. Perhaps most concerning is that there is so little clarity on how, exactly, bias can be magnified by automated systems—as well as limited insight into how employers arrive at layoff decisions—leaving workers with scant opportunities to raise concerns.

Meta has disputed the claims put forth by the plaintiffs, and the company has explicitly denied that managers took leave history or disability status into consideration during the layoff selection process. But as the employment attorney Christine Webber points out, this could actually make managers more likely to sign off on a termination decision that might have required closer scrutiny. “Their claim of the managers not knowing actually makes it all the more likely that they would rubber-stamp something that really was driven by those excused absences,” says Webber, a partner at Cohen Milstein and cochair of the firm’s civil rights and employment practice.

Even when companies ensure humans are in the loop on critical decisions, they might be implicitly cosigning recommendations made by AI, especially when they’re being tasked with reviewing vast amounts of data. “What I’ve seen commonly in cases involving reliance on AI is this claim that it’s not really making the decisions,” Webber says. “But then, if you look at how quickly we go from the AI’s recommendation to the final decision, there’s not really a lot of room in there for the managers to have done much.”

‘An invisible cage’

As the use of AI and algorithms in the workplace has been normalized, employees are being confined by an “invisible cage,” according to Rahman, the Northwestern professor. This cage effectively controls their “opportunities, success, and layoffs,” but they still have little transparency into how those systems actually work. “It’s that information asymmetry for what data is being collected, how it is being processed, and how it is being used,” he says. “And I think it’s throughout—it’s from hiring and performance to layoffs.”

A few states already have laws in place to address exactly this issue. In addition to a new law in California that creates guardrails around how companies use AI to execute layoffs, both Illinois and Colorado now require companies to disclose when AI dictates a termination decision. In New York, employers who submit WARN notices—federally mandated advance notifications of mass layoffs—must also disclose if a layoff is due to AI adoption.

Employers may have little incentive to disclose how they navigate layoffs beyond what is legally required, but they may also not be particularly well served by offering so little transparency. In the absence of information, workers will fill in the gaps—and likely assume the worst.

“Workers will come up with their own theories of how decisions are being made,” Rahman says. “Organizations that can find the right balance in terms of providing transparency without encouraging gaming or those types of behaviors will hopefully generate more trust and long-term commitment from workers.”

Retaining humanity

Without more transparency, one of the few ways workers can gain insight into how companies operate—and possibly appeal decisions they suspect did not adequately involve human judgment—is through lawsuits like the one brought against Meta. As business leaders urge them to embrace AI and tout sizable investments in the technology, employees have ample reason to question how companies are making consequential decisions about their workforce.

“I think we have to have humanity at the center of all of these decisions,” says Landers. “The lack of human values around the edges of these kinds of decisions is really degrading the experience of work for everyone, which I think we’re feeling right now. . . . One of the reasons why there’s such a mass negative reaction to AI is because most of the use cases are actively removing the humanity from the equation.”

#10576. Why taste, conviction, foresight, and courage are the traits we need in the age of AI

Many call the development of artificial intelligence the Fourth Industrial Revolution, but I disagree. I would say AI is the first, and the last, intelligence revolution.

Every industrial revolution makes something that was once expensive very cheap—power, computation, information. In the AI era, the cost collapse is cognition itself. Imagine you buy a Tesla in 2026 for $39,990, and in two years, the same model costs $200. That’s the price collapse we are now seeing in AI inference, where models turn learned knowledge into actions.

Millions of us are toiling away in jobs that AI can do better and cheaper. I have long worried about this. As a graduate student at Carnegie Mellon University, I asked Hans Moravec, director of the Mobile Robot Laboratory at the time, what would become of human meaning if machines grew smarter than us. He said, “Why do you assume the meaning of humanity must rest on the fact that we are the smartest?”

In the subsequent four decades I have spent building AI, I have come to see four traits that will provide both meaning and mastery for humans in the AI era: taste, foresight, conviction, and courage. All of us must now tap into our latent capabilities here to excel.

Taste

On taste, Coco Chanel is emblematic of how uniquely human dissatisfactions led to a breakthrough in fashion that changed the modern world. As a woman, she knew in her own body that women’s clothing of the era was confining, not a liberation. Her ability to sense dissatisfaction and latent desire before others could articulate them was an act of empathy. AI has no lived physical experience, and it cannot sense a shared human frustration.

Her next move was even more illustrative of the agency that humans uniquely possess: to ask whether women’s clothing could be made as comfortable as men’s, and still be beautiful enough to be desirable. The two were always thought to be mutually exclusive. Ask an AI model to improve a 1910 dress and it will optimize—better cut, better fabric, a cleverer corset. It will never ask whether the corset itself should exist.

Conviction

For conviction, take Jeff Bezos. For years, traditional analysts saw Amazon’s financial performance as a joke, with every sniff of profit being reinvested back into the business. Barron’s ran a cover story entitled “Amazon.bomb” in 1999. Pessimistic analysts behaved much like AI models, cleaving to consensus and past data. Conviction means choosing a direction the evidence does not yet support, then spending years turning it into reality.

Foresight

For foresight, Steve Jobs remains the defining pioneer. His superpower was to create new paradigms and anticipate consumer wants before consumers felt them, from the personal computer and the graphical interface to the iPod and the App Store. At every defining moment of his career, including his time at Pixar, his approach was not to analyze existing data and optimize the status quo. He went out to create a new human need in users, both consumers and professionals. 

Courage

For courage, look to Elon Musk, who invested nearly all of the money he made from PayPal in Tesla, SolarCity, and SpaceX, not even owning a home. By 2008, SpaceX had failed three consecutive Falcon 1 launches. The fourth, his last chance, succeeded, leading to a $1.6 billion NASA contract a few months later. Tesla, hard hit by the credit crunch of 2008, was days away from bankruptcy before a rescuing financing round on Christmas Eve.

Any rational model would advise against such apparent recklessness. And that is the point. Courage is what takes over when stopping is the rational choice. And nothing happens to an AI model that was wrong. It has never lain awake over a difficult decision, and it will not wake up poorer for a bad judgment. No consequence, no courage.

Models are good at brainstorming within the boundaries of what they have learned or been asked. They tend toward the most acceptable answer, or the answer their user wants to hear. Alignment tends to push them further toward a safe middle. But most breakthroughs in business come from contrarian bets at or beyond the margins of imagination, made by stubborn men and women with a stake in the result.

Chanel, Bezos, Jobs, and Musk are generational pioneers. Their survivorship bias weeds out all the entrepreneurs who had courage and conviction but were wrong and faced ruin, or who had excellent judgment and taste, but didn’t capture the cultural moment. The point is that these traits run counter to the very machinery of AI, and in every form and flavor, they will be our enduring strengths, long after the dust has settled.

#10574. Building CRM Automations That Don't Break: APIs, Webhooks, and AI Validation

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Modern businesses rarely struggle with a shortage of software. The bigger problem is getting their existing systems to communicate properly.

A lead arrives through a website form, but the sales team doesn't receive a notification. Someone books an appointment, yet their CRM record isn't updated. A prospect replies to an automated message, but the follow-up sequence continues as though nothing happened.

These are integration problems rather than software problems.

Platforms such as GoHighLevel aim to solve them by combining contact management, messaging, appointment scheduling and workflow automation in one environment. However, the real value comes from how these components are configured and connected to other applications.

For developers, agencies and technical consultants, GoHighLevel provides an interesting example of how low-code automation and API-driven development can work together.

This guide explores the architecture behind an effective CRM automation system, how GoHighLevel's integrations operate, and where custom development makes sense. The patterns below — data normalisation, idempotent event handling, queue-based processing and validated AI outputs — apply to any CRM; GoHighLevel is used here as the running example because it combines native workflows with a public API and webhooks in one platform.

It's tempting to think of automation as a collection of simple rules.

When someone submits a form, send an email. When a customer books a meeting, update their status. When a payment arrives, trigger a notification.

Individually, these are straightforward tasks.

Problems emerge when different workflows start interacting.

Consider a business that generates leads through several channels:

Referral partners and external lead providers

Each channel introduces different information, sometimes in different formats.

One source might supply a full name, phone number and email address. Another provides only a phone number. A third might include campaign information, product preferences or qualification answers.

Without a consistent data structure, CRM records become fragmented. Duplicate contacts appear, important information disappears and automations begin acting on incomplete data.

A well-designed system needs to address these problems before adding more workflow complexity.

GoHighLevel combines native workflow tools with an API that allows developers to integrate external services.

Its core components include contact records, conversations, calendars, opportunities, pipelines and workflow automation.

Developers can interact with supported resources through the platform's REST API, while webhooks provide a mechanism for reacting to events.

Information enters the system through forms, funnels, integrations, inbound communication or API requests.

At this stage, the priority is collecting reliable information and associating it with the correct contact.

Once information has been received, workflows can evaluate conditions and initiate actions.

For example, a newly created lead might trigger an internal notification, a qualification sequence or an appointment invitation.

Some business processes require functionality beyond the CRM's native capabilities.

An organisation might need to synchronise an accounting platform, update an external database or pass information into a proprietary application.

This is where APIs, webhooks and middleware become especially useful.

The important architectural decision is determining which tasks belong inside the CRM and which should be handled externally.

GoHighLevel's developer platform offers REST endpoints for supported operations involving contacts, conversations, calendars, payments and other CRM resources.

Its API uses JSON and supports token-based authentication, including OAuth 2.0 for applications requiring delegated access.

For developers creating an internal integration, a Private Integration Token may be appropriate. Public applications serving multiple accounts generally require a different authentication architecture.

A basic API request follows a familiar pattern:

This example illustrates the structure of a contact-creation request. Production implementations should validate the current endpoint schema, manage tokens securely, handle retries and consider using an upsert operation when duplicate prevention is required.

Two production details deserve attention up front. First, treat a 429 rate-limit response as a normal control signal, not an error: back off exponentially, respect any retry-after value, and never retry in a tight loop. Second, prefer upserts keyed on a stable identifier (a normalised phone number or email address) over blind creates, so a retried request cannot spawn a duplicate contact.

The same principles apply when synchronising information from external applications.

For example, an insurance business might receive enquiries through a specialist quoting system. Rather than manually transferring those records, an integration could normalise the information and send it to the CRM.

The key is to treat the API as part of a broader data architecture, not merely as another way to trigger messages.

APIs are useful when an application needs to retrieve or modify information.

Webhooks address a different requirement: knowing when something has happened.

A webhook-driven architecture might look like this:

A configured webhook sends event data to an external endpoint.

The receiving application verifies and processes the event.

This is particularly useful when connecting CRM activity to internal dashboards, fulfilment systems or custom databases.

However, webhook integrations require careful handling.

A publicly accessible webhook endpoint shouldn't automatically trust every request it receives.

Where the platform provides signature verification, developers should validate the signature using the documented method before processing the payload. In practice this means computing an HMAC over the raw request body with the shared signing secret and comparing it to the signature header in constant time — never parsing the body first and re-serialising it, which can change the bytes the signature was computed over.

If an appointment event triggers an invoice, processing the same event twice could create duplicate invoices.

An idempotent handler should recognise an event that has already been processed and avoid repeating side effects. The reliable way to do this is to store each event's unique ID (or a hash of its payload) in a small processed-events table and check it before running any side effect — a duplicate delivery then becomes a cheap no-op instead of a second invoice.

Long-running operations shouldn't necessarily execute inside the initial webhook request.

A more resilient approach is to validate the event, record it, acknowledge receipt and place any substantial work on a processing queue. Return a 2xx response within a second or two so the sender does not retry, then let a worker handle the slow steps. Failed jobs should retry with exponential backoff and, after a set number of attempts, land in a dead-letter queue where a human can inspect them instead of being retried forever.

This helps prevent slow third-party services from disrupting event handling.

Consider a company receiving enquiries through paid advertising, website forms and telephone calls.

The objective is simple: respond quickly, qualify potential customers and book appointments.

A practical CRM workflow could follow several stages.

Incoming data should be converted into a consistent format.

Phone numbers should be standardised, source information should be preserved, and duplicate detection should be considered before creating another contact.

It's also important to retain communication preferences and the relevant consent information.

Not every lead should be distributed randomly.

Assignment logic might consider geographical territory, product category, team availability or an existing customer relationship.

For more complicated organisations, routing decisions may need to be calculated in an external service.

Once the lead has been assigned, the system can initiate an appropriate follow-up.

This might involve notifying a sales representative, sending a permitted acknowledgement or offering an appointment booking link.

The workflow needs to respond to changing circumstances.

If the prospect books an appointment, unnecessary booking reminders should stop.

If a sales representative takes ownership, automated outreach may need to pause.

If the prospect opts out, the system must respect that preference across relevant communication channels.

Finally, the system should record what happened.

Useful measurements include lead response time, appointment booking rate, appointment attendance and conversion rate.

These metrics make it possible to distinguish a workflow that simply runs from one that improves business operations.

Artificial intelligence introduces additional possibilities, particularly around lead qualification, conversation handling and information extraction.

An AI-powered system might analyse an incoming enquiry, identify the customer's requirements and suggest an appropriate next action.

It could also summarise a conversation for a sales representative or help classify enquiries into different service categories.

However, introducing an AI model creates new engineering considerations.

Allowing an AI model to generate unrestricted text is rarely sufficient when that output controls business processes.

A more predictable approach is to require structured responses and validate them before updating CRM records.

The application should check that the response matches the expected schema before performing any action.

Some actions are better handled by deterministic rules.

Communication consent, payment processing, account permissions and important customer record changes shouldn't depend solely on a language model's interpretation.

AI can help classify or summarise information, but critical operations should remain subject to validated rules and appropriate human oversight.

AI assistants can reduce repetitive work, but they should also recognise their limitations.

A prospect asking an unusual technical question or requesting a customised proposal may need to speak with a person.

The best automation systems provide clear escalation paths rather than trying to automate every possible interaction.

GoHighLevel's visual workflow builder makes many routine automations accessible to non-developers.

A basic appointment reminder or lead notification may require little custom development.

The situation changes when several systems need to work together.

For example, a business might need to migrate thousands of existing contacts, integrate custom booking software, synchronise records with an accounting platform and implement specialised lead routing.

These projects require more than familiarity with a visual workflow editor.

They involve data modelling, integration architecture, testing, security and operational reliability.

The decision rule is straightforward: use native workflows for anything the platform already does well, and write custom code only where the platform's model runs out — multi-source routing, external system synchronisation, or reporting that spans several platforms. That distinction matters because unnecessary custom development can make a system harder to maintain, while forcing every process into native workflows can create an equally fragile architecture.

Even technically capable teams can run into problems when implementing CRM integrations.

Large workflows containing dozens of branches are difficult to test and maintain.

Smaller, clearly defined workflows are generally easier to troubleshoot.

An integration that works with ten contacts may behave very differently when processing thousands of records.

Developers should account for rate limits, batch processing, exponential backoff and retry behaviour.

Creating a new contact for every incoming event can quickly damage CRM data quality.

Contact matching, normalisation and carefully selected identifiers help reduce duplication.

Access tokens should never be exposed in client-side JavaScript or committed to public repositories.

Use appropriate secret management and grant integrations only the permissions they require.

Successful execution doesn't necessarily mean the system behaves correctly.

Test scenarios should cover missing information, duplicate events, failed API requests, appointment cancellations, opt-outs and unexpected responses from external services.

Observability matters too. Logs and alerts should help identify which step failed and whether the operation can be retried safely.

Not every automation problem needs an API integration.

A useful decision framework is to start with the simplest reliable implementation.

Standard appointment reminders — Native CRM workflows

Basic lead assignment — Native workflows where supported

External application synchronisation — API or integration middleware

Complex routing across several data sources — Custom logic with CRM integration

AI-assisted lead classification — AI service with validated outputs

Enterprise reporting across multiple platforms — Dedicated reporting or data pipeline

Hybrid architectures are often the most practical.

The CRM manages customer interactions and standard business processes, while specialised services handle requirements that benefit from custom code.

This separation can also make future maintenance more manageable.

CRM automation has become considerably more accessible, but ease of implementation shouldn't be confused with reliability.

A workflow that sends a message after a form submission is easy to demonstrate. A system that handles thousands of contacts, prevents duplication, respects communication preferences, synchronises multiple applications and recovers gracefully from failures requires more careful design.

GoHighLevel provides a useful foundation for combining customer management with automation, particularly when its native capabilities are supported by well-designed integrations.

For developers, the opportunity isn't simply to automate more tasks. It's to design systems that remain understandable, maintainable and reliable as businesses grow.

The strongest implementations aren't necessarily the ones with the most workflows, API connections or AI features.

They're the ones where the right information reaches the right system at the right time — and everything continues working when something inevitably goes wrong.

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

#10572. Meet the new gadgets designed to kill our dependence on gadgets

On our video call, Shreyas Narlanka holds up a 3-inch square screen called Meadow. It looks like a thick, equilateral iPhone 13 mini. It would easily fit in my jeans’ front pocket. 

I instantly wanted one.

The Meadow is just one of a small but growing merry band of devices that want to help people cut their hyperdependency on their smartphones. There’s Ikko’s MindOne Pro, a kids’ phone called Freckle, the Light Flip phone, and even an aluminum case called RePod designed to turn obsolete Apple Watches into iPhone-iPod hybrids.

These gadgets are coming at an opportune time because every day I hate my iPhone and my Mac a little more. I ache for a compact device that gives me the convenience of digital life that doesn’t take the life out of me. Which explains why I keep dreaming awake about ditching my iPhone only to end up doomscrolling Instagram (the irony!) so I can watch reels that show how people use these new gadgets to stop doomscrolling Instagram.

Plenty of others seem to be as tempted as I am. The Hong Kong startup that makes the RePod tells me that it has sold more than 50,000 units since July. Ikko’s MindOne drew 3,411 backers on Kickstarter, who pledged nearly $1.5 million. And Meadow’s first two preorder batches sold out, Narlanka tells me. Its second production run is now underway. 

I like to believe that, eventually, there will be a flip in the gadget spacetime continuum. One in which a big silent majority of people, fed up with their own toxic digital stupor, decide that it’s time to get rid of the smartphone in favor of a new class of device. I don’t know what the final form of this device will be. Perhaps AI agents will enable something different, hopefully not tied to Google, Apple, or Meta? Maybe. For now, I look at these gadgets and I see promise.

Rethinking the user experience

Narlanka, 25, dropped out of University of California, San Diego, where he was studying cognitive science focused on human-computer interaction. He sold his previous startup, an e-commerce and inventory management platform built for sneaker and collectable resellers, and decided it was time to craft a phone designed to stop using your phone.

He knew that this phone would require a new user experience. Just like the designers at Light, who are betting that “intentional friction” will help a generation quit doomscrolling, notifications, and anxiety-driven app checking, Narlanka and his team knew that the first thing that needed to go was the apps that enslave us to our screens. But that wasn’t enough. 

“We started with a prototype that was basically a small smartphone, shrunken down like an iPhone mini, but it didn’t feel any different,” Narlanka tells me. Since almost every app on earth is drawn for a tall, skinny rectangle, their brains wanted to do what they were doing before: “We still checked it like a smartphone. We pulled it out and tried to scroll it.” 

With the Meadow, the objective was to create a useful tool, not another phone. It’s a tool you’re supposed to put away—one that doesn’t sing a promise of endless entertainment, like a mermaid living in your back pocket. “When you put it away, it should feel like it’s really gone until you need it,” Narlanka says.

The team went looking for the shape “that made your brain think it was totally different.” The answer, they discovered, had less to do with the shape itself than with what the shape lets you refuse more easily. The square works because it helps our smartphone-trained brains anticipate a new kind of experience. And, as a bonus, it also turned the phone into a conversation starter.

“It doesn’t look like an iPod. It doesn’t look like a phone,” Narlanka says. “So people naturally go, ‘Oh, what is that?’”

But that was only half of the Meadow equation. If you install an ordinary app into a square window, it turns into a cramped, cropped mess. So the design team decided to offer only a handful of apps that its customers might want on the go.

Before Meadow, Light went the same route back in 2015, when the company started to push digital detox with its first “dumb” phone. Built as the anti-Google, anti-Apple, anti-Meta phone, Light’s devices are designed to function as a tool, with apps that enable users to call, consult their calendars, or check for directions with minimalist user interfaces. 

Instead of building every tool from scratch like Light did, Meadow handpicked a short list of real apps and made them fit into the square format: calls and texts; navigation; Uber and Strava for getting around; Spotify and Apple Music for the ride. There’s also a camera, location sharing, note-taking, memo recording and a way to show a QR code. The first software update also brought in voice dictation, swipe typing, and an optional ringtone. That’s it. There is no browser, no social media, no news feed, no Instagram or TikTok.

“When you have an app and you can just freely download any app and you have hundreds on your phone, it makes it a really anxiety-inducing thing,” Narlanka says. “When you’re bored, you’ll naturally go toggle between the apps.”

I asked him how it is possible to have square-interface Spotify and Apple Music apps. Meadow didn’t build new apps with those companies, Narlanka explains. Instead, he says, the phone runs the official apps, untouched under the hood. But when it’s time to create the interface, Meadow is able to tweak it thanks to the way Android OS is layered, Narlanka says.

“If you go a level deeper into Android, you can actually affect how things are rendered out, how text is laid out [on the screen],” he says. The elements of the UX get carefully rearranged for the square format, he says, without actually changing the official app.

It would be impossible to do this UI rearranging for the millions of apps out there but, since Meadow’s design philosophy is reaching a digital anxiety-free nirvana, the company doesn’t have to translate the whole Google Play Store into squares. Just the handful of tools that ship with the phone. 

One size doesn’t fit all

For some people, that will be too limiting. There’s always “one more app” that someone will ask for. That’s why, a few months before unveiling its new Light Flip phone model, Light released a software developer kit. The kit allows third parties to build focused tools like a wallet app to store and show QR boarding passes, which are not built into Light OS. But that app store will be extremely limited and curated. The apps’ UI must fit Light’s UX guidelines, forbidding infinite scrolling, app monetization, and any form of social media.

Ikko took another route, showing what happens when users can install anything on a square phone. The company, based in Shenzhen, China, and best known for earbuds, is saturating Instagram with testimonials from users trumpeting its MindOne Pro phone, a slim, card-size Android phone with a nearly square 4.02-inch display and a $500 price tag.

It looks very pretty and very well made. Sspai, a Chinese gadget site, says the workmanship “is quite exquisite,” from its tight seams to the feeling of the material. “Holding it in your hand, you can really feel a sense of quality. . . . This almost square body proportion is visually very cute and small. When holding it with one hand, the thumb can almost touch the upper edge and the other side of the screen,” it points out. “It feels wonderfully like holding a smart home panel with excellent texture.”

The problem is the apps. The phone stretches everything to fill the square in any way it can, cropping the tops and bottoms of videos and web pages. Even Ikko’s custom phone and messaging apps aren’t well adapted to the screen’s proportions, says Sspai. The Verge’s Allison Johnson writes that “fighting to look at webpages and apps built for rectangles through a square-shaped window is just a losing battle.”

The screen is also too big, at least according to Meadow’s philosophy. After choosing the square form, the design team went shopping for a display that matched the sketch. They met with Ikko’s display manufacturer and passed.

“It was too big,” Narlanka says. “For us, the size is really important.” Meadow’s rule is that a single thumb has to reach across the whole screen, a principle that Steve Jobs himself pointed out about the original iPhone when it was introduced. “The square [Meadow got] is really satisfying,” he adds. 

Friction as a feature

The main contrast between Ikko and Meadow, however, is the friction both devices introduce and the way they do that. Ikko’s MindOne Pro has lots of friction, but the annoying kind. It feels less like the result of a design philosophy than a byproduct of a poorly thought-out approach. The Meadow introduces the friction on purpose. It is the feature, by design. It is baked into the design, designed throughout the entire UX. 

On the Meadow, you can text, but there’s no inbox to doomscroll. “To text someone, you have to actually go into the contact and click ‘message,’” Narlanka says, which is “much more intentional than just like, oh, let me see who texted me and let me scroll this thing and refresh it.”

On the MindOne Pro, you get a conventional inbox-style messaging app. On the Meadow, there’s no front camera; just a little mirror next to the rear lens for framing selfies. You get one look at each photo to check it’s in focus, then it’s locked away until it syncs over Wi-Fi and you open it somewhere else.

“You’re not scrolling your camera roll,” he says. “You’re actually living life.” On the MindOne Pro, the main camera flips around to take the selfie, and offers the same experience as your regular Android photo apps but in a cramped screen.

On the Meadow, the square shapes the UX. On the MindOne Pro, the square destroys the UX.

My only worry here is that, just like Light opened a door to apps through its albeit limited SDK, it seems that the friction keeps getting sanded down. One example: Texting wasn’t in Meadow’s original plan. The company added it once the team started using it outside the lab and saw how people wanted to be able to receive texts from people outside of their synced contacts.

Spin to unspin

The search for cures to our digital anxiety doesn’t stop at phone-like devices. Hong Kong-based Techpod had another idea: Repurpose an old strapless Apple Watch to give it a new form factor and functionality using a metal case with a spinning wheel control.

Spinning the wheel physically rotates the Watch’s Digital Crown, operating the Watch’s screen. The RePod C1 (starts at $60, plus whatever your watch was) mixes the rounded, elongated form factor of the fourth-generation iPod nano with the physical controls of the original iPod.

Kore—Techpod’s founder—tells me that his device is partly for people who “want to stay connected without constantly reaching for a full-size smartphone and getting pulled into messages and social feeds.” Depending on the Watch model and setup, he says, the result can feel like a “mini iPhone.” Put an LTE-enabled Apple Watch inside and you get a mini-iPhone mini. Like Meadow, it’s small enough to hold the essentials and too small to swallow your free time.

The RePod’s minimalist design philosophy goes beyond changing the form factor. Recycling a device that otherwise would have died alone in a drawer aligns perfectly with this slow-living philosophy that seems to power the whole digital detox movement. The physical design of the case also aligns with the less digital, more analog part of the philosophy.

Techpod tried a Bluetooth version first, “but the experience wasn’t good enough,” Kore says, so they decided to go with “purely physical controls.” It was very hard, he points out. Getting a metal ring to grip and turn a tiny crown without slipping took “extremely tight tolerances,” several manufacturing partners, and a year refining the manufacturing process, he recalls. On top of that, they had to keep those precision levels across seven versions for different Watch sizes.

Kore insists RePod isn’t an iPod knockoff, however. “Our tribute is primarily to the wheel-based way of interacting with a device,” he says. But he doesn’t hide his love for the original iPod: “Apple no longer makes the iPod, but the desire for that kind of experience hasn’t disappeared.” His slogan is “Less scrolling, more spinning.” Perhaps the slogan should have been: “Less scrolling, less spinning your wheels. More living your life.”

I’m afraid these devices can’t really get us out of the infinite rabbit hole that we got ourselves into. Neither the RePod, the MindOne Pro, nor the Meadow is capable of that. Narlanka himself tells me that the Meadow is not a phone replacement: “It’s just you leave your phone at home and take this when you want to unplug.” But at $448 plus a mandatory T-Mobile plan from $15 to $35 a month (only available in the U.S.) that seems like a pretty big ask.

At that price, I want a thingamajig that holds all the keys to all the things we truly need on the go, without the noise, leaving everything else to my MacBook. I have the inkling that, perhaps, the advent of truly effective AI agents will be the key to making this happen.

But I digress. Right now, these new-wave devices are making a good case for that dream device. A square that won’t scroll, an app list that won’t grow, and a wheel that won’t let you swipe are a great demonstration that we can do a lot better than our current time-sucking devices. If the worst habits of our phones were designed in, these machines prove that they can be designed out.

#10570. A night of historic firsts: 2026 midterm election winners set new milestones for diversity and generational change

Midterm elections often fail to bring the enthusiasm that surrounds a general election, but this was no ordinary year.

Americans across the country went to the polls and delivered a significant rebuke of President Trump and candidates who support him. Democrats took back the House and the Senate, flipping long-standing red states like Texas along the way. 

Election results are still rolling in, but an incredible number of historic milestones have already occurred, from the first Muslim American U.S. Senator to the country’s first Native American woman governor. 

Read on for some of the historical accomplishments of the 2026 midterm elections:

  • New Mexico’s Deb Haaland, a member of the Laguna Pueblo tribe, is the country’s first Native American woman elected governor. 
  • Gina Hinojosa has been elected the first Hispanic governor of Texas. 
  • New York has elected its first all-woman gubernatorial ticket, with Kathy Hochul winning reelection. Her running mate, Adrienne Adams, has become the first Black woman elected lieutenant governor in the state. 
  • Keisha Lance Bottoms is the first woman and the first Black person elected governor of Georgia. Bottoms is one of five Black governors elected on Tuesday, marking the first time multiple Black governors will serve at once. 
  • Amy Acton has become the first woman governor elected in Ohio. 
  • Abdul El-Sayed of Michigan will serve as the first Muslim U.S. Senator. 
  • California has elected its first modern Latino governor, Xavier Becerra. 
  • New Hampshire’s Chris Pappas is the first openly gay man elected to the U.S. Senate. 
  • Peggy Flanagan, a member of the White Earth Band of Ojibwe, from Minnesota will be the first Native American woman serving as a U.S. Senator. 
  • Nevada elected Aaron Ford as its first Black governor.  
  • Amy Klobuchar will serve as Minnesota’s first woman governor. 
  • South Carolina’s Annie Andrews becomes the state’s first woman elected to the U.S. Senate. 
  • David Crowley will be Wisconsin’s first Black governor. 
  • New York’s Claire Valdez, a citizen of Ysleta Del Sur Pueblo tribe, will be the state’s first Native American Congresswoman. 
  • Colorado elected Melat Kiros as the first Gen Z woman in Congress.
  • Adam Hamawy will be New Jersey’s first Muslim member of Congress. 
  • Michigan will send Jeremy Moss to Congress, the state’s first openly gay man in the position. 
  • Nebraska elected its first Hispanic member of Congress, Denise Powell.
  • Tennessee elected its first woman governor, Marsha Blackburn.

#10567. The National STEM Festival is taking its science fair global

In 2023, the National STEM Challenge launched with a goal of attracting students to technical fields—including those who wouldn’t have necessarily seen themselves as traditional science types. 

Roughly three years later, the event—now known as the National STEM Festival—has drawn students from across the country to present innovative projects at its annual gathering in Washington, D.C. The festival, run by the educational media company Explr, has also provided connections with business leaders and public officials for students looking to explore what careers in science might look like for them and how they can take their projects further, whether that means additional research or filing for intellectual property protection.  

“We’ve been able to connect students with leaders and mentors and just bigger opportunities, so that they could develop their ideas even further,” says Kari Byron, who cofounded Explr and is a former cast member of TV’s MythBusters. 

Now, the festival is planning to expand abroad, starting with an inaugural United Kingdom festival slated for May 2027. The U.K. event is open to students between 13 and 19 from England, Scotland, Wales, or Northern Ireland, who can apply by November 22 to participate. Up to 40 will be chosen to present their work at the festival in London. As in the United States, part of the goal is helping make STEM fields accessible to people from a variety of communities and backgrounds, so travel and accommodations will be paid, says Jenny Buccos, Explr cofounder and CEO. Students will have the chance to meet a range of potential mentors and supporters.  

“Very much like the U.S. has federal government participation, one of our events is taking place at the House of Lords,” Buccos says.  

In the U.S. festival, students have presented projects ranging from a machine to recycle waste plastic into 3D printing filament to to a pill-counting app aimed at pharmacies with limited resources that lack traditional pill-counting machines. In the 2026 festival, which took place in June, about 42% of projects involved artificial intelligence to some degree, according to Explr data, though only about 13.6% were primarily focused on AI and machine learning itself, suggesting students aren’t simply following the latest trends. 

“The projects are increasingly showing us what young people believe is worth solving,” Buccos says. 

In addition to honing and showing off their scientific prowess, students receive help with networking and presenting their ideas, including via a science networking course offered through the festival, Buccos says. There’s even a podcast where some participants have discussed their ideas. 

“We’re trying to go back and give them the skills that they can carry with them throughout their life, even if they choose to not go into a STEM career,” she says. “Being able to express your ideas and adjust it to an audience is really, really important.” 

Students naturally connect with each other through the festival—“They started as strangers, and by the end, these kids are dancing, they’re singing Taylor Swift songs together,” says Byron, the Explr cofounder—and remain digitally connected after they return home. Some have come back to help the next cohort of students present their ideas, and they’ve offered feedback on the festival itself. 

The events are unlikely to be limited to the U.S. and U.K. Plans are underway for a festival in Guadalajara, Mexico, in November 2027, and Explr is in early discussions about hosting a regional festival in the United Arab Emirates, focusing on the Middle East and North Africa. 

“The idea is, we have the Olympics every four years,” says Buccos. “Why aren’t we having innovation summits like this for young people?”
