#10566. The Future Of Actuarial Risk Modeling Is Smarter, Not Just Faster, Computing

Sanchet Pachpute is CIO of New York Life Insurance Company, where he leads platform modernization for Institutional Annuities.

​For decades, actuarial modeling has operated under a constraint most of the business never sees: the sheer computational cost of understanding risk. Projecting and valuing a large insurance portfolio means running thousands of stochastic scenarios across a multi-decade horizon, accounting for mortality, lapse behavior, market conditions and regulatory reserve requirements at every step.

Done the traditional way, on a single machine working through calculations in sequence, that kind of analysis can take weeks. By the time the results land, the market conditions they were modeling have often already moved. This is a problem every carrier running sophisticated stochastic models eventually runs into, regardless of size or line of business.

In the past, how I’ve approached solving this is through high-performance computing: taking a workload built to run sequentially and rearchitecting it to run in parallel across a distributed cluster instead. In one case, a full valuation of a multibillion-dollar portfolio that took roughly 50 days of continuous sequential processing was rebuilt as a distributed workload and came down to roughly two hours. It was the same calculation and the same accuracy requirements spread across many machines working simultaneously, instead of one machine working through a queue. That’s close to a thousand-fold reduction in runtime, and it changes what the function can actually do day to day.

The architecture behind this kind of solution is, at its core, a straightforward idea executed with discipline: Break a massive computation into independent pieces, run them in parallel and reassemble the results efficiently. A coordinating process distributes scenarios across worker processes, each running its assigned share and streaming results back as it goes, with a final step combining everything into a portfolio-level answer.

None of the individual pieces are exotic. What makes this kind of system reliable in production is the discipline around it: fault tolerance so one failed worker doesn’t take down the whole run, careful memory management so dozens of processes aren’t duplicating the same reference data and verification steps that recheck a sample of results the traditional way. This confirms the parallel version hasn’t introduced errors nobody would otherwise catch.

The business impact shows up immediately in cadence. Valuations that used to run quarterly, because that was all the computational budget allowed, can run daily instead. Scenario coverage that used to be limited by time constraints can expand by an order of magnitude, which matters directly for tail-risk analysis. These include the rare, severe scenarios that conventional scenario counts tend to underrepresent simply because running more of them used to be computationally unaffordable. Going from 50 days to two hours doesn’t just save time; it converts actuarial modeling from a once-a-quarter batch report into an on-demand tool the business can actually iterate on.

Where this gets more interesting, and where I think most carriers haven’t caught up yet, is the next layer: using AI not to replace this kind of computing architecture, but to make it smarter about where it spends its cycles. Not every one of a thousand scenarios contributes equally to the final answer. Many cluster tightly around similar outcomes. Machine learning techniques can identify which scenarios are actually representative and which are redundant, letting a cluster spend its compute budget on the scenarios that materially affect the result rather than treating all of them as equally important.

Similarly, trained models can act as fast approximations for the most expensive parts of a calculation, with the full computation reserved for validation and for the scenarios where precision matters most. The computing architecture earns you the raw speed; applying AI on top of it earns you speed where it counts.

That second layer comes with a cost that’s easy to underweight in the excitement of the first. A faster model is only useful if you can still explain it to a regulator, and insurance is an industry where every material modeling assumption eventually has to be defensible, not just accurate on average. A surrogate model that approximates a reserve calculation needs validation against the full calculation on a meaningful sample, documentation of where it’s expected to be reliable and where it isn’t and a clear record of why it was trusted for production use. Skipping that step to chase speed is how a modeling shortcut turns into a regulatory finding 18 months later.

For technology leaders facing this same computational wall, the sequencing matters more than the headline speedup. Start with the distributed computing foundation and prove it against the existing process before trusting it. A 50-days-to-two-hours result only means something once the new system’s output has been validated against the old, slower one at scale.

Only after that foundation is solid should you layer AI-driven optimization on top, because AI applied to an unvalidated computation just produces unvalidated answers faster. Speed that your regulators and your own risk committee can’t fully stand behind isn’t actually an advantage; it’s a liability wearing the costume of one.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#10564. AI won’t end developer jobs

I’ve known hundreds of software developers during my career in financial services. Maybe even thousands. What nearly all of them share is the pride and respect they have for the craft. Good code takes time, discipline, and judgment.

So, what happens now that AI can generate working code in seconds and then test it, fix it, and deploy it? Many are asking if the coding profession is on borrowed time.

But that framing misses what’s changing and why.

History tells us that when building becomes easier, the world needs more builders. E-commerce opened new markets, so more people started selling. Cloud computing widened what was possible, so businesses built more software. AI is now bringing that same expansion to software development. It makes code easier to produce and puts more value into the decisions that can’t be automated away. That is the human part.

AI will eliminate the idea that a developer’s value is typing code. The developer role is evolving into something broader and more strategic: software engineering. Great engineers define the problem, design for resilience, and embrace system-level thinking. They guide and validate complex architecture to ensure the product is safe and accurate for customers. High-quality engineers know when a feature is ready and when an AI-generated answer is wrong. They understand when a faster release creates more risk. That judgment is still important.

Together, AI and developers can turn more good ideas into secure, reliable, and valued products. At Synchrony, we’re already seeing this happen. In early pilots with our engineering teams, busywork and rework has shrunk and quality risen, with an estimated 25% faster delivery, 30% fewer defects, 120% more unit tests, and nearly 40% fewer security vulnerabilities.

TRUST REQUIRES GOVERNANCE

AI also relies on other human traits that it doesn’t possess, including the curiosity and discipline to challenge what it produces. Remember, mistakes including security gaps, flawed assumptions, and even data exposure can ship just as fast as clean code. We know we can build it, but can we trust it? We need engineers to test the output and challenge the assumptions. The engineers know when to reject bad code the tool produces. Ultimately, companies can ship code faster, with better outcomes and more time to spend on problems that require human judgment.

That’s why governance can’t be an afterthought. It must be built into how teams use AI, with clear guardrails on approved tools, data use, human review, traceability, quality, and security checks. AI can support work end to end, but accountability stays with people. Even in the age of AI, that is still the only way to create software customers can rely on.

FOR EARLY CAREER TALENT, AI IS AN ACCELERATOR

Those same guardrails and fast feedback loops affect talent too, especially early-career engineers. Some argue AI makes junior hiring unnecessary. That’s not what we find. Early-career engineers often experiment and get creative with these tools quickly, bringing new ideas to the table. Used well, AI is a programming partner that can explain unfamiliar code, flag downstream effects, and help a junior engineer get unstuck faster.

But the benefits are not automatic. Junior talent contributes faster only when companies invest in the fundamentals: a culture of trust and responsibility and real mentorship. AI can accelerate learning, but it can’t replace ownership. We need early career talent to become tomorrow’s senior engineers and leaders, and leadership is built by learning the craft from the ground up.

A WIDER ON-RAMP FOR SOFTWARE CAREERS

AI can also make the profession more accessible for people who did not start on a traditional path. This is personal for me. Growing up, I didn’t think it was possible for me to be a C-suite leader of a financial services company. I loved computer science, but I opted out of AP computer science in high school because I was the only girl in the room. Moments like that shape who sees a future for themselves in the industry.

What changes that experience is faster feedback, safer ways to ask questions, and more support while you build confidence. For many people, the missing piece is confidence, which requires a better way to practice. And leaders still have to do the work around coaching and setting clear expectations.

Universities are responding too, blending AI with a broad range of disciplines from language to critical thinking. What’s emerging is the foundation for modern development that combines the best of human and machine.

The optimist in me sees that there is no set playbook and that creates opportunity to thrive. The people and teams that improve fastest will practice with the tools every day and build habits around review and quality, as well as accountability. Leaders should support these behaviors by investing in them. They can transform workflow and reward quality and judgment over raw volume and output. Developers should reinvent themselves as engineers by building fluency with AI the same way they build fluency with any core tool. They should own the results, using it often and testing what it gives back.

The future will belong to those who can build software people can trust. And that means more software engineers using judgment to solve harder problems.

Carol Juel is executive vice president and CEO of digital platform at Synchrony.

#10562. Executives need a point of view

There was a time when executives could hide behind a brand. Not anymore. Today, people want to know the humans behind the companies they buy from, work for, and invest in. They also expect them to sound like people, not talking points with the life edited out of them.

That expectation has made executive visibility a major part of the job. Leaders who speak credibly in public build trust and create awareness, while giving people another way to understand the companies they represent.

A TRUSTED BRAND NEEDS TRUSTED VOICES

Not every leader needs the same kind of public profile, however. What matters is that when they do show up, they bring more than the company line. They bring their own experience, expertise, and perspective. They should also take part in the conversations shaping their industries, not just show up when the company has something to announce.

But, as with all things in life, there is a balance. Leaders must be careful not to swing too far in either direction, whether being too promotional of their company or self.

I recently chatted with a long-standing partner of ours, Len Covello, CTO and cofounder of Engage People. He explained, “Executive visibility is about making the expertise and perspective behind a company accessible to the people who need to understand and trust it…(Not being too promotional) helps give you credibility and positions you as an industry expert rather than someone who is only talking about their own company.”

And that credibility shouldn’t rest on a single person. When one founder or CEO becomes synonymous with a company, the brand can become overly dependent on them. Organizations should develop a bench of curated spokesperson executives who can speak about different areas of the business.

During a recent conversation with another Caliber partner, Mylo’s president and COO Belen Tokarski shared that “…having multiple spokespeople demonstrates the credibility of the team. We are not one leader, but a group of professionals who work well together.”

Covello added, “When visibility is distributed thoughtfully, it makes the company feel more human and gives people a clearer sense of the expertise behind the brand.”

The goal is to give multiple credible leaders a voice.

BALANCE PERSONAL WITH PROFESSIONAL

One of the most difficult aspects of building an executive brand is determining how much to say and what is worth saying. Some executives become so concerned about how their comments might be perceived that what they say ultimately sounds inauthentic and robotic. Or they just stay silent.

Others go too far in the other direction, becoming so comfortable sharing that they create the opposite problem: too much noise. Every company update or personal experience becomes an opportunity for posting, and eventually people tune them out.

Tokarski believes, “It’s important…to be a vocal advocate for everything we do that’s unique in the industry. But that doesn’t mean screaming louder than others.”

One way to achieve an effective balance is for the executive to assemble a small, trusted group of advisers who can provide candid feedback.

PLAY TO YOUR STRENGTHS

As an executive wondering about your personal brand, think about what you would share at a board table or dinner with a colleague or client. What perspectives do others seek from you? What issues could you comfortably discuss without relying on a script?

Those conversations are often the strongest foundation for a personal brand. If what an executive discusses publicly is completely different from what they would naturally talk about in person, the content can feel forced. Audiences pick up on that almost instantly. So start with what feels natural, build confidence, and expand from there.

MOVE THE BUSINESS FORWARD

As with any business strategy, companies should consider how executive visibility supports broader goals, and then identify ways to measure its impact.

For one executive, the goal may be becoming a more recognized voice in their industry. Another may be focused on recruiting, building relationships with prospective clients, or raising awareness around a particular area of expertise.

No single metric tells the entire story. A few key metrics—dare I say KPIs—are worth looking at. That might mean media share of voice, engagement, or speaking opportunities, alongside broader business outcomes like client and employee retention, sales, or recruiting data over time.

There’s also a newer metric worth watching, and the stakes are real. LinkedIn ranks as a top source for LLM citations, with personal profiles accounting for 75% of all LinkedIn citations. As AI search reshapes how people find companies and expertise, executive activity on LinkedIn could be the difference between being discovered and being invisible.

NO UPSIDE TO INVISIBILITY

Ultimately, there is no universal formula for building the right executive profile—and that’s a good thing. The appropriate balance between corporate messaging and personal perspective should vary depending on the leader, audience, and situation.

What is clear is that executives who never show up are leaving an opportunity on the table.

When companies encourage leaders to share their perspectives publicly, they create more opportunities for audiences to learn about the expertise and people behind the business. And that visibility can strengthen the company’s reputation and support outcomes that matter to the business’s bottom line, from recruiting and retention to client relationships and sales.

Grace Keith Rodriguez is CEO of Caliber Corporate Advisers.

#10559. How to Create Effective Cold Email Sequences That Generate Replies: A Developer's Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting with prospects via cold email isn't always straightforward, particularly when recipients receive dozens of messages every day. Even a relevant email can get lost in a crowded inbox. That's why many B2B companies use cold email sequences to maintain consistent communication and manage follow-ups.

However, creating effective email sequences involves more than writing messages and choosing sending intervals. Developers building or integrating email automation systems must also consider API reliability, message scheduling, data validation, email authentication, security, and workflow monitoring.

A well-designed email sequence combines relevant communication with reliable technical infrastructure. This article explains how to structure cold email sequences and explores the development practices that make automated email workflows more dependable.

A cold email sequence is a planned series of emails sent to prospective customers who have not previously established a direct relationship with the sender. These messages are delivered over a defined period, with each email designed to introduce an offer, provide additional context, address a potential concern, or encourage a response.

For example, a software development company might contact a growing SaaS business to discuss opportunities to improve its application infrastructure. The first email could introduce the company's expertise, the second could explain a relevant technical challenge, and the third could share a useful observation about application performance.

Each message supports the same overall objective while contributing something different.

From a developer's perspective, a cold email sequence can also be understood as an event-driven workflow. Every contact has a sequence state, scheduled messages, and conditions that determine whether the next message should be delivered.

A basic sequence might follow this structure:

This model is useful for developers working on CRM integrations, email platforms, customer engagement systems, and automated business workflows.

The first step is to establish a clear plan before writing any messages. Determine who you want to contact, what problem your offer solves, and what action you want recipients to take.

Start by defining your ideal customer profile, identifying relevant decision-makers, and researching their business needs. Next, select one primary campaign objective, such as generating positive replies, booking qualified meetings, or exploring potential partnerships.

Decide how many emails you will send, what each message will communicate, and how much time will pass between follow-ups. Make sure every email adds value instead of repeating information from previous messages.

For example, a software consultancy targeting B2B SaaS companies might introduce an application performance challenge in the first email, share a practical recommendation in the second, provide a relevant engineering case study in the third, and close the sequence with a polite final follow-up.

Before launching the campaign, review the prospect list, verify contact information, check email authentication, and test the complete sequence.

Developers should also establish technical requirements before implementation:

Define how contact information will be stored and validated.

Determine which events trigger scheduled emails.

Implement conditions that prevent unnecessary follow-ups.

Establish secure API connections between applications.

Record delivery results, failures, and recipient responses.

These considerations help transform a basic email campaign into a maintainable automation workflow.

Every cold email sequence needs a specific objective. Without one, messages may contain several unrelated offers or ask recipients to take different actions.

A SaaS company might want to schedule product demonstrations, while a software consultancy may want to connect with businesses planning infrastructure improvements. A recruitment firm might aim to establish relationships with technical hiring managers.

Your objective should influence the message and call to action.

For developers building campaign dashboards, goals should also be represented through measurable application events.

This example demonstrates how a dashboard might calculate a positive reply rate from stored campaign events.

In production, developers should also handle zero denominators, duplicate events, inconsistent reporting periods, and differences between accepted, delivered, and failed messages.

Effective cold email sequences begin with the right audience. Even a well-written email is unlikely to generate meaningful replies if it reaches people who have no interest in the subject.

Start by defining your ideal customer profile. Consider industry, company size, location, business model, growth stage, and the challenges your product or service addresses.

For example, a development consultancy offering cloud migration assistance might contact CTOs, engineering managers, and technical founders. Although these professionals may benefit from similar services, their priorities can differ.

A CTO may focus on infrastructure reliability and operational costs, while an engineering manager may prioritize deployment workflows, maintainability, and team productivity.

Segment prospects according to meaningful differences rather than sending identical messages to everyone.

Audience segmentation can be implemented through database queries, CRM filters, or API-based contact management.

Structured data makes segmentation more predictable and helps prevent inconsistent personalization.

Contact data should be collected and processed lawfully, with appropriate access controls and retention policies.

Personalization helps prospects understand why you are contacting them specifically. However, effective personalization involves more than inserting a first name, company name, or job title into a template.

Research relevant professional information that connects the recipient's business to your offer.

For example, a development consultancy contacting a SaaS company could mention a recently announced application launch and explain how scalable deployment practices may support future growth.

The observation should be accurate and connected to a genuine technical challenge.

A practical personalization process involves three steps:

Identify a relevant professional observation.

Explain why it may matter to the recipient.

Connect it naturally to the proposed solution.

This simple example demonstrates template personalization.

The subject line gives recipients an initial indication of what an email contains. A clear, relevant subject line helps them recognize the message's purpose.

Keep subject lines concise and connected to the recipient's business needs. Avoid exaggerated promises, artificial urgency, and misleading claims.

The first email introduces your business and gives the prospect a reason to consider your offer.

A useful structure includes a personalized opening, a clear value proposition, a brief explanation of relevant expertise, and one simple call to action.

Avoid introducing every service you offer or listing multiple benefits in the same email.

An effective cold email sequence follows a logical progression. Each message builds on the initial contact by introducing new information, clarifying value, or offering another way to engage.

Follow-up emails give prospects another opportunity to respond when they overlook an initial message or need additional information.

Each follow-up should introduce a useful detail, clarify the offer, or address a relevant concern.

Beyond content and scheduling, developers need to ensure that automated email workflows operate securely and consistently.

Email authentication helps receiving systems evaluate whether a message is authorized to use its sending domain.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework): Identifies servers authorized to send mail for a domain.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail): Uses cryptographic signatures to help verify message integrity and signing-domain responsibility.

DMARC (Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance): Defines how SPF or DKIM authentication should align with the visible From domain and provides reporting capabilities.

Developers responsible for email infrastructure should understand how these mechanisms work together.

SitePoint's article on email deliverability provides additional context about authentication, sender reputation, and delivery reliability.

Modern email workflows often integrate CRM systems, databases, automation platforms, and email service providers.

REST APIs allow developers to exchange information between these applications.

For example, an application might synchronize contact records, create campaigns, retrieve reply events, or update sequence states.

Avoid exposing sensitive information in logs.

These practices improve maintainability and reduce the risk of inconsistent campaign data.

Messages are scheduled at the correct times.

Failed jobs do not create duplicate messages.

Templates render correctly across email clients.

API failures produce useful operational logs.

This architecture introduces several engineering considerations.

Data synchronization: Contact records must remain consistent between the CRM and the email platform.

Error handling: API failures should be recorded and retried safely when appropriate.

Security: API credentials and contact information should be protected.

Observability: Developers should be able to investigate failed operations without exposing sensitive information.

The same architectural principles apply to other email automation providers. Before choosing an integration, review its current API documentation, authentication model, event coverage, rate limits, and data-handling requirements.

Several common mistakes can reduce the effectiveness and reliability of cold email outreach, even when businesses use automation.

Sending generic messages: Emails that could apply to any business fail to establish relevance. Use appropriate segmentation and accurate personalization.

Repeating the same pitch: Every follow-up should contribute something new instead of repeating the original message.

Making emails too long: Excessive explanations and multiple offers make it harder for prospects to identify the main point.

Using aggressive follow-ups: Sending messages too frequently can frustrate recipients. Choose reasonable intervals and respect recipient preferences.

Making unsupported claims: Exaggerated results and misleading promises damage credibility.

Ignoring recipient responses: Stop automated sequences when someone replies, opts out, or requests no further contact.

Failing to handle duplicate events: A webhook may be delivered more than once. Applications should use idempotent processing to avoid repeated actions.

Ignoring API failures: Temporary provider outages and rate limits should not silently corrupt campaign state.

Insufficient testing: Automation workflows should be validated with controlled test data before production deployment.

Scaling too quickly: Increasing sending volume before validating deliverability, infrastructure, and workflow reliability can amplify existing problems.

Avoiding these mistakes helps teams maintain a professional communication process while reducing operational risks.

The goal is not merely to send more emails. It is to create a reliable communication workflow that respects recipients and supports meaningful conversations.

#10558. Markets rise, but SpaceX and Tesla stocks fall, after Dems take back the House and Senate. Here’s why

There’s no two ways about it: Yesterday’s midterm election results were nothing short of a massive victory for the Democrats (a “blue tsunami,” indeed). The Democrats will now control both chambers of Congress by a wide margin, radically altering the balance of power in Washington.

While change brings uncertainty—and no one likes uncertainty less than investors—stock markets have so far reacted positively to the news.

As of the time of this writing, S&P futures are up more than 1%, Dow futures are up more than 1.4%, and the tech-heavy Nasdaq futures are up nearly 1%.

But while many stocks are trending up this morning, two of the biggest ones, Elon Musk’s SpaceX (Nasdaq: SPCX) and Tesla (Nasdaq: TSLA), are down significantly in premarket trading.

And yes, the election results are likely the biggest reason for their fall. Here’s what you need to know.

Tesla and SpaceX shares fall after historic Democratic wins

As of the time of this writing, shares in Elon Musk’s two public companies, Tesla and SpaceX, are down by notable percentages.

TSLA shares are currently down around 5.5% in premarket trading, with SPCX shares down more than 5.5% as well. 

To put those numbers into perspective, the broader Nasdaq, where Tesla and SpaceX trade, is up nearly 1%.

And other Big Tech contemporaries of Musk’s public companies are also seeing green, including Alphabet, Amazon, Apple, Microsoft, Nvidia, and Meta.

So if most of Big Tech is up on the midterm results, why are Musk’s companies down significantly?

The short answer is that investors are likely worried that a Democratic-controlled Congress could make things harder for Musk’s companies going forward, mainly through extra scrutiny on Musk’s business empire and the companies’ existing government contracts.

Musk has spent much of the last several years making enemies of the Democrats as his political alliances shifted right.

During that time, Musk became one of the largest donors to the GOP and, in the midterm elections alone, contributed hundreds of millions to Republican coffers.

Musk also infamously ran the Trump administration’s Department of Government Efficiency (DOGE) in 2025, which cut funding for numerous programs important to Democrats. 

Without a doubt, Musk is the most high-profile critic of the Democrats, frequently lambasting them and the left in general on his social media platform X, while embracing the current administration, which has close ties and contracts with Musk’s various properties, including SpaceX and its Starlink satellite business.

But now the balance of power has shifted in Washington—not to mention Texas, where the Democrats won the governorship and Senate race, turning the state blue for the first time in decades, and where Elon Musk relocated his companies. Investors this morning clearly fear that shift now leaves Musk’s business interests vulnerable.

How could Tesla and SpaceX be impacted?

The main thing investors in SpaceX and Tesla seem to be fearing today is that the new Democratic-controlled House and Senate could increase scrutiny and regulatory oversight of Musk’s companies’ business dealings and government contracts.

This is especially true for a prospective merger between Tesla and SpaceX, which the boards of both companies are studying, Musk has confirmed.

As Kathleen Brooks, research director for brokerage firm XTB, told the Wall Street Journal, “While Congress cannot stop a merger between SpaceX and Tesla, they could slow it down via extra scrutiny.”

She added: “There is now a political risk premium attached to both Tesla and SpaceX that could weigh on their share prices.”

And in premarket trading today, that “political risk premium” does indeed seem to be in effect.

Currently, TSLA is trading at around $375.50, and SPCX is trading at around $133.

Of course, any increased scrutiny would not come immediately. Although the Democrats swept the midterm elections last night, the new Congress is not sworn in until January 3, giving Musk and his investors time to digest and better plan for the possible changes ahead.

#10557. As New Cyberthreats Loom, Businesses Must Focus On Results

James Lamond is the Executive Director of Trusted Future, a Washington-based think tank focused on technology policy.

​After a summer of high-tension developments in the geopolitical space, including U.S. intelligence assessments that Russian leaders could target NATO members in coming years, some analysts are warning that bad actors and government leaders could be mounting threats of all levels.​

These potential scenarios, some of which have already started to unfold, exist on a spectrum. On one end of the spectrum, the less likely and more extreme end, are invasions, sabotage, airspace incursions and more. ​

On the other end of the spectrum, the more likely and less extreme end, is an increase in cyberattacks. In fact, European and Western officials have already reported an intensifying campaign of Russian cyber operations targeting critical infrastructure, digital economies and government networks.​

How should companies think about operating in this threat environment where increased cyber intrusions are the new norm? ​

Leaders need to focus on outcomes, not documents. One of the challenges that we have seen in Europe over the last couple years is an increase in regulations, reporting requirements and compliance frameworks that haven’t actually resulted in meaningful cybersecurity. Even as organizational spending on information security has doubled, cyber incidents have increased. Companies should avoid getting bogged down in this compliance trap.

Ukraine has many lessons to offer here. Their cyberdefenses have proven to be incredibly innovative and resilient, even in the face of war. This is partially due to the fact that Ukrainian institutions have increasingly adopted outcome-based performance metrics like objectives and key results (OKRs), which measure real-world impact like reduced service disruption or fraud losses. This focus on measuring outcomes that matter should be the highest priority for companies. ​

Leaders must also increase information sharing with both the private sector and governments. Too often, threat intelligence is thought of as a competitive advantage. However, increased cooperation between companies on incident response could go a long way in increasing resilience.

Airline safety provides a useful model to learn from here. Safety and threat intelligence is shared constantly because safety in the sky is seen as urgent, operational and noncompetitive. In this increased threat environment, there is no reason that cybersecurity should be thought of any differently.

Unfortunately, the infrastructure for effective EU-wide operational coordination among firms remains fragmented. An industry-led initiative here could help bridge this gap. ​

Leaders should continue to rigorously use AI red-teaming. While the debate about the pace of development of AI and the appropriate governance models for it is incredibly important and should continue, cyber practitioners have to deal with the reality as it exists today. Current AI models are able to find software flaws at a pace and efficiency previously inconceivable. Just as nation-states and criminal organizations can use these tools to exploit weaknesses, they can also give defenders their most powerful tool to find and close those vulnerabilities. ​

Finally, leaders must invest in trusted security architectures. The twin threats of nation-states and superior AI models present a dangerous combination. The CSA CISO Alliance, a group of the world’s leading cybersecurity practitioners, recommended key steps that technologists can use for becoming cybersecure in the age of AI. They include focusing on known security basics, like those that have been embedded directly into the core architecture of smartphones and mobile devices, to protect users.​

The digital environment is facing new and growing threats, but there are also new and exciting opportunities here for leaders hoping to make their mark on the future of security. Companies that are able to focus on a results-oriented approach to cybersecurity may be best able to take advantage of these opportunities.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#10550. Why Data Residency Is Becoming An AI Strategy Decision, Not A Compliance One

Michael Nicosia, COO and cofounder at Salt Security.

For the better part of the last 10 years, data residency was usually something that came up when you needed to meet some compliance standard. You’d identify where data had to reside if you wanted to offer your service in another country. Consult the compliance standard and move on to the next task. You would then assign that to your legal department and IT to figure out.

That is no longer the case. With AI becoming embedded in the business process of companies, many are starting to see where data needs to reside earlier in the expansion journey. Where once data residency was relegated to the bottom of a list of tasks, it has crept its way up to a C-level concern. If you aren’t thinking about data residency, you may not realize you’re making that decision.

When dealing with traditional software, data is typically confined to a certain area. You have applications that store data and use that data to provide functionality, so where that data resides is a fairly simple question.

AI, however, works with data differently. It learns from that data and will use it to learn even more. Once you introduce AI into your business, you may find your data traveling to various places, such as across international borders, to feed your AI model. If you can’t trace back where your data went after an AI application began utilizing it, you may run into residency issues.

As more countries continue to update their regulations regarding where data can reside, you’ll find yourself having to account for this in your expansion plans.

If you’re looking to grow your business, it’s important to consider where data can reside when deciding where you want to expand. In some cases, it may not be possible to keep data within the country you wish to offer your services in. In some markets, if you can’t keep data in-country, you simply can’t do business there, no matter how good your product is. If your target customers care about where data resides and you are unable to meet that demand, you won’t be able to do business in that country.

By considering where data needs to reside before you begin your expansion into that country, you can avoid some headaches down the road—whether that be a delay in offering your service or building infrastructure that won’t meet the demands of your customers.

There are some questions that I believe leaders should be asking themselves when trying to decide where to place their data.

• Do you know where your data is located once you begin feeding your AI models with it?

• Are you thinking about data residency early enough? If you are waiting until you choose a country to expand into and then ask yourself where data needs to reside, you’re already too late.

• Can you prove where your data is located? Customers will want to know where their data is being kept. Make sure you have the ability to show your customers where their data is.​

Data residency is no longer something that can be overlooked. It’s something that can dictate where you can offer your services and how long it will take to offer them. Once you factor in where your data will reside and how that will affect your AI, you can begin to create a roadmap that allows you to grow your business at a faster pace.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#10546. How to Organize CSS with Cascade Layers in Large Web Projects

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

As a website grows, its stylesheet often becomes harder to maintain. A rule added to fix one component can unexpectedly affect another, and developers may find themselves adding increasingly specific selectors to override previous styles. Over time, these changes make CSS difficult to understand and update.

CSS cascade layers provide a way to organize styles into named groups and control their priority. Instead of relying entirely on selector specificity and the order of declarations, developers can establish a predictable structure for their stylesheets.

CSS determines which declarations apply by considering factors such as origin, importance, cascade layer priority, specificity, and source order. When several rules target the same element, understanding these factors helps explain why one style overrides another.

If all three rules apply to the same button under normal author styles, the ID selector wins because it has greater specificity.

This can become inconvenient when a project contains third-party styles, reusable components, and application-specific overrides. Developers may respond by adding more specific selectors, making future changes increasingly difficult.

Cascade layers offer another approach: establish a clear priority order before writing individual component rules.

A useful starting point is to divide styles according to their purpose. For example, a project might contain reset styles, third-party styles, reusable components, and page-specific adjustments.

You can establish these layers at the beginning of your stylesheet:

The declaration at the top establishes the order for normal declarations in these layers. For normal author styles, later layers have higher priority than earlier layers, regardless of selector specificity between those layers.

This means a simple selector in a higher-priority layer can override a more specific selector in a lower-priority layer.

For more background on building adaptable interfaces, developers can explore SitePoint's introduction to CSS container queries, which explains another way to structure maintainable component styles.

Not every project needs the same number of layers. A small website may require only a few, while a large application may benefit from a more detailed structure.

This is a suggested structure rather than a mandatory convention. The important part is defining the priority order intentionally and applying it consistently.

Cascade layers can help avoid selectors that become unnecessarily complicated.

For normal declarations, the second rule wins because overrides has higher layer priority, even though the first selector contains an ID and more class selectors.

This can make changes easier to review because developers do not need to keep increasing selector specificity to achieve the intended result.

However, layer order should not become an excuse to place every exception in a global override layer. Keep related styles close to their components and use overrides only when the exception is intentional.

Cascade layers have rules that developers need to understand before adopting them across an existing codebase.

For normal declarations, later layers have higher priority. For declarations marked !important, the layer priority is reversed. Important declarations in earlier layers take precedence over important declarations in later layers.

Unlayered normal author styles also take precedence over normal declarations inside author cascade layers. This matters when integrating a layered stylesheet with older CSS that remains outside the layer structure.

The MDN documentation on CSS cascade layers explains these priority rules and provides additional examples.

Before reorganizing a production stylesheet, test how existing third-party rules, inline styles, and important declarations interact with the new structure.

Rewriting an entire stylesheet at once can introduce regressions. A safer approach is to define the intended layer order, migrate one group of styles at a time, and compare the rendered interface after each change.

Start with styles that are easy to isolate, such as resets or a small set of reusable components. Check buttons, forms, navigation, and responsive layouts at several viewport sizes.

Browser developer tools can help identify which declaration is winning and whether it belongs to the expected layer. Visual regression tests are also useful when the application has many shared components.

For teams, documenting the layer order and agreeing on where new styles belong can prevent the stylesheet from becoming disorganized again.

CSS cascade layers provide developers with a practical way to manage style priority in growing applications. By separating resets, vendor styles, components, and utilities into a clear hierarchy, teams can reduce specificity conflicts and make stylesheets easier to maintain.

The best results come from a deliberate layer structure, consistent naming, and careful testing during migration. Cascade layers do not remove the need to understand CSS specificity, but they give developers another tool for controlling how styles interact.

#10538. Sony's final 4K Blu-ray player listed as out of stock in Japan, firm may be preparing to abandon standalone players

Sony's Japanese 4K Blu-ray lineup is disappearing, raising questions about the future of standalone disc players

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Sony may be preparing to abandon the standalone 4K Blu-ray players, and the signs are becoming harder to ignore. According to a report by Forbes, its last remaining model in Japan, the UBP-X700K, was marked as unavailable on October 22, with local retailers reportedly told not to expect another shipment— a development that has raised questions about the future of Sony’s dedicated followers. Sony's U.S. website has also listed the player as ‘out of stock’.

So, is Sony really done making 4K Blu-ray players? That isn't clear. The company is yet to announce a worldwide withdrawal, and its responses to questions about the reports leave that possibility open. When asked by Forbes whether Sony is leaving the market, its UK operation replied: “This information has not been publicly announced by Sony. We are aware of the recent media reports, but prefer to refrain from commenting on speculation.”

There is an important difference between what is happening in Japan and what buyers can still find elsewhere. The UBP-X700K remains listed on Sony’s U.S. website, with Best Buy offering it for $329.99 at press time, which means Sony 4K buyers haven’t missed their chance yet. However, Amazon is no longer selling the player directly as a new product. Finding one could be harder if the company decides not to restock.

This is not the first time Sony has stepped away from physical media. In January 2025, the company stopped producing blank recordable Blu-ray discs, later announcing in February that it would also end its production in Japan. In July 2026, Sony announced it would stop producing physical PlayStation game discs from 2028— although it remains unclear if the next PlayStation console will have a disc drive. Notably, the PS5 Pro launched without a built-in disc drive, but customers who want to play physical games can purchase the optional drive separately.

However, if Sony ultimately leaves the market, buyers will still have alternatives. Panasonic remains the biggest name in dedicated 4K Blu-ray players, although its products are not that cheap. Currently, the Panasonic UB154 lists on Amazon for $289.99, while the UB820 goes for $649.99, a steep climb from its original $499 launch price. The higher-end UN9000 is retailing at $1,299.99 on Walmart. Buyers could also consider gaming consoles such as a disc-equipped PS5, which is listed at $649.99, or the Xbox Series X, priced at $799.99. Both can play 4K Blu-ray movies, but neither supports Dolby Vision when playing discs.

Sony’s move, if true, would add it to a growing list of manufacturers that have dropped the Blu-ray hardware. In 2018, Oppo stopped producing players while Samsung ended plans for new U.S. Blu-ray players a year later. Other companies include LG, which dropped the business in 2024, with French company Reavon halting production in 2025.

But it's important to note that 4K Blu-ray demand has not collapsed in the same way as the wider physical disc market. Figures laid out by the Digital Entertainment Group show that overall U.S. disc sales declined by roughly 9% in 2025. Spending on 4K discs, however, climbed by 12% over the same period, before climbing another 18% in the second quarter of 2026. That said, for existing Sony owners, there is no reason to panic just yet. A player that works today will continue playing the discs already in a collector’s library — and there is no evidence that Sony plans to push an update that would stop existing players from reading movies.

#10535. What Is the Best UI Testing Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner.

Prices are taken from each vendor's site or published comparisons as of October 2026. Check current pricing before you buy.

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

Argos is an open source visual testing platform with SDKs for Playwright, Storybook, Vitest, and Cypress. It adds cloud baselines and team review on top of the screenshots your tests already produce.

Strengths: Simple flat pricing. The Hobby plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month, and Pro is $100/month for 35,000 screenshots, with a budget cap available.

Trade-offs: Argos focuses on visual diffs. Argos compares screenshots captured by your own test runs, so browser coverage depends on what your CI runs, and it doesn't offer hosted mobile simulators. It also has a smaller ecosystem than the larger platforms.

Best for: Small teams that want hosted visual review at a predictable price.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic, Percy or Argos.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, Chromatic, Percy and Argos all offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

A federal judge rejects xAI’s mobile-turbine exemption argument, allowing the NAACP’s Clean Air Act lawsuit over Colossus 2 to proceed.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

SpaceX has suffered a setback after losing in a lawsuit aimed at stopping the Clean Air Act requirements on the turbines powering xAI’s Colossus 2 data center in Southaven, Mississippi. According to a report by CNBC, a judge rejected the company’s motion to dismiss the case on Friday, October 30, allowing the National Association for the Advancement of Colored People (NAACP) to proceed, rejecting xAI’s argument that its turbines fall under the ‘mobile sources’ category that exempts it from air permitting requirements.

The case, brought in April 2026 by the NAACP and represented by the Southern Environmental Law Center (SELC) alongside Earthjustice, targets xAI’s operation of a gas-fired power plant to supply electricity to its facility in the Greater Memphis area. SpaceX owns and operates xAI, implicating Elon Musk's wider aerospace and technology company.

According to court documents, NACCP accuses xAI and its subsidiary, MZX Tech, of installing 27 gas turbines in Southaven last year without obtaining the required permits. The applicants also argued that the company’s equipment has been polluting the environment through nitrogen oxide emissions and fine particulate matter, which can contribute to breath-related hazards to residents. The SELC particularly says the plant could emit over 5,00 tons of nitrogen oxides, 400 tons of fine particulate matter, and 47 tons of formaldehyde — a cancer-causing chemical — annually.

The judge also rejected xAI’s attempted classification of the turbines as mobile equipment, holding that the company did not qualify for exemptions under applicable regulations and that the turbines were not exempt from Clean Air Act permitting requirements. Just two weeks earlier, SpaceX suffered another setback after the same court rejected a separate Department of Justice lawsuit that argued against the case proceeding.

“This decision is another important victory in our effort to hold xAI accountable. The courts have repeatedly rejected attempts by xAI and the DOJ to dismiss the NAACP’s Clean Air Act case, affirming our right to pursue justice for communities impacted by pollution. Our position has been clear: xAI cannot operate gas turbines without the required permits. Now that the motion to dismiss has been denied, we look forward to continuing to defend our position in court,” Abre’ Conner, NAACP Director of Environmental and Climate Justice, said. SpaceX is yet to comment on the ruling.

Colossus 2 installed 59 natural gas turbines in July, immediately sparking concerns from residents about emissions and the impact on nearby communities. The Memphis facility plans for about 860,000 Nvidia GB300 GPUs across four Colossus buildings —but it could be more — with Musk stating, “There will be a total of around 1.2M Blackwells at Colossus 2 by end of year”

Elon Musk just gave a massive update on Colossus 2 in Memphis, and the scale of what's being built is absolutely insane Two buildings already packed with GB300s, two more are coming online within weeks, and it is heading toward roughly 860,000 NVIDIA GB300s Here's the breakdown: • Macroharder: 220,000 GB300s, 800-gig NICs, configured for training • Minihard: 220,000 GB300s, same training configuration • Gun Range: 220,000 GB300s, tracking to be operational by the END OF NOVEMBER • Spam: ~200,000 GB300s, configured for inference, targeting DECEMBER That's roughly 860,000 GB300s across these four buildings Spam is designed for inference, which needs much simpler networking and less than HALF the fiber of the training buildings. And yes, the building once housed Hormel products Elon personally walked the entire Colossus 2 site around midnight and again the next morning to check progress Building by building, SpaceXSI is assembling SI compute at a speed no one else on Earth can matchOctober 31, 2026

In August, the company stated that it would remove its unpermitted turbine generators and replace them with a larger permitted power plant — but that could take at least a year. The latest setback adds to a string of lawsuits surrounding the gas-powered infrastructure used to power xAI’s data centers. In June, the DOJ had asked the court to dismiss the NACCP’s lawsuit, arguing that Colossus 2 supports crucial national security operations. That request was, however, rejected. More recently, SpaceX started manufacturing turbine blades in-house to fast-track generator production for its xAI data centers.

#10527. Anthropic clamps down on Claude’s AI video game clones

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Anthropic is steering Claude away from re-creating specific titles, but users can still use it to make their own games.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Anthropic has updated Claude Code’s system prompt to prevent the AI tool from being used to re-create existing video games, as reported by Tom’s Hardware. Retro gaming is currently being overrun with AI-made decompilations, recompilations, and ports, but while Anthropic will still let people use Claude Code to make games, it doesn’t want people to use it for direct clones.

Last week, a user on X posted a screenshot allegedly showing two new sentences added to Claude Code’s system prompt: “Do build original games, games in a genre, and games from the user’s own designs and assets. Don’t build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is named or called original.”

Anthropic’s Addy Osmani, in a reply, didn’t confirm that specific language but did say that the company had updated the prompt in Claude Code on Opus 5.5:

We added two sentences to Claude Code’s prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else’s specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.

Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to keep working.

We’ll continue to iterate to ensure we’re striking the right balance. In the meantime, if Claude turns down something that’s yours, send it my way.

We’ve asked Anthropic if it has or will be updating other Claude system prompts as well.

#10525. Samsung can’t decide between a larger Galaxy Z Fold 9 or one with worse specs, and neither can I

Following a smash hit in the smaller and wider Galaxy Z Fold 8, Samsung is obviously weighing its options behind the scenes when it comes to sequels. According to one leaker, the company is currently deciding between adding another Galaxy Z Fold 9 with a larger display, or one with lesser specs, and I can’t decide which one I want to see either.

Leaker Lanzuk on Naver says that Samsung is adding more options to the Galaxy Z Fold 9 lineup next year after the Galaxy Z Fold 8 garnered “much better response than expected” (translated). It’s all-but-official that Samsung is expanding the lineup – we’ve heard this repeatedly at this point – but exactly what joins the lineup remains unclear.

This latest rumor suggests two possibilities.

The first is a larger version of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, a “Galaxy Z Fold 9 Plus,” the leaker suggests. This wouldn’t be an Ultra device, but rather would share the same wide form factor as the standard Fold 9, just with a larger pair of displays.

The other option is a “Galaxy Z Fold 9 FE” which would downgrade the specs to achieve a lower price point. Samsung tried this with the Galaxy Z Flip 7 FE to essentially no success, but the company’s cuts on that device were far from compelling on a form factor that was already in decline.

Samsung is said to be testing both form factors and “a decision will be made soon,” but only one of the two would move to production and a full release.

Personally, I’m split here. I’ve been using the Galaxy Z Fold 8 as my daily smartphone since its launch, and the size is certainly one of its most attractive features. On the one hand, I’d love to have a larger version for better media consumption and multitasking, but I also think Samsung would run into diminishing returns with a larger size. The wider form factor wouldn’t scale nicely to a larger size too, I think.

Meanwhile, a more affordable version seems like a clear win. This form factor has so clearly resonated with customers, and having an option that makes strategic cuts to hit a price of say, $1,400, would be a home run. However, given Samsung’s existing struggles with profitability amid RAMageddon and ever-rising prices, that also feels unrealistic.

I’m very torn on which would be the better option. Which one would you prefer?

#10523. Generating Web-Ready Images With AI: Formats, Sizes, and srcset Done Right

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming an important part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product visuals, blog illustrations, backgrounds, and other digital content. However, creating an image is only the first step. Preparing it for efficient web delivery is equally important.

Unoptimized images can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay page rendering. These problems can affect Core Web Vitals, especially Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques, including image resizing, modern formats, Node.js automation, responsive delivery, and performance testing.

Images often represent a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. When developers upload large images without processing them, visitors may experience slower loading times, particularly on mobile networks.

Large image files that take longer to download.

Incorrect dimensions for different screen sizes.

Missing image dimensions that cause layout shifts.

Excessive compression that reduces visual quality.

Unnecessary downloads caused by poor responsive configuration.

A consistent optimization workflow helps address these problems while maintaining the intended visual appearance.

Before preparing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width hero banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small article illustration.

These are suggested dimensions, not fixed requirements. The actual size depends on the website layout and the devices being supported.

When preparing source visuals, developers can compare different AI image tools to understand how image generation workflows handle aspect ratios, output dimensions, and visual details.

Regardless of the generation method, keep an original high-quality version. This master file can be used to create smaller versions without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Choosing an appropriate image format can reduce file size while maintaining acceptable visual quality.

AVIF: Provides efficient compression for many photographs and detailed illustrations.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and environments requiring broad compatibility.

PNG: Suitable for screenshots, lossless graphics, and images that require transparency.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare the resulting file sizes and inspect image quality before selecting a format.

For technical details, consult the MDN Web Docs guide to image file formats.

Manually resizing multiple images can become time-consuming. Automation provides a consistent way to generate different image dimensions and formats.

Sharp is a Node.js library that supports resizing, compression, and image conversion.

The script generates WebP and AVIF versions at several widths.

The quality values are starting points. Adjust them after checking the output for compression artifacts, loss of detail, and unnecessary file size.

For additional configuration options, refer to the official Sharp documentation.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on available resources and screen layout.

The HTML srcset attribute provides multiple image candidates, while sizes describes the expected rendered width.

The browser uses the available image candidates and layout information to select a suitable resource.

Make sure the sizes attribute accurately reflects the actual website layout. Incorrect values can cause browsers to download larger images than necessary.

Responsive delivery is particularly useful when the same visual appears in different layouts across desktop, tablet, and mobile devices.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide alternative image formats with a fallback.

The browser selects a supported source, while the img element provides fallback behavior and alternative text.

Always verify that the referenced files exist and that their dimensions match the intended layout.

Images without defined dimensions can cause page content to move while resources are loading. This can negatively affect Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Include width and height attributes whenever possible:

The browser can use the dimensions to reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

Images below the initial viewport can also use native lazy loading:

However, avoid lazy-loading the main hero image when it contributes to LCP. Important above-the-fold images should be loaded promptly.

Reducing file size should not significantly affect the visual details required by the website.

This is particularly important for images containing:

A practical quality-checking process includes:

Compare the optimized image with its original version.

Inspect the image at its actual display dimensions.

Test the output on mobile and desktop screens.

The smallest file is not necessarily the best option. A slightly larger image may provide noticeably better visual quality.

After implementing the optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Open Chrome DevTools and select the Network panel. Filter requests by images and reload the webpage.

Mobile devices receive appropriately sized images.

The browser downloads unnecessary image files.

Modern formats are being served correctly.

Important images load without unnecessary delays.

Image dimensions match their display requirements.

You can inspect the image source selected by the browser using:

For websites containing multiple images, inspect each relevant element.

Finally, run an audit using Google PageSpeed Insights. It can help identify oversized images and other performance issues.

The web.dev guide to optimizing LCP also provides useful technical guidance for diagnosing loading delays.

Remember that image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, and JavaScript execution can also influence page loading.

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one image format always provides the best compression.

Using incorrect responsive image settings.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Ignoring the actual image selected by the browser.

Measuring file size without checking visual quality.

A proper testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but results depend on image content, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best approach.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

Yes. Reducing the size of the main content image, providing responsive versions, and avoiding unnecessary loading delays can improve LCP when that image is the largest contentful element.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of a webpage should generally load promptly.

The fundamental optimization techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, generated visuals may contain complex textures and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design, but efficient delivery requires more than simply exporting and uploading a visual.

Choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive delivery. Include image dimensions to prevent layout shifts, prioritize important visual elements, and test the results across different devices.

By following these practices, developers can maintain visual quality, reduce unnecessary downloads, and create faster, more reliable website experiences.
