Musk's memo called out Altman's claim that it's "quite terrible" for home computers to have RAM amid a global supply crunch.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

SpaceX CEO Elon Musk has taken to X, the social network that he owns, to mock OpenAI CEO Sam Altman's stance that it's "quite terrible" for home computer users to have DRAM in their machines. Altman made the claim during an appearance on the YouTube channel Cheeky Pint, noting that it would be better for DRAM to be used in AI data centers instead. Mocking the point, Musk shared a meme on X that referenced a movie in which a German WWII officer appears to be hunting for people hiding sticks of DRAM.

Altman's stance has already drawn plenty of ire across the internet. "From a global allocation perspective, actually, it's quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world's limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time," Altman said during the YouTube interview. "You want maximum utilization in the cloud if you're trying to deal with this very supply-constrained environment. And somehow many people's intuition is the opposite of that."

The people deserve to have their RAM too! https://t.co/q6dwauALw4November 1, 2026

OpenAI does, of course, offer some open-source models, but it makes its money from models hosted in data centers, which it can charge users to access at home. With that in mind, it's perhaps unsurprising that Altman would rather people not have home computers that are capable of running local models, negating the need for cloud-based alternatives. However, Altman's comments are ill-timed — computer enthusiasts and average users alike are facing increased prices across the board, whether buying DRAM kits themselves or computers as a whole. As chipmakers retool factories to produce components for AI data centers rather than the consumer market, prices have skyrocketed on everything from DRAM kits to the storage used in devices of all shapes and sizes.

Even Apple, a company previously thought immune to component price increases, hiked prices across its lineups earlier this year. Its recent iPhone launches saw a price increase across the board, too. Beyond Apple, Lenovo said in June that it didn't expect prices to return to pre-2026 levels anytime soon, if ever. Makers of DRAM, Samsung and SK hynix, have also warned that AI-driven memory shortages will last until 2027 and beyond.

As the perceived demand for more AI data centers shows little sign of slowing, the demand for the components that power them will similarly maintain levels that put all other technology at risk of price increases and limited availability.

#10517. Public Discord messages might start showing up in Google Search, unless users opt out

Discord announced today that Discoverable Server web pages will begin showing summaries and pulled content, which might show up in search engines.

Discord splits its servers into three different types: private, community, and discoverable. The latter is the most public version. No one needs an invite, and anyone can message, given that the admins of that server haven’t blocked you completely.

In a recent press release, Discord notes that all Discoverable Servers will get a Server Web Page. The page is essentially an overall summary of that server, so users can easily gauge whether they its something they want to join.

Discord says that discussion summaries will also appear on those server web pages, extracted from public channels. Any conversation that might be deemed noteworthy, whether by an AI model or moderator, could appear on that web page, and by extension, search engines like Google.

It sounds like Discord is hoping channels surface in Google’s AI Mode or other engines, like Reddit, when users search for certain topics. It’s not a bad idea, as message boards often contain answers to incredibly niche questions otherwise hard to find on the internet.

The release makes it clear that server admins can exclude certain channels and channel summaries, but doesn’t say the entire server can opt out. That seems to only be possible by switching server types.

Discord says these changes will take effect starting November 16. Admins and users wishing to opt out of having their public messages and channels included in web pages, and by extension, search results, will need to opt out before then.

#10515. Xbox’s smallest-ever controller is surprisingly comfortable

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Even though I knew Microsoft’s new Xbox Nano controller was tiny, I still gasped when I unboxed it a few days ago. The compact controller is small enough to slip into a pocket unnoticed, and it has been designed specifically for younger people, or for anyone with smaller hands.

I’m not young, but I do have small hands, and I’ve been surprised at how comfortable the Nano is during gaming sessions. At just over 5 inches long, almost everything on this controller has been shrunk down somehow. The ABXY buttons are ever so slightly smaller than a regular Xbox controller, and the thumbsticks feel like miniature versions. Microsoft hasn’t changed the D-pad, though, or the size of the glowing Xbox button at the center of the controller.

The big difference I noticed with the Nano over a regular Xbox controller was the size of the triggers. While they are fully analog and pressure sensitive, they have more of a click when you bottom them out compared with the thud on a bigger Xbox controller. They’re noticeably smaller, as you’d expect, and the bumper buttons above the triggers feel less clicky than the regular ones.

I thought the Nano controller would feel awkward after a few hours of gaming, but the grips really help with comfort levels — a lot of smaller controllers sacrifice grips to keep the controller thin and lightweight. It does mean the Nano doesn’t sit flush on a table, but at 101 grams (0.22 pounds) it’s still really lightweight.

I can’t speak for those with bigger hands, but I’d expect the Nano controller won’t be well-suited to everyone. The size makes it ideal for young children, though. I tried to get my 1-year-old son to try the Nano, and he responded with a smile, so I guess he likes it.

I haven’t had enough time to get a full idea of battery life yet, but it seems to drain around 10 percent in around 30 to 40 minutes. That suggests a lot less battery life than the 30 hours you’d get on a rechargeable pack for the larger Xbox wireless controller. Thankfully, the Nano charges quickly, and I found I could get from 30 percent to 60 percent charge in less than 10 minutes. You can plug in a USB-C charger to top up the battery and carry on playing while you charge.

The only thing missing on the Nano controller that I would have loved is vibration support. I assume it’s not there because of an impact on battery life, because even the smaller Nintendo Joy-Con controllers have rumble support.

The Xbox Nano controller will be available on November 4th, priced at $49.99.

Meanwhile, we now have direct reports of reverse engineering efforts being blocked, while online complaints grow.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic's recent move to have Claude Code deny video game cloning and possibly reverse engineering efforts has come under heavy fire from the software development community. Hot on the heels of Friday's report on the matter, an Anthropic employee responded to the tweet with the purported rule update with a direct statement confirming that the firm added guardrails to Claude Code to prevent game recreation.

In the full tweet, which you can read below, Addy Osmani says that "[Anthropic] added two sentences to Claude Code's prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else's specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against [its] usage policy." He goes on to state that work on original games is meant to continue working, and that Anthropic is iterating on the rules to try and "strike the right balance."

We added two sentences to Claude Code's prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else's specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy. Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to keep working. We'll continue to iterate to ensure we're striking the right balance. In the meantime, if Claude turns down something that's yours, send it my way.October 30, 2026

There's no telling what those sentences might be, though given Osmani's choice of words, it's possible that said sentences are indeed what X user Chasm posted a screenshot of: "Do build original games, games in a genre, and games from the user’s own designs and assets. Don’t build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is renamed or called original."

In our previous report, we had already linked to recent issues raised at Claude Code's own GitHub repository, comprised of users complaining that the AI assistant was refusing to work on their games or on reverse-engineering tasks. As far as we can ascertain, it appears the game-related blocking is limited to the combination of using Claude Code and the Opus 5.5 model. Should that prove true, then users should be able to sidestep the limitation by calling upon Opus using another harness like Ollama or forwarding specific requests through a wrapper of some sort.

Regarding the blocking of reverse engineering, we have received direct confirmations. The first comes from our own alumnus Jarred Walton, who was trying to run checks on the software he's developing. He notes that such work had been performed with no issue until yesterday, October 31, and that switching to Opus 4.8 let him continue his work.

Walton theorizes that Anthropic may have added a new rule to Code specifically for reverse-engineering. Considering that timing, plus the fact that other reports of refusals all popped up around the last week, may indicate that the company could be fiddling with cybersecurity-related rules in the same manner as they are for video game-related content.

We also received screenshots from a staffer's direct acquaintance, who asked Opus 5.5 (via Code) to try and reverse engineer a product activation, a request that likewise triggered safeguards. Meanwhile, notices that Claude Code appears to block reverse-engineering continue to mount, now with a Redditor allegedly having their account suspended, and then reinstated, over using Opus 5.5 and Reverse Engineer Anything. The company has an ongoing Cyber Verification Program, in which only approved entities get access to the full set of frontier models' cybersecurity capabilities.

#10513. RAMageddon is ready to rage on through 2030

SK Hynix, one of the major companies behind memory production, says that it’s sold out through 2029, leaving RAMageddon ready to rage on for at least the next three years.

On its latest earnings call today, SK Hynix confirmed that production of both DRAM and HBM (high-bandwidth memory) are effectively sold out not just for this year, not just for next year, but through 2029. That puts RAMageddon, the global shortage of memory, lasting at least another three years before we see any relief.

SK Hynix’s CFO explains (via The Motley Fool, Seeking Alpha):

…memory supply cannot keep pace with this level of demand growth, thus, we expect it to be difficult to fully meet customers’ volume requirements in the near term, given the complexities involved in implementing leading-edge processes and the lead times required to construct new production facilities. A situation where supply cannot keep up with demand is expected to persist for some time, a constraint projected to continue through 2030.

Micron, one of the other companies responsible for memory production, previously said to expect 2027 to be “even tighter” compared to 2026.

The SK Hynix call also details how the company is making long-term agreements which already account for “more than 50% of our total capacity for 2027 and 2028.”

SK Hynix isn’t the biggest name in DRAM/HBM production, with Samsung accounting for a larger share, but everyone else is dealing with the same constraints. PCMag points out that SK Hynix is working to build out more production capacity, as everyone else is too, but it takes time to build out the complex infrastructure.

Buckle up folks, this isn’t ending anytime soon. 🫠

#10508. Amazon’s latest Echo smart displays and speakers reinforce Google Home’s lost lead

Amazon just launched a new lineup of Echo smart speakers and a new smart display, all while Google Home’s lineup remains pretty quiet.

Amazon has five new devices launching – two smart displays, a “Photo Frame,” and two speakers.

Starting with the speakers, the latest Echo Dot has “a built-in smart home hub” and upgraded audio and starts shipping today for $100. Meanwhile, Echo Studio builds on that foundation with even-better audio and, optionally, a built-in camera. Those start at $220.

On the smart display side, there’s the Echo Time, which is like an Echo Dot but with a small circular display built-in. Amazon touts “hundreds of clock faces” and “Omnisense” which can track your sleep just like Google’s second-gen Nest Hub, and it starts at $200.

Echo Show 16, meanwhile, is a big 16-inch smart display meant to live on your wall with support for smart home controls, the ability to show a family calendar, and full Fire TV built-in as well. It starts at $500 and, like everything else mentioned so far, is built around Amazon’s AI-enhanced Alexa.

Rounding out the lineup is the Alexa Photo Frame, a simpler smart display that has Alexa built-in but focuses on showing photos from your Amazon Photos library. It has a 10-inch anti-glare display and a design that looks like a normal photo frame and starts at $200.

Google Home really is just so wildly behind isn’t it.

In looking at Amazon’s lineup, I can’t help but feel like Google is just wasting potential here. A smart speaker that doubles as a Nest Cam would be awesome. The same goes for a giant Nest Hub that doubles as a Google TV.

I still don't understand how nobody is questioning Google when they still don't support basics stuff like:

casting from the phone using a "video" app (YouTube, Netflix, etc) to Google Cast speakers -> AirPlay has supported this for a decade

being able to set Google Cast speakers as audio output devices of Google TV devices (and not the extremely limited - and frankly, basically useless - implementation that there's only between Google TV Streamer and the new Google Home Speaker) -> both Amazon and Apple (as well as many competitors) have supported this for several years, including with with surround setups (4 speakers)

Their latest Google Home Speaker was a major disappointment. I almost have no doubt that a new Google Home Hub and a new Google TV Streamer will be disappointments too.

It's years that I keep wishing for Google to prove me wrong, but all they do is keep digging themselves deeper in their grave.

And, seriously, how does Google not sell (or at least partner with someone) a Google Photos digital photo frame. Google Photos is a wildly successful platform and that would sell like hotcakes. Big “how am I not in that movie” energy.

I’ve said it before, but Google Home had such a lead in the smart speaker and smart display market, especially with Google’s ecosystem tie-ins, but Amazon is currently running laps around it with products that Google should be selling. Throw in that even Apple has a smart display now where Google hasn’t even teased a new Nest Hub, and it just feels all kinds of bad to be in Google’s camp right now.

#10507. Indie games are missing launch dates because Steam is backed up

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Many indie game developers have recently posted on social media about unexpectedly long approval wait times for their game submissions on Steam, with some of the approvals causing developers to miss their planned launch dates.

For example, Studio Daimon, the team behind an upcoming title called Food Devils, had planned to launch the game in early access today after submitting its build on October 12th. But the game is “still awaiting approval,” according to a post from game director Alfred Kamon. “Even now, we don’t know when Steam will give us the final OK to finally release our game. We sent another ticket last week saying that the situation was urgent and that we might miss our release date because of this, but it also got ignored.”

In October, publisher 2 Left Thumbs said it missed its planned launch of Wheel Make It “by nearly two weeks” due to the long wait for build reviews. You can read some details about the studio’s setbacks, including failing a review for a “one single option in the menu” not being accessible with a controller, in a Steam post. There are lots of other recent posts about review build waits as well.

Valve says on a Steam developer website that a review of a game’s store presence “typically takes 3-5 business days to complete,” though the company suggests submitting a page for review “at least 7 business days before you want it live to account for potential changes you’ll need to make.”

However, as of late, Steam has seemingly been grappling with a sharp increase in games; October’s Steam Next Fest had 7,240 games, a 65.2 percent increase over June’s Steam Next Fest, according to How to Market a Game. A Reddit user also found that 49.9 percent of all games released since Valve started asking developers in 2024 to label games that use AI now have an AI disclosure.

Kamon and 2 Left Thumbs suspect the longer waits are due to “slop” slowing down the process. “We can only assume it’s because Steam’s review queue is clogged up by AI slop, but the whole process is now taking several weeks for some developers,” said Kamon.

In a new post from Valve published after we first ran this story, the company acknowledged that following a “a significant increase in submissions for store and page review in recent months,” it’s “running a little behind right now” and taking “up to 12 business days” for reviews, saying that much of the delay is “having to re-review games.” It has updated its release checklist for developers, including requiring them to run a game through Steam for at least five minutes. Valve also says that further improvements are on the way for the build review process.

Itch.io has also been dealing with a rise in games, with the platform saying last weekend that “The number of game pages being created per-day has doubled in the past 2 months.” Last night, the platform said it “Just deleted a couple terabytes of ai slop 😌”

Update, November 3rd: Added new post from Valve.

The Russian Federal State Statistics Service (Rosstat) admitted that its servers were hit by coordinated cyberattacks between October 29 and 31. #10505. Generators and Iterators in JavaScript: Lazy Data and Async Flows

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most JavaScript code loads data first and processes it afterward. That works for a list of ten items, but it falls apart when the list has a million entries, never ends, or arrives one network page at a time. Generators and iterators offer another approach: produce values only when someone asks for them. This article explains the iteration protocol, shows how generators build on it, and finishes with async generators for paginated data.

An iterator is any object with a next() method. Each call returns an object with two properties: value, the next item, and done, a boolean that becomes true when the sequence ends. An iterable is an object that hands out an iterator through a method keyed by Symbol.iterator. Arrays, strings, Maps and Sets are all iterables, which is why for...of, spread syntax and destructuring work on them.

Spread syntax calls [Symbol.iterator]() once, then calls next() until done is true. The code is correct but noisy. You manage the state yourself, and every new sequence needs the same scaffolding.

A generator function, declared with function*, returns an iterator without the manual bookkeeping. The yield keyword pauses the function and sends a value to the caller. The next call to next() resumes execution exactly where it stopped, with all local variables intact.

Calling countdown(3) does not run the body. It creates a generator object, and the body runs in slices as the consumer asks for values. Generator objects are iterable too, so spread and for...of work on them directly. The suspended state is the whole trick: the function body acts as a small state machine that the engine writes for you.

Values can also flow back in. The argument passed to next() becomes the result of the yield expression:

The first call to next() only runs the generator up to its first yield, so any argument passed to it is ignored. That detail trips up many developers.

Laziness is where generators earn their place. Array methods such as map and filter process the whole array at each step and allocate a new array each time. A generator pipeline handles one item at a time through the entire chain, which makes it possible to work with sequences that have no end:

The naturals generator never finishes, yet the pipeline completes because take stops asking after four results. No array of squares is ever built, and no number beyond 49 is computed.

There is a useful cleanup rule here. When a for...of loop exits early, through break, return or a thrown error, it calls the iterator's return() method. For a generator, that runs any finally blocks and ends the sequence. In the pipeline above, finishing take releases the generators upstream as well, so a generator that holds a file handle or a database cursor can close it inside a finally block.

Real applications spend more time waiting than computing, and async generators bring the same lazy model to asynchronous work. Declare one with async function* and consume it with for await...of. Each iteration waits for a promise to settle before the loop body runs.

A paginated API is a natural fit. Assume each response contains an items array and a nextUrl property that is null on the last page. This generator requests one page at a time and yields its items:

Once collectTitles has enough titles, the return statement ends the loop, which calls return() on the async generator. No further requests are made. Without a generator you would write a recursive function or a manual loop with its own stopping logic, and callers would have no say over the pace.

Async iteration also suits streams. In Node.js, readable streams are async iterable, so for await...of lets you process a large file chunk by chunk instead of holding all of it in memory.

Iterators define how to walk a sequence, generators make writing them painless, and async generators extend the same idea to data that arrives over time. Start small: replace one function that builds a large intermediate array with a generator, or one manual pagination loop with an async generator. The pull-based model keeps memory use predictable and gives the consumer control over how much work gets done.

As prices soar, you'll want to pick up a bargain on storage when you can find it. For a limited time only, you can pick up a 512GB Samsung P9 microSD Express card for just $99.99 at Amazon. This price brings it back down to its original MSRP and means you're saving $50 compared to its current list price. This is the perfect option for a Nintendo Switch 2 owner looking to boost their storage for new games.

The Samsung P9 is one of the best microSD Express cards you can buy right now, delivering ultra-fast sequential read speeds of up to 800 MB/s. Those speeds mean it's a great option for installing games and playing them on your Switch 2 without gameplay lag. Write speeds aren't shared, but in this class and capacity, you can usually expect up to 300 MB/s.

The Samsung P9 is a microSD Express card that offers great performance and reliability for its price. The 512GB model is currently priced at its original MSRP of $99.99.

You'll want the highest possible write speeds, but for a Switch 2 owner, 300 MB/s is still going to be fast enough to install new games and updates without huge waiting times. The high speeds you're seeing here will mean you won't have too big a bottleneck compared to installing directly onto the Switch 2's internal storage.

That said, these speeds make the Samsung P9 Express up to four times faster than a typical UHS-I microSD card. It also ships with features to help get the most out of your card, including Host Memory Buffer (HMB) functionality to help improve data transfer speeds, along with Dynamic Thermal Guard (DTG) support to help stop the card from overheating and limiting its performance.

microSD cards are small, so durability is an issue to think about, especially if you're handling it often. Samsung reckons this is a model that's well protected, even if you drop it in water, along with protection against extreme temperatures and even X-ray exposure. It has a 3-year limited warranty, and it supports Samsung's Magician software, so you can keep an eye on the card's health, as well as check if it's genuine or not (although that's not an issue here).

The Switch 2 is one of the first consoles to include microSD Express support, so you can use this Samsung P9 Express card's faster storage speeds compared to older UHS-I cards. The $99.99 sale price makes this 512GB Samsung P9 a brilliant bargain to pick up ahead of the holiday season, especially if you're a current or soon-to-be Switch 2 owner. Google secures approval for its Minnesota data center despite local protests, lawsuits, and power supply concerns

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Google has secured approval for a proposed data center in Hermantown, Minnesota, following a unanimous vote by the city council, MPR News reports. The decision, reached on Friday, October 30, clears a major hurdle for the company following at least two postponed meetings and hours of public opposition, though the project still faces lawsuits and a separate inquiry into its electricity supply. The proposed development could cover just over 200 acres in a rural residential area outside Duluth with three data center buildings spanning approximately 1.66 million square feet.

The council approved a development agreement, a special use permit, and provisions for decommissioning the site if construction is abandoned or the facility eventually closes. The meeting, which was capped at 85 people and offered a livestream, ended shortly before midnight, with some criticizing the attendance restriction. Council member John Geisler, however, clarified the move, noting that the decision concerned local land use application rather than the wide debate over artificial intelligence or big technology companies.

“What we've done tonight is not about AI. It's not about big tech. It's not about software. It's about a project here in Hermantown, a land use application, and agreements that are associated with that,” Geissler said.

The approval comes at a hefty price for Google, with the company being forced to make several changes during the approval process. The company changed its initial plan of putting up four buildings to three, capped their height at 33 feet, and agreed to monitor nearby private wells —but each building would still be larger than six football fields. Google also agreed to withdraw an earlier request for up to $80 million in property tax breaks. The revised arrangement is now expected to generate about $3 million in annual property tax for the city, with a separate decommissioning agreement valued at $210 million in place.

Supporters point to those provisions as evidence that Google had responded to local concerns. Opponents, however, argue that the changes did not address the important questions of whether such a colossal development belongs in the area. The dispute echoes other data center battles within local communities, with Alabama residents being unable to halt a 50MW Bitcoin mining facility in August, despite local moratoriums, exposing gaps in the state's zoning rules. In September, a proposal for a 1,300-acre data center in Pennsylvania by NorthPoint Development also faced resistance despite its developer offering $10,000 to thousands of nearby households. Key issues raised mostly by residents in both cases have been about noise and water consumption by such facilities and property values.

It’s important to note, however, that Hermantown’s vote does not mean Google can immediately begin construction. Three lawsuits filed by The Minnesota Center for Environmental Advocacy and Stop the Hermantown Data Center challenge the city’s zoning process and ownership of the land, and are also demanding the project’s environmental review. The project’s environmental needs are also another pain point, given it requires about 500 megawatts, comparable to the combined demands of homes in Minneapolis, St. Paul, Duluth, and Rochester, as per the Citizens Utility Board of Minnesota. Google still needs approval from the Minnesota Public Utilities Commission on power consumption, despite its agreement with Minnesota Power.

The scale of demand is a recurring point of friction in data center proposals. Last year, the OpenAI Stargate facility in Michigan received approval for a 1.4-gigawatt power supply — construction, however, started in June 2026 despite objections from local residents. Google is also pursuing a separate data center project near Pine Island, close to Rochester. That proposal is now subject to a more extensive environmental review following a legal settlement.

#10494. AI model gets a 32-bit RISC-V CPU compiled directly into its weights, then runs DOOM on it

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Project takes a stock Qwen 3.5 4B and compiles a RISC-V CPU into its weights.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AI engineer Divyansh Agrawal has revealed the Qwenputer. Described in its most basic terms, Qwenputer is an experimental blend of the stock Qwen 3.5 4B AI LLM with a 32-bit RISC-V CPU compiled and inserted directly into its weights. In use, Qwenputer can run processor arithmetic operations – or even run C and MicroPython code on its built-in CPU, eschewing any external runtime or tool use. Moreover, it can run Doom, albeit very slowly. You can see the Qwenputer running Doom on its RISC-V CPU by expanding the tweet below.

QWenputer by @divagr1925 is so cool! but... can it run DOOM? Ofc yes! E1M1 on the RISC-V CPU compiled into Qwen3.5-4B weights. will opensource too https://t.co/hEa46rD6CJOctober 29, 2026

The implementation of Qwenputer raises some interesting concepts and solutions. For example, the developer used Qwen’s Gated DeltaNet (GND) state as RAM, which Agrawal reckons is “a pretty neat trick.” They admit that using these ‘fast weights’ as RAM isn’t an original concept, but modifying Qwen 3.5 this way does seem to be a first.

Unlike traditional LLM creation, adding a ‘CPU’ doesn’t involve training. This RISC-V CPU implementation “is 11 transformer layers in front of Qwen's own, and none of their weights are learned: a compiler computes every one,” it is explained. Agrawal boasts that Qwenputer’s built-in CPU “passes the official riscv-tests, its programs match qemu, and it has run 18 million instructions free-running without a single error.”

The developer stresses that Qwen makes use of this ‘CPU’ to send instructions and read them back with no external tool or runtime calls. In practice, it works as intended. Use of the CPU inside Qwenputer provides exact calculations, overcoming the arithmetic weaknesses of many LLMs. Moreover, users can run code on the CPU inside the LLM, with C and MicroPython compatibility.

Not content with wedging a CPU into an LLM, Agrawal decided to also give it a filesystem. They describe this extension of the model as relatively trivial, since RAM is already in place. Indeed, the filesystem storage also uses the DeltaNet state. There are 1,024 slots holding up to 512 files. Moreover, this storage is persistent if you save the state at the end of a conversation with the LLM.

Due to its expanded resource use, Qwenputer has basically doubled the size of the underlying Qwen 3.5 4B LLM to almost 19GB. Thus, Agrawal recommends a workstation with 24GB of VRAM or more for testing Qwenputer in its current version. Efforts are being made to streamline coding in Qwenputer, as well as make it run faster.

The project is experimental. One gets the impression that Agrawal built Qwenputer primarily as an experiment to explore what is possible, rather than to solve a specific problem. Nevertheless, the creation of this custom LLM raises some interesting concepts and makes the mind wander regarding potential practical applications.

In addition to the above-linked blog, you can learn more about Qwenputer from its MIT-licensed code on GitHub. If you want to jump straight in and tinker with this fascinating project, you can now grab this LLM from Hugging Face.

#10491. How to Source Passive Candidates with AI in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Passive candidates answer when a message is specific, short and clearly about them. This is a nine-step playbook, and each step shows how to do it in Pin. The method works in any tool. For a shorter version, see Pin's 7-step guide to sourcing passive candidates.

For recruiters sourcing passive talent at scale, Pin covers the workflow end to end. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and ranks them against your brief, and the recruiter makes every call.

A passive candidate is a working professional who isn't applying for jobs but might say yes to the right conversation.

Gallup's fourth-quarter 2025 panel sizes the pool. It found that 51% of U.S. workers are actively looking (11%) or watching for opportunities (40%).

The practical reading: only 11% of workers are actively looking, so most of the market isn't on a job board, and about half is paying attention.

Write the brief as outcomes and skills, not a title. Use five lines:

Skills-based pools are larger. LinkedIn's Economic Graph estimated in March 2025 that defining eligibility by skills rather than prior job title would expand the typical talent pool by a median 6.1x globally and 15.9x in the United States. That is a modeled estimate, with "eligible" meaning at least half of the target job's top skills.

In Pin: paste the job description, add a job listing link, upload two or three ideal résumés, or import the role from your ATS. Pin asks clarifying questions about the brief, such as "Is remote considered?" Answer them. They are the parts of the brief you hadn't written down.

Pin says its AI sourcing searches 850M+ profiles across multiple sources, including North America and Europe, aggregated from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. Ryan Levy, Managing Partner at Cruit Group, says: "Pin gave us the ability to find candidates that didn't appear on LinkedIn Recruiter. The platform is easy to use and is continuing to evolve!"

Here is the same search written two ways. Pin's Boolean search guide for recruiters covers the operators in the first version.

("senior data engineer" OR "staff data engineer" OR "lead data engineer") AND (Snowflake OR BigQuery OR Redshift) AND (dbt OR Airflow) AND (fintech OR payments OR "financial services") NOT (intern OR junior OR consultant OR recruiter)

Senior data engineer who has built a warehouse from scratch at a Series B to D fintech. Strong dbt and Airflow. Remote in US time zones. Avoid consultancies.

The string misses anyone titled "Software Engineer, Data Platform" who has done the same work. Skills-based matching reads the work, so title mismatches stop hiding good people. Filters can be written in natural language, and advanced filters stay under your control.

Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, puts it this way: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

A ranked list is only useful if you can see why each person ranks. Pin gives every candidate a fit score and an AI-written reason. Read the reason first, then accept or reject.

Pin learns from accept and reject feedback across the team, so the second batch should look better than the first. If the reasons show the wrong kind of match, fix the brief rather than rejecting one person at a time.

Spend contact credits after the shortlist, not before it. Pin returns personal email, work email and phone number, at 1 credit each. The Free plan includes 5 credits a week. Solo includes 500 a month, and Professional includes 1,000 per seat a month.

Reference the person's actual work, give one reason this role might fit, and make one small ask. Keep it short. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's own profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice, so you edit rather than start blank. Email steps send automatically. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks with an AI-drafted message, and you send them. Pin's guide to engaging passive candidates goes deeper on message length and personalization.

The messages below are illustrative. They are templates with placeholders, not real results.

Hi [First name], I read your write-up on moving [Company]'s billing pipeline to dbt. The point about [specific lesson] is something we're working through too.

I'm hiring a senior data engineer at [Company]. The job is to [one-sentence outcome]. It's remote in US time zones.

Worth a 15-minute call to see if it's relevant? If not, no problem at all.

Message 2: follow-up in the same thread (about day 3)

Hi [First name], one detail I left out: [team size, comp range or what the first 90 days look like]. If the timing is wrong, tell me and I'll check back in a few months.

Message 3: an SMS task you send (about day 5, only where you have a number and the rules allow)

Hi [First name], [Your name] from [Company]. I emailed about a senior data engineer role. Worth a quick chat? Happy to stop here if not.

Pin reports that its multi-channel outreach sequences deliver 5x better response rates than industry averages, measured against LinkedIn's published 10-25% InMail reply benchmark. That figure is vendor-reported, so test it against your own baseline.

Follow-ups do most of the work. In Pin's Recruiting Outreach Benchmarks 2026, covering 4,000,000+ messages sent between June 2025 and May 2026, follow-ups earned 56.2% of all replies. The first three touches captured 93.2% of the replies a sequence earns, and a fourth raised that to 97.7%. Half of replies arrived within 4 hours and 74.8% within a day, so spacing touches about two business days apart gives each message its chance. The data comes from Pin's own platform, so treat it as a starting point.

A workable plan: three to four touches about two business days apart. Change the content each time, and add a channel when you have a legitimate way to reach the person. In the same data, sequences that paired email with a LinkedIn message were associated with 2 to 4x the replies of email alone at the same touch count. Recruiters add the LinkedIn touch for the candidates they want most, so part of that gap reflects prioritization.

Pin's multi-channel sequences combine automated email steps with AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence. Email sends on schedule. Pin queues each LinkedIn and SMS step as a task with the message drafted, and you send it. Sequences adapt per candidate, any reply pauses the sequence, and every channel for a candidate stays in one thread. A shared candidate pool with a dedupe layer keeps two recruiters from contacting the same person. The Free plan has email-only sequences. Multi-channel sequences start on Solo.

Passive candidates book around a day job, so offer early, late and lunchtime slots. Pin's AI interview scheduling handles time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, and confirmations land in the ATS.

Track positive replies separately, since a reply is not the same as interest.

Change one variable at a time, such as the opening line or the day of the follow-up, and compare within the same role. Pin's funnel dashboards show pipeline velocity, conversion by stage and activity by recruiter. Pin's 2026 user survey across 2,000+ organizations and 20,000+ users reports a 14-day average time-to-fill and 12 hours saved per recruiter per week. Test those vendor-reported figures against your own.

Once steps 1 to 8 work by hand, hand over the repetitive ones. Pin's AI recruiting agent has two modes. In Assist, you approve each step. In Autopilot, the agent sources, screens and ranks, drafts outreach, sends the email steps, follows up on silence, schedules interviews and updates the ATS. LinkedIn and SMS steps stay tasks that you send. Closing and offers stay human. For how other agents compare, see best AI recruiting agents.

Start in Assist on your first two roles and read every draft. Move email follow-ups and scheduling to Autopilot first, since they repeat most. Guardrails keep a human in the loop, every action appears in a feed, and the agent runs 24/7.

Pin also has an MCP server. It lets AI assistants such as Claude Desktop or ChatGPT set up searches, review candidates and tune ranking, and its page says outreach can't be sent silently.

Miles Randle, Head of People & Talent at Flip CX, says: "As a small people and talent team, we don't have a ton of time to spend hours sourcing and messaging. Pin has made it possible for us to focus on the people side of things!"

Several, and they often produce the warmest conversations:

These channels are slow and hard to scale. Use them for senior and niche roles, and add the names to Pin so the dedupe layer prevents double outreach.

Pin is built for outbound sourcing. Teams that only need high-volume inbound applicant screening will get less from it. For everyone else, Pin assists recruiters and never replaces them: a recruiter still reads the reasons and decides.

Profile counts are the vendors' own, and competitor details come from their pricing and product pages as of October 2026. For longer roundups, see SitePoint's 12 best AI recruiting and sourcing tools and AI recruiting tools for agencies. Teams replacing LinkedIn Recruiter can compare options in LinkedIn Recruiter alternatives.

Start with a brief written around skills and outcomes, then search several sources rather than one network. Shortlist by fit, get a verified email or phone number, and write a message that references the person's own work. In Pin (pin.com), you can paste a job description, get a ranked shortlist with an AI-written reason per candidate, and run the email, LinkedIn and SMS sequence from the same product.

Combine AI sourcing for reach with human channels for trust. AI search covers hundreds of millions of profiles and ranks them against your brief, which manual search rarely matches. Referrals, communities and open-source work give warmer introductions for senior or niche roles. Pin covers the first part: search, shortlist, contact data, outreach and scheduling in one workflow.

Keep it under about 100 words. Name something specific from the person's work, say what the role is and why it might fit them, and make one low-pressure ask, such as a 15-minute call. Pin drafts each message from the candidate's own profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice, so you edit a draft instead of starting from a blank page.

Plan three to four touches about two business days apart. In Pin's Recruiting Outreach Benchmarks 2026, built on 4,000,000+ messages, the first three touches captured 93.2% of all replies and a fourth raised that to 97.7%. Stop when someone replies. In Pin, any reply pauses the sequence, and LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks you send.

AI sourcing works from profile and work signals, not job-board applications, so it can surface people who never applied anywhere. Pin says it searches multiple data providers across North America and Europe, including sources such as GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents and academic publications. Read each AI-written reason, accept or reject, and let the model learn from your feedback. A human still decides who to contact.

#10488. Apple finally has a touchscreen MacBook. Why now?

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The Windows world has had touch for over a decade. Is Apple getting in the right way?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Last week, Apple finally brought two new display technologies to the MacBook Pro: OLED and touch. For the former, it was just a matter of time; Apple has been using OLED in the iPhone and Apple Watch for years, and also just brought it to the iPad Mini. But touch has never felt like a sure thing. While many of Apple's most successful products have relied on touch: the iPhone, the iPad, the Apple Watch, and some late-stage iPods all featured touchscreens. But until now, it never made its way to Apple's laptops.

Former Apple CEO Steve Jobs' words are infamous: "It's ergonomically terrible," he said of touchscreen laptops in 2010, describing how "your arm wants to fall off" due to fatigue. "Touch surfaces want to be horizontal," he added.

Since then, touchscreens have become ubiquitous on Windows PCs and Chromebooks. Outside of the Touch Bar, which was ultimately reversed because Mac users were not fans, Apple hasn't had a real touchscreen on the Mac.

That changed, as the company announced 14-inch and 16-inch MacBook Pros with "PROLED XDR" displays with, yes, touch support. The devices are also lighter than existing MacBook Pros, have updated speaker systems, and, yes, are more expensive.

Apple eschewed touch on its laptops for years. But it seems, however, that at least some Mac owners disagreed.

"Apple tends not to move to a new technology until it can bring to market a differentiated product," Tom Mainelli, group vice president of device and consumer research at IDC, told Tom's Hardware. "Based on all the demos they are showing here in LA, the drive for 'why now' is because they see a desire for touch from creators who use the Mac."

He added: "The more business-focused answer is the company is creating further segmentation in a mature market, offering a new, more premium tier to its pro lineup."

There's one last uphill climb that the touchscreen on the new MacBook Pro faces: the price. After all, the touchscreen is debuting on just the new, redesigned 14- and 16-inch models.

In time, touch will likely fall down the stack, to the cheaper MacBook Pro models, the MacBook Air, and possibly even the Neo. You may see a touch iMac at some point. That will take time. After all, the Mac is just getting OLED, too. That means it's unlikely we'll see mass adoption of touch on the Mac anytime soon. It still has to reach the most popular devices, and even then, it has to coexist with the old standbys, keyboard and mouse.

You could trace the touchscreen laptop back to 2008's HP TouchSmart tx2, which had a swiveling screen. The real touch revolution started around two other releases: The Microsoft Surface Laptop RT and the release of Windows 8 in 2012, which had a touch-forward interface and led to a flood of touch laptops.

Windows 10 came and went with more touchscreens, and by the time of Windows 11's release in 2021, touchscreens largely became the default for Windows laptops. On all but the cheapest devices, I tend to assume a Windows notebook has a touchscreen.

On Chromebooks, the 2013 Chromebook Pixel was considered premium because of its touchscreen. In 2019, I reviewed the Samsung Chromebook Pro and Plus, as Google first experimented with Android apps in Chrome. I remember sitting there poking and prodding the device, thinking that "if Android apps are going to be on Chromebooks, every single one needs a touchscreen." I was seven years too early.

When Apple introduced the MacBook Neo earlier this year, it shocked the industry on price and performance. But it did cut some features, like a backlit keyboard. But some companies that made competitors, such as Dell with its XPS 13, made a point to add that not only did it have a backlit keyboard — it had a touchscreen. (HP also kept the touchscreen with its OmniBook 5. Acer did away with it on the Swift Air 14).

In an interview with Steven Levy at Wired, Apple's chief hardware officer Johny Srouji said that "Ten years is a very very long time," and said that a lot has changed, from display technology to custom silicon to software architecture, which now "met our bar." In the same interview, Apple's senior vice president of worldwide marketing, Greg Joswiak, explained that most creatives have an iPhone or iPad and find it natural: "There’s a generation of young creators and young people using our MacBook Pros that expect to touch."

This might betray my age, but in general, outside of tablets and 2-in-1 laptops, I don't make a ton of use of touchscreens on computers. It hasn't bothered me once in the past five years that my personal MacBook Pro doesn't have a touchscreen. In general, I agree with Jobs; The ergonomics aren't great.

There are other reasons you might skip a touchscreen. For a long time, they were thought of as battery hogs. The digitizer can add extra weight or cost. And operating systems haven't been designed with touch in mind.

Windows 11 was supposed to be more designed for touchscreens, having taken cues from the unreleased Windows 10X, designed for touchscreens and foldables. But Windows is Windows, and the underlying OS still feels best with a keyboard and mouse.

One of the best operating systems I've seen for touch is Googlebook OS, if only because it's so based on Android 17 that some parts make more sense with a finger than a keyboard and mouse.

For macOS 27 Golden Gate, Apple has added system gesture APIs to make apps more likely to support touch. Apple says its own apps are ready, and in its press release, listed a number of other apps like the Microsoft Office suite, Zoom, Notion, Da Vinci Resolve, Adobe Lightroom, Blender, and Algoriddim DJay Paro.

But it also didn't remove the keyboard and touchpad. It's a Mac with a touchscreen, but not touch-first.

For creative professionals who need true accuracy, specialty tablets that work as peripherals will still be king.

There's one last uphill climb that the touchscreen on the new MacBook Pro faces: the price. After all, the touchscreen is debuting on just the new, redesigned 14- and 16-inch models, starting at $2,899 for the 14-incher and $3,399 for the 16-inch models, respectively.

In time, touch will likely fall down the stack to the cheaper Pros, the MacBook Air, and possibly even the Neo. You may see a touch iMac at some point. That will take time. After all, the Mac is just getting OLED, too.

That means it's unlikely we'll see mass adoption of touch on the Mac anytime soon. It still has to reach the most popular devices, and even then, it has to coexist with the old standbys, keyboard and mouse.

#10485. Announcing DPoP Support for Google OAuth: Securing Refresh Tokens

Traditional OAuth bearer tokens operate on a "finders-keepers" model, making them vulnerable to Infostealer malware (e.g. RedLine, NodeStealer) and Adversary-in-the-Middle (AiTM) attacks.

DPoP (Demonstrating Proof-of-Possession) is an application-layer OAuth 2.0 security profile (RFC 9449) that neutralizes token theft and replay attacks by cryptographically binding tokens to a client-generated asymmetric keypair at the application layer, ensuring that even if a token is stolen, it cannot be replayed without the corresponding private key.

This article is meant to inform you on where and how to adopt DPoP and describes Google's specific DPoP implementation. A DPoP adoption SKILL.md is also available to help implement DPoP.

Some optional features and implementation decisions in RFC 9449 are delegated to Google as an authorization provider. Google Identity, and its OAuth Platform, function as a developer-facing service where engineers implement OAuth 2.0 and cryptographic security controls like DPoP to protect API integrations. Separately, Google Workspace Context-Aware Access (CAA) leverages DPoP to secure enterprise AI workloads on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform by cryptographically validating agent identities at the Agent Gateway.

Google's OAuth Platform supports DPoP binding specifically for refresh tokens. This design choice reflects a pragmatic engineering tradeoff: by sender-constraining the long-lived refresh token, we neutralize the primary vector for persistent offline impersonation. Meanwhile, short-lived access tokens remain standard Bearer tokens, ensuring that high-QPS resource APIs do not incur the per-request asymmetric cryptographic verification overhead of DPoP. This approach delivers robust protection for your most sensitive credentials while maintaining the high performance and low latency of Google API integrations.

Unlike standard Bearer token flows, DPoP requires client-side cryptographic key generation, strict timestamp and nonce handling, specific claim computation (htm, htu, iat, jti), exact header construction, and DPoP proof JWT construction. These changes may introduce additional system constraints to support secure computation, handling, and storage.

The IETF’s Best Current Practice for OAuth 2.0 Security (RFC 9700) discusses sender-constrained refresh tokens. Additionally, the OAuth 2.0 for Browser-Based Applications IETF draft discusses three models: Backend-for-Frontend (BFF), Token-Mediating Backend, and Browser-based OAuth Client.

Following this guidance, Google recommends using DPoP to sender-contrain refresh tokens in these models.

Unlike transport-layer token binding (e.g., mTLS/RFC 8705) which often breaks or terminates at modern cloud proxies and load balancers, DPoP operates at the HTTP header level. This makes it highly resilient for complex, multi-hop environments, including emerging Model Context Protocol (MCP) and autonomous agent architectures.

A common question from security architects is: If DPoP is designed to sender-constrain tokens, why are Google's access tokens still standard Bearer tokens?

The answer lies in operational scaling and performance at Google's scale. Verifying asymmetric cryptographic signatures (such as ES256) requires significant CPU cycles. If every API call to high-throughput Google resource servers (like Gmail, Calendar, or Drive APIs) required verifying a DPoP proof, the compute overhead at the API Gateway layer would be massive, introducing measurable latency and scaling costs.

By constraining DPoP binding strictly to the refresh token, Google's architecture achieves a balance:

Long-Lived Credentials Protected: The highly sensitive, long-lived refresh token (which can be used to obtain new access tokens indefinitely) is cryptographically bound to the client's key pair. If exfiltrated, it is useless to an attacker.

High-Performance Serving: Short-lived access tokens (typically valid for 1 hour or less) remain standard Bearer tokens. This ensures that the vast majority of API requests incur zero cryptographic verification overhead, maintaining the high performance and low latency developers expect from Google APIs.

DPoP is a powerful tool for securing OAuth 2.0 tokens, but it is important to understand its boundaries.

As autonomous AI agents and Model Context Protocol (MCP) architectures emerge, securing tool-use tokens becomes critical. While DPoP is highly resilient in single-hop client-to-server connections, multi-agent workflows (where Agent A delegates a task to Agent B, which calls Resource C) introduce delegation challenges.

In a multi-agent chain, Agent B cannot sign a DPoP proof on behalf of Agent A without access to Agent A's private key—and sharing private keys violates the core security principles of DPoP. Because Google's core consumer OAuth endpoint (oauth2.googleapis.com) does not support OAuth 2.0 Token Exchange (RFC 8693), developers building multi-agent or federated cloud workloads should consider Google Cloud Secure Token Service (STS). By routing delegation through STS, the intermediate agent can exchange the upstream token for a new, short-lived federated token cryptographically bound to its own local key pair, preserving the sender-constrained security model across the entire agent chain without key exposure.

See our DPoP Adoption Guide, our DPoP adoption SKILL.md, and improve your platform security today.

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Cost per task: Using cheap classifiers to route across Gemini models and thinking levels

Nvidia replaces potentially exposed SSH keys after detecting unauthorized activity on its Brev cloud platform

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Hackers have struck Nvidia again, this time targeting its BREV cloud development platform, with users reporting unauthorized activity between October 4 and October 20. According to a notification sent to affected customers, some accounts may have been exposed. The company said it had investigated the incident and identified unidentified activity on the platform, adding that information stored on the platform may have been exposed to malicious actors. The said information included the SHH key set up by Brev.

The company has already replaced the affected SHH keys and updated customer machines with new ones but says they found no evidence of improper use. “We are writing to inform you that Nvidia Corporation has experienced a security incident that may have affected the security of your data," the notification reads. “Our analysis determined that your information from our Brev network may have been affected. This SSH key has since been rotated, and your instances have been updated with the replacement key.”

However, the company fell short of explaining how the attackers gained access, or whether anyone attempted to use the compromised data. Moreover, it has not disclosed whether customer data or workloads were accessed. SSH is a protocol used to establish a secure connection between ‘virtual machines’ or remote computers working together. Developers or system administrators can use it to manage remote servers, transfer files, and execute commands without needing physical access to the machine—much like AnyDesk or TeamViewer, but now for developers. Brev uses SSH keys to help customers access these services in the cloud, meaning if attackers access these keys, they could potentially take control of a virtual machine, depending on its specific permissions and other security controls.

And now @NVIDIAAI Brev also got a security breach. At this point in time, every major tech company is going through some sort of security issues - CVEs, stolen keys/API, credentials of users 🥲 https://t.co/3VgUZriQ7lOctober 30, 2026

The incident comes as several firms hosting AI development services and cloud infrastructure grapple with mounting security incidents involving customer credentials. On October 30, cybersecurity firm Wiz released a blog dubbed ‘Black October’ for AI developer platforms. The firm identified at least nine platforms that have had security incidents during the month, including Zeabur, Fireworks AI, Lightning AI, Opencode, Warp, Northflank, among others. As per the firm, the incidents were recurrent, with researchers noting that “all public reports highlight targeted exploitation, explicitly hunting stored environment variables, with a focus on those granted AI access.” Wiz further shared two cases involving compromised Amazon Bedrock credentials where, in one, attackers generated about 800,000 API calls over 30 hours while another key was used to make roughly 1.7 million calls over 13 hours. The combined usage came to a staggering $100,000 at list prices.

Such incidents are not just restricted to Nvidia, with major US banks including JP Morgan, Morgan Stanley and Bank of America restricting Agents from accessing customer accounts earlier this month. Their primary concern is that automated assistants could veer off set instructions and initiate unauthorized approvals. Similarly, the CyberCartel hacking group has previously used Anthropic’s AI tool Claude and Grok’s XAI to conduct attacks on more than 400 Latin American institutions, mostly targeting banks and mobile payments services after attackers obtained customer login credentials. China-linked attackers have also been accused of using AI agents in a ConnectWise supply chain attack that left about 2,250 Microsoft 365 tenants exposed, with some attacks reportedly reaching privileged access in under three hours.

Though updating compromised software may not be enough to eliminate an attacker’s access, Bradford Regeski, a threat intelligence analyst at RH-ISAC, has recommended extensive checks for customers in SHH-related incidents, asking users to “Isolate hosts that installed the compromised extension during the exposure window,” remove unauthorized SSH keys and persistence mechanisms, and rotate credentials and sessions.

#10478. AMVR’s new head strap is my favorite way to keep cool with a Meta Quest 3 or 3S

The upgraded AMVR BS1 head strap includes better ergonomics, a built-in fan, magnetic rechargeable batteries, and a new breathable mesh top support for ultimate comfort.

I don’t know how companies like AMVR and BoboVR keep doing it, but AMVR’s latest head strap is now the most comfortable cooling head strap I’ve ever used for the Meta Quest 3 and Quest 3S. If you’re still using the included cloth head strap or haven’t used a “cooling head strap” before, here’s how it works.

The AMVR BS1 uses a breathable forehead pad attached via magnets to the support bar. Inside that support bar is an adjustable-speed fan, which you can easily adjust using the recessed wheel on the right side of the head strap. This fan keeps sweat down and helps prevent lens fog, two problems that can be a constant annoyance.

The fan is powered by the magnetically detachable, rechargeable 8,800mAh battery pack that mounts on the back of the head strap, helping to even out the weight distribution across the headset. That battery pack will also charge your Quest while playing and adds around 4 hours of additional play time. You can always purchase more packs to keep things going for longer, if you need to.

Lots of padding can be found all around the headset, and the mesh support on top accepts any weight that remains. Every bit of padding is easily removable and washable, so you can keep things clean and tidy (especially if you share your headset with others). You can also get extra pads if you’d rather do it that way.

The back of the head strap cradles your skull with a mix of half supple padding and half grippy silicone. You’ll be double happy there, as the pad on the top part further cushions any headset weight, while the silicone keeps the headset from slipping forward and shifting weight onto your cheeks.

If you don’t need a fan, the BoboVR M3 Pro+ is currently $17 less than the AMVR BS1. I find the BoboVR M3 Pro+’s hybrid design to be slightly more comfortable, but the lack of a fan is a big negative for this particular BoboVR strap.

Once you’ve chosen a head strap, grab an AMVR FC4M face pad to add another layer of comfort to the headset. Like the AMVR BS1’s forehead pad, this face pad attaches to the Quest 3 (NOT the Quest 3S) via magnets and is made of breathable, easy-to-clean material. It’s perfect for staying cool year-round and makes the Quest so, so much more comfortable.

#10477. How Applicant Tracking Systems Read Resumes: 7 Checks Developers Should Make

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A resume that looks perfect on screen can look like a pile of scrambled text to a program. Applicant tracking systems (ATS) and hiring tools do not see your layout. They extract text from a file, guess where the sections are, and store what they find. If that step fails, the best experience in the world never reaches a human reader.

This matters whether you are sending your own resume or building a tool that processes thousands of them. This article walks through seven checks you can make with a few lines of Python, including one script you can run today.

A resume is a document with no guaranteed structure. One person writes "Work History", another writes "Experience", and a third uses a two-column layout that reads out of order. Any tool that processes resumes first has to turn this into predictable fields: contact details, sections, dates and skills.

Thinking in terms of a target schema helps. SitePoint's article on JSON schema design for document AI shows how the structure of the labels you define determines whether a document pipeline works. The same idea applies here: decide which fields you need before you write any extraction code.

Do not judge a resume by how it looks. Judge it by what comes out when you extract the text. SitePoint's walkthrough on building a JSON dataset from PDF documents describes a pipeline that extracts text from PDFs and then cleans up common extraction artifacts. That cleanup step exists because raw extraction is rarely tidy. Read the raw output first, and note what is broken.

PDF files store where characters are placed on a page, not what they mean. The pypdf documentation on extracting text from a PDF says this directly: there is no semantic layer, spacing is hard to get right because text is absolutely positioned, and pypdf is not OCR software, so it will never extract text from images.

Two practical results follow. A resume saved as a scanned image has no extractable text at all. And fancy layouts such as columns, text boxes and tables can come out in the wrong order.

Once you can extract text, a short script can catch the common failures. This one reads a PDF and reports whether it contains text, whether an email address is found, and which standard sections are missing:

Run it on a resume and look at the result. If has_text is false, the file is probably an image. If sections are missing, check whether your headings use unusual names. A passing result does not prove that every ATS will read the file the same way, but a failing result is a clear warning.

Different tools parse differently, so one script is only one opinion. A resume writing service such as ResumeCroc lists a free ATS resume checker among its tools, which is a quick way to see how another tool reads the same file. The linked page is a commercial service page, and every ATS behaves differently, so treat any checker as one data point and compare it with your own extraction results.

Most extraction problems come from layout. Single-column pages, standard headings, plain fonts and real text instead of images give parsers the best chance. Avoid putting contact details in headers or footers, since some tools skip them. If you are building a parser, test files with columns and tables on purpose so you know how your tool fails.

Create a folder of sample resumes: a clean one, a two-column one, a scanned one and one with unusual headings. Run your check against all of them whenever you change your parsing code. This turns "it seems to work" into something you can verify, and it makes regressions obvious.

Do all applicant tracking systems read PDFs the same way? No. Parsers differ in how they handle layout, headers and tables, so test more than one file format and more than one tool.

Is a Word file safer than a PDF? Not necessarily. Both can parse well or badly depending on layout. Test your own file instead of assuming.

Can a script guarantee my resume will pass an ATS? No. A script can find obvious problems, such as missing text or headings, but each hiring tool has its own rules.

Hiring tools read text, not layout. Define the fields you care about, extract the raw text first, understand what PDF extraction cannot do, automate basic checks, compare with an outside checker, keep formatting simple, and test against a small set of sample files. The script above is short, but it moves the question from "does it look right" to "what does a program actually see".

A Redditor by the name of /u/antisocialnae describes their Asus TUF VG258QM 280Hz gaming monitor as "the best $280 [they] ever spent" after it blocked a bullet that passed through the wall of their home, nearly striking them directly as they were playing some "late-night Call of Duty." Asus has responded to the post with a promise to investigate, though no confirmation of a replacement monitor just yet.

It probably doesn't need explaining that the only reason the plastic-and-plastic monitor was able to stop a bullet is that it had already passed through the exterior wall of the home, and that monitors are generally not ideal for sheltering from gunfire.

But even still, it's a wonderful tale, especially given the TUF branding of the display. TUF technically stands for "The Ultimate Force", but most TUF gear is actually subjected to U.S. Department of Defense MIL-STD-810H testing protocols, including shock & drop tests, vibration tests, high-and-low temperature tests, humidity, sand and dust ingress, and more. That sand and dust ingress protection could be important given the sheetrock powder all over the back of his rig.

What Asus doesn't test for is bullet ingress, which would be quite a thing. While it wouldn't actually be that hard to build, it's difficult to imagine the circumstances where someone would specifically select for that when buying a display.

While these events don't seem to be all that common, we've previously covered a couple, including an event where a user's gaming PC deflected a bullet that came through the wall earlier this year. A few years ago, another user's Razer headset deflected a bullet, almost assuredly saving their life. For their part, /u/antisocialnae says that they've been meaning to buy a new display anyway, so this makes the perfect excuse. For now, they apparently have two other Asus monitors to game on.

Incredibly, the monitor actually still works aside from the damaged portion of the LCD. It makes a decent advertisement for Asus TUF monitors, which mostly miss our selections of the best gaming monitors — beaten by pricier rivals — not because of any problems with the quality. In fact, our last TUF Gaming monitor review scored very well and earned itself an Editor's Choice award. Of course, we also don't test for bullet ingress.

#10473. Some Pixel Watch users report a sudden decrease in battery life

A surge of online reports indicates that some users are experiencing a recent increase in battery drain on their Pixel Watch, by as much as 20% every few hours.

Sometimes new software introduces extra stress on device batteries. New processes can take a toll on phone or smartwatch energy at a higher cost than expected, and that might be what’s happening with some Pixel Watch users.

Update 11/2: A Google spokesperson has since reached out, noting it’s working on a fix.

We are aware of a battery drain issue, affecting a small number of Pixel Watch users, and are working to roll-out a fix as soon as possible.

It’s nowhere near a definitive trend, but it’s intriguing to see several new posts on Reddit pop up with the same complaint – their Pixel Watch’s battery is draining faster than normal (via Android Authority). One post has 65 comments, with a healthy number sharing the same concern. One user wrote that their Pixel Watch 2 is getting 12 hours of use on a full charge, instead of the normal 48 hours with AOD off.

The other user claims their Pixel Watch 4 now lasts less than a day. Their normal usage reportedly gets them up to 2.5 days. That’s high for the Pixel Watch 4, but it’s not unheard of. Both of these users dropping by 12 or 24 hours from one charge isn’t nothing.

Interesting, because in the last week or so I have noticed shorter battery life. Typically I was getting 2 full days with my Pixel Watch 4, the last handful of cycles I've noticed the low battery alert coming on maybe 4 - 5 hours earlier.

Sporadic comments echo the same situation, but many report they haven’t noticed a difference. Timing isn’t entirely consistent across comments, either. Some say they started seeing the issue a few days ago, while others say this happened to them a while back.

Still, it might indicate a recent software update is to blame. Google’s recent September 2026 update reaches the Pixel Watch 2 and newer. It adds “Gemini Personalization,” which Google says leverages past conversations and Google apps like Gmail or Calendar to offer personalized responses. It’s also possible that the battery is affected by the recent Google Health update with automatic activity tracking, but that wouldn’t explain why the Pixel Watch 2 is having an issue.

It’s unclear if that’s playing a factor, but Google has yet to officially acknowledge the issue for these users.

What about you? Is your Pixel Watch suddenly dying in the middle of the day? The comments section is all yours.
