A new website seeks to distill over 10,000 public comments across more than 410 local governments, and it's bad news for data center builders.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As more and more AI data centers are being built across the United States, the backlash is starting to gather pace. Following the recent news that Amazon canceled plans to build four new facilities after a public outcry, a new website seeks to detail how sour public sentiment on the subject has turned. American Sentiment is operated by Prophecy Gov, an AI startup based in San Francisco, and it has turned 10,202 public comments at 417 local government meetings from 2026 into a scrollable collection of facts and figures that will give data center builders nightmares, revealing that more than 90% of public comments on data centers are negative.

The website notes that, while public comments don't always match the country's true sentiment, it's clear that the discussion around data center buildouts has reached a "fever pitch" of late. That's something borne out by the data, which takes in 48 states and 417 local governments, with 906 public meetings tracked. Of the 10,202 comments, 90.5% were negative, with just 7% saying positive things about the potential for a new data center being built in their backyard. The remaining 2.5% simply asked questions about the subject. A staggering 73% of meetings had no data center supporters whatsoever. Even those who did support the prospect of a data center being built found themselves outnumbered 6:1, according to the stats.

Predictably, a range of concerns were raised in opposition to data centers, with water use being the most common. In May this year, it was reported that a single AI data center project had consumed 29 million gallons of water. It used so much water, in fact, that local residents noticed a drop in water pressure as a result. A month later, Microsoft claimed its new AI data centers use as little water annually as a restaurant. Beyond water consumption, the impact on local quality of life and the lack of transparency around the project made up the top three complaints. These concerns aren't offset by the benefits of building such facilities, with many people saying that they didn't believe that the claimed benefits would ever come to fruition. Perhaps surprisingly, AI and its potential negative effects weren't mentioned in 4/5 of the comments. Just 21% made any mention of AI as a whole.

Members of the public called out some of AI's biggest players when arguing against data centers, including Google, Amazon, Meta, and Microsoft. Concerns also turned personal, with SpaceX/xAI CEO Elon Musk by far the most mentioned during meetings, followed by OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. Notably, Palantir's Peter Thiel was third, ahead of Meta's Mark Zuckerberg and Jeff Bezos when discussing Amazon.

At a time when the United States may seem more divided than ever, the lack of desire for local data centers appears to be a thoroughly bipartisan issue. Red and blue counties oppose the prospect at almost exactly the same rate, although rural counties raised different issues than urban and suburban ones. The former most commonly worried about the impact on a community's character, farmland, and property rights. Commenters in urban and suburban areas raised the issues of transparency, government distrust, air pollution, and utility bills more often. Pollution is one issue that has been raised previously — a report earlier this month warned that U.S. data centers could wipe out 15 years of progress in greenhouse gas emissions.

There was one group of people largely in favor of new data centers, however. 79% of commenters who said they were union workers were in favor of data centers. Conversely, just 3% of property owners and 3% of residents agreed. Even just 40% of data center workers were in favor of actual data centers, according to the figures.

#10468. Google had a very good, very bad Q3

Good morning! Today I’m reading about the death of the internet, deciding whether to buy a PROLED or many pro LEDs, building all my IKEA furniture with this step-by-step 3D app, trying desperately to get into the Readwise Books early access, and playing an “Enhanced 3D” Command & Conquer: Red Alert on my PC.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Alphabet’s Q3 earnings were interesting. Despite a 25% rise in revenue and a healthy net income of $26 billion, analysts were disappointed with the company’s results, sending the stock down as much as 11% in after-hours trading.

On the plus side, the company’s cloud business is booming, doubling year-over-year revenue to $30 billion as it attracts frontier labs to its data centers. But advertising revenue didn’t meet expectations, and investors appear to be worried that its search advertising business, one of the most efficient sources of recurring revenue in history, is not keeping up with changing user behavior.

As Anthropic gets ready to soar into its IPO later this year, mature businesses like Alphabet, Microsoft, and Amazon are racing to adapt to a market where demand for AI infrastructure is outpacing compute supply. But investors are looking at the capital expenditure tied up in future data center buildouts and getting nervous that, if the bubble bursts, even these well-capitalized companies will take massive hits.

The biggest question surrounding Google’s stock performance is whether it can sufficiently monetize AI-powered search before demand for its traditional search inventory falls off a cliff. As the company sends less and less traffic to the open web, it’s touting the fact that AI Mode and AI Overviews keep people on Google for three times as long as traditional search.

So it’s no surprise that it’s expanding ads in AI Mode to 10 new markets this week, with more to come by the end of the year. While the post was only a few paragraphs long, expanding its AI ad business may end up being one of the company’s most consequential announcements of 2026.

Dune: Part Three debuts in December, and we now have the final trailer that showcases the impending showdown between Muad’Dib and Chani as the former realizes the consequences of his galactic war.

On the gadgety side, there’s another impending showdown brewing between XREAL’s Android XR-powered Aura glasses and Meta’s upcoming VR Glasses.

I liked Jasmine Uniza’s video below comparing the two because, until I watched it, I didn’t realize the extent of the diverging approaches to reality they’re each taking. The Aura glasses are more traditionally translucent and somewhat awkwardly project two OLED screens on top of the dimmed lenses, allowing the outside world to filter through. Meta’s VR Glasses eschew the traditional headset shape and weight of the Quest but block out far more of the outside world and use external cameras, like their predecessors, to pipe in mixed-reality footage.

If you know me at all, you know I love a podcast, and Will Sattelberg was my co-host at the Android Police podcast for three years before he left for 9to5 last year. I’m so excited to be doing this again every week with him, and I hope you’ll tune in to our most recent episode about the death of the web browser and other good news.

When Apple announced the new M5 Max and M5 Ultra configurations of the Mac Studio in August, it said a configuration with 512GB of RAM would be available in October. Today, the company officially revealed the pricing of that option.

If you head to the Mac Studio configurator on Apple’s website, you’ll see the new 512GB upgrade option. As previously announced, the 512GB option is only available with the top-tier M5 Ultra chip that has a 36-core CPU and 80‑core GPU.

The 16GB Raspberry Pi 5 launched at a price of $120 in January 2025.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Raspberry Pi boss Eben Upton has officially announced price rises for some 16GB products. Specifically, the price of the 16GB Raspberry Pi 5 is being raised by $80 to $385, and the 16GB Raspberry Pi Compute Module 5 will go up by $40. In a glimmer of better news, the price of the Raspberry Pi 500+ all-in-one desktop, which also features a 16GB model, is being held, but it was already rather pricey.

Upton framed the decision to raise prices as being due to “the steep rises in the cost of memory over the past two years.” He explained that other computer makers have had to make similar decisions recently. That sounds fair, and he added that Raspberry Pi had been doing what they could to minimize costs passed on to customers.

The price rise of the headline 16GB Raspberry Pi 5 to $385 is effective immediately. As is the $40 increase to the CM5 16GB variant. Of course, both these 16GB devices have seen prior increases in price since being launched at $120 and $135, respectively.

Some moderately good news was that there would be no price increase today for the Raspberry Pi 500+ 16GB all-in-one keyboard wedge computer. Upton didn’t mention that this is already rather expensive at $485, though.

Indeed, there have been several waves of price increases affecting Pis since the components RAMpocalypse began. Only a month ago we saw a similar cost-of-memory post by Upton on the official blog, this time accompanied by news that there were price increases being implemented for the 2GB Raspberry Pi 4 and Raspberry Pi 5 models. Prices of both rose by $12.50, to $67.50 and $77.50, respectively. Perhaps this price gulf between the 2GB and 16GB Pi 5 helps highlight the heavy impact of RAM pricing even more clearly than today’s blog. 1GB devices haven’t seen price rises recently.

The price rises, and comparison in prices between high-and-low memory density models makes it all the more important “to make sure you’re not paying for more memory than you need,” indicates the Raspberry Pi blog about the latest price increases. Sadly for the Pi business, hobbyists and makers already seem to be moving away from the full-sized Pi and CM products for many DIY projects.

Higher pricing is affecting all sorts of computer products, but it might have a particular impact on Raspberry Pi’s business. Generations of this device were explicitly designed around a strict budget constraint, and very famously stuck to a $25–$35 target price for many years.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Now the core Raspberry Pi business is being squeezed by simpler microcontrollers at the low end, where the use of Picos and ESP32 boards have become increasingly popular for small projects. At the other end of the market, with some Pis approaching $400 or $500, makers start to eye entry-level x86 SBCs and mini PCs. Even a cheap mini PC can be powerful with a stronger CPU, GPU, NPU, and more. Mini PCs can win on flexibility and expandability too, and there’s a wide variety to choose from capable of running the most popular and capable OSes and applications.

#10463. Evaluating AI agents: moving past the vibe check

Your agent can loop, burn tokens, or give a completely hallucinated answer and still look fine. A vibe check won't catch that. This post is the playbook behind our AI Agent Clinic episode How to Actually Evaluate (Any) AI Agent: Beyond the Vibe Check, where we build an eval pipeline for a LangGraph agent in 60 minutes. Here's what's inside:

When teams start building an AI agent, testing usually looks the same. You wire up a model, hand it a few tools, run three or four prompts in a terminal, and eyeball the output. If the answer looks reasonable, you ship it.

In practice, this breaks down fast. You tweak a system prompt to fix an edge case in one workflow, and suddenly the agent stops calling a required tool in another.

Worse, agents fail differently than standard software. An agent can get stuck in a retry loop, fire off multiple tool calls, yet still return a polite, plausible-sounding final answer.

In production, that hidden failure is costly:

Integrating a reliable evaluation suite into your development lifecycle can seem like extra overhead during initial prototyping, right up until modifying your system prompt causes unexpected breaking changes. The key is recognizing that evaluation is a progressive journey: any repeatable evaluation system (no matter how simple) is better than relying on ad-hoc spot checks or no system at all. You don't need an elaborate automated grading architecture on Day 0. Instead, start with simple, repeatable checks and evolve your evaluation into a more complex, automated framework as your agent's capabilities and production demands grow.

Establishing a repeatable evaluation system is made substantially easier by integrating these decisions into an evaluation framework. Transitioning beyond simple subjective checks relies on two core pillars: human oversight and targeted tools. In this article, we explore how to set up an evaluation framework using the open-source agent-eval toolkit. We'll demonstrate how combining manually reviewed traces with automated verification helps detect silent regressions early in development and scales seamlessly as your agent transitions to production.

Before writing test code, you need a shared vision of what a good outcome actually looks like. Anchor your tests in critical user journeys (CUJs) rather than abstract benchmarks.

A CUJ defines what a user expects the system to achieve, structured around their persona, goals, constraints, and verification conditions:

Every clause in that statement becomes an evaluation point: Did the agent resolve the correct transaction ID? Did it check the ledger before drafting? Did it map internal IDs back to the external nomenclature?

Defining these expectations upfront forces product stakeholders, downstream users, and engineers to agree on defining how “success” is measured.

You do not need six months of production logs to build your initial golden dataset. Instead, break each CUJ down into concrete scenarios (both happy paths and edge cases) and feed them into a user simulator LLM (we like ADK user simulations). Primed with a persona and behavioral constraints (for example, an impatient analyst giving incomplete ticket numbers, or a developer asking a multi-part debugging question), the simulator mirrors the messy phrasing, typos, and multi-turn complexity of real users.

By testing your agent against a synthetic user and realistic scenarios, you can record comprehensive execution traces (including tool logs) prior to user interaction. This enables you to pinpoint and resolve any performance gaps against your release standards. As live traffic begins to flow, you can progressively replace these synthetic setups with real production traces.

A single top-line score will never tell you why an agent failed. To isolate root causes, layer four types of checks: unit tests, deterministic metrics, LLM-as-a-judge rubrics, and integration tests.

Setting up evaluation metrics involves trial and error, and it is tempting to delete a noisy test or swap out sample queries between runs. Every time you add or remove a test, though, you invalidate your baseline: you cannot tell if a score changed because the agent got better or because the test suite moved. Keep your golden dataset frozen while you tune a feature, change one variable at a time, and only retire a test when you are certain the underlying requirement has changed.

LLMs are notoriously inconsistent at grading qualitative 1-to-5 scales; the boundary between a "3" and a "4" is fuzzy and often shifts from run to run.

Whenever possible, turn your judge rubrics into boolean (pass/fail) questions rather than open-ended rating scales. Rather than asking "How helpful was this answer from 1 to 5?", ask "Did the agent verify the account ID before calling the refund tool? (Yes/No)". (We like Adaptive Rubrics)

A response that passes every quality check is still a production failure if it took 40 seconds, made twelve redundant tool calls, and cost sixty cents per query.

Always evaluate the whole system rather than just the final text. Track latency, token consumption (prompt, reasoning, and completion tokens), API cost, and tool-call counts right alongside answer quality.

Switching orchestration frameworks often forces teams to scrap and rewrite their entire testing suite. While most frameworks emit OpenTelemetry traces, they don’t use the same semantic conventions for tool calls, steps, and tokens. Standardizing your traces around open specifications like OpenInference solves this by decoupling evaluation from your orchestration framework. Tools like the agent-eval toolkit support this natively, with minimal setup.

You do not need to build an observability platform from scratch. You can stand up the evaluation pipeline in a couple of hours using open-source utilities like the agent-eval toolkit which pairs OpenTelemetry trace capture with the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). But building your actual evaluation strategy requires more thought.

Start small and stay close to the raw data:

Why the red pen matters: Manually reading twenty traces feels slow compared to auto-generating a chart, but it saves days of wasted effort. Instead of guessing at hypothetical failure modes and writing generic rubrics that miss the mark, an hour with a red pen shows you how your system actually breaks in reality. Aggregating those notes gives you a high-signal list of the exact deterministic assertions and boolean judge checks you need to build next.

Once you have that initial list of checks, tools like agent-eval toolkit takes over the heavy lifting and standardizes execution. Helping you with things like:

Establish a regular cadence of runs with your eval pipeline:

Once your Day 1 framework is active, optimization becomes a disciplined, mechanical loop:

In a real deployment, reaching production quality often takes 50 to 60 small iterations. You are not rewriting the entire system prompt every morning; you are gradually turning individual knobs. One tweak clarifies a tool parameter description. The next adds a negative constraint. The next adjusts the JSON output schema.

For instance, upon completion of each evaluation run, the agent-eval framework constructs an interactive, all-in-one HTML report. This report aligns pass/fail boolean metrics alongside key operational metrics, including latency, token usage, API expenditure, cache utilization, and detailed tool-call execution paths. Additionally, it incorporates automated Gemini root-cause analyses for failed conversational turns, pinpointing the exact prompt directive or parameter setting that triggered the failure to guide your next adjustment.

That data replaces hunches. A prompt change might feel much smarter in manual spot-checking, only for the test battery to reveal that latency doubled and tool-call hallucinations ticked up by 15%.

The lesson for teams building agents: small, measured changes beat big rewrites every time.

Once you trust the signal coming out of your eval pipeline, turn it into a permanent regression safety net:

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Running three or four prompts in a terminal only shows you the final text response. An agent can loop on retries, hallucinate tool parameters, burn token budgets, or trigger duplicate side effects while still returning a polite answer. Automated trace evaluation checks intermediate tool calls, state transitions, latency percentiles, and API cost alongside answer quality so silent regressions don't ship.

Treat evaluation as a progressive rollout from Day 0 to Day 2 rather than an all-or-nothing jump. On Day 0, break your critical user journeys (CUJs) into a handful of test scenarios and run them through ADK user simulations. Read those first 5 to 20 execution traces with a "red pen" to see where the agent hesitates, picks the wrong tool, or rambles.

On Day 1, turn those red-pen notes into automated checks so you can tune one variable at a time. Once your pass rates stabilize, lock those checks into Day 2 CI/CD pull request gates.

LLM judges don't grade 1-to-5 scales consistently because the boundary between a "3" and a "4" shifts from run to run. With Adaptive Rubrics, you split broad quality goals into strict Yes/No questions like "Did the agent verify the account ID before refunding?" Pair those boolean rubrics with deterministic computation metrics for tool parameters and reference checks, plus trace assertions for latency and token budgets.

Yes, if you decouple your evaluation pipeline from your agent framework by standardizing OpenTelemetry traces on OpenInference semantic conventions. The open-source agent-eval toolkit routes those traces straight to the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). You don't have to rewrite grading code when switching orchestration frameworks.

Once your Day 2 pipeline exports traces and scorecards to BigQuery or Cloud Storage, you can compare live traffic against your pre-production baseline. When users hit edge cases your initial scenarios missed, ambient quality agents like The Outer Loop help you diagnose those failing production traces. You can then promote those real traces straight into your Day 0 golden dataset so your test suite evolves with live usage.

#10460. Samsung’s advanced sleep apnea detection is coming to these Galaxy wearables

Sleep apnea detection was first introduced to Galaxy devices in 2024, after Samsung gained approval from the FDA in the US. Users will need to record at least five hours of sleep twice within 10 days for the sleep apnea detection tool to properly get results on their Galaxy Watch or Ring.

#10459. How to Quickly Check What Your HTML Code Looks Like in a Browser

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Writing HTML is one of the main parts of building a website. It is mainly responsible for webpage structure and content.

A small error can result in layout problems. Oftentimes, you don't notice these issues until you preview the page in a browser. A quick way to ensure that your layout looks the same as you want is to preview your HTML code.

In this blog, we’ll show you easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time. So keep on reading till the end.

The following are two easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time.

This is a simple way to preview your HTML. You don't need a web server to preview your HTML code. You have to follow the following steps.

Open a code editor or simply Notepad on your device. Create a new file and paste your HTML inside it.

Step 2 - Save the File With an HTML Extension

After adding your HTML code in Notepad, save the file as index.html. You have to ensure that the extension is .html, not .txt.

If you are using Notepad on Windows, click on “File” and navigate to the “Save As” option and click it. Then enter index.html as the filename. Make sure that Notepad doesn’t save it as a text document.

Locate the saved file on your device. Double-click to open it in your default browser. The browser will render the HTML elements to show the headings, paragraphs, and buttons on the page, as you can see in the image below.

This is how you can create a simple file to preview your HTML before deploying it. If you see any errors, you can correct them in your file and preview it again.

However, it is worth considering that HTML may not show the complete website behavior alone. The final result can also depend on JavaScript, external CSS, or other resources. You should check those files as well.

This method provides an easy way to preview your HTML code. It involves using a specialized online HTML viewer. This approach is even quicker and doesn't even require you to create a local file first.

Step 1 - Open the HTML Viewer Open your web browser and search for an HTML viewer. You may see multiple tools in the search results. Select one that allows HTML preview online. For demonstration purposes, we are using HTML Viewer by minifier.org.

Once you’ve selected the tool, open it. Navigate to its input box and paste your HTML code into it. We are using the same HTML code from the first example.

You can also upload your file or paste its URL into the tool. After entering your HTML code, click on the “View” button to preview your HTML code.

In the screenshot above, you can see the tool showing the HTML preview on the right side. This tool also gives you the option to edit your HTML code and see the results in real time through the “Auto-update” option.

If the auto-update option is checked, you can see the changes in real time as you edit the code. This makes editing much easier, as you can modify the code and preview the changes immediately without returning to your original file. It also allows you to verify how your layout will appear on desktop, tablet, or mobile. You can check this by clicking on the icons provided in the top right corner of the tool. Once you are satisfied with the output, you can copy or download your edited HTML file easily.

This tool also allows you to test the HTML code of your game or video in real time, as you can see in the screenshot below.

These features make such specialized HTML viewers practical. These are quick and easy to use. However, the features shown above may not be available on all the tools. If you are specifically looking for such features, you can read the tool’s description for these features before using it.

HTML is one of the main components of a website. It defines the structure and content of webpages. Many users need to check how their HTML code will look in a browser to identify potential issues before deployment.

To preview your HTML code, you can use simple methods. One is to paste your code into Notepad and save it as index.html. Then open it in your browser to get a preview of your HTML code.

You can also use a specialized HTML viewer. Such tools can work by just pasting the code into the input box. Once you paste your code, click on the view button to get a preview of your HTML code. Some tools also offer various other features, such as editing, viewing games, and videos in real time.

Both of these methods work fine. The choice depends on the user's preferences and needs.

#10458. Creality K3 Review: It’s a Tool Changer, not a game changer

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Creality’s K3 is a solid entry into the swelling tool changer market, but without the lid and TPU Kit ready at launch, we’re left wondering what else this machine is capable of doing. The overly large spool holders impose a frustrating limit on the brands of filament you can use. Still, this is a good printer for fans of the Creality lineup who only need PLA or PETG.

Top hat and TPU printing kit not available at launch

Spool holders are too large for many 3rd party spools

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Creality has finally caught up with the tool changer fad, presenting us with a speedy, four-nozzle system named “KliTek” mounted on a Creality K3. Tool changers are the way to go, since they do not compromise speed for color and eliminate the need to waste your filament in the form of “printer poop.” The K3 is still programmed to create a purge tower on multicolor prints, but this is a small fraction of the filament wasted using any type of AMS-style color system.

The new KliTek nozzle system supports printing with different-sized nozzles in the same print, which sounds more useful than I actually found practical. Users will also be able to blend colors in Creality Print, a trick picked up from Snapmaker’s open-source Full Spectrum. This method creates the optical illusion of new colors by stacking very thin layers of filament.

The tool-changing system works very well and never dropped or misplaced a tool during the review. Creality has tucked 37 sensors inside the printer, with 12 dedicated to the nozzle swapping process alone. No, I’m not going to name all of them, but they do work. I didn’t get a single bad print during the review, even when running at higher speeds.

However, the K3 looks suspiciously like a Snapmaker U1 clone, departing from the black and grey aesthetic of the K1 and K2 3D printers. It has conveniently placed, side-mounted spool holders that are a tad too large for many 3rd party brands of filament. We’re not sure if they’re trying to herd us towards a walled garden of Creality plastics, or if they simply didn’t check out all the options.

Unfortunately, the Creality K3 needs a lid for printing high-temperature filaments, and this “tophat” was not ready at the time of the review. Creality says the top hat will be packaged with a TPU Kit to enable the printing of super soft materials.

The K3 is currently on preorder with a price tag of $849. The competition for affordable tool changers is pretty stiff, and Creality hasn’t brought anything new to the table, which is keeping the K3 off our list of the very best 3D printers you can buy. But if you’re a fan of the Creality ecosystem, it’s a welcome addition to the fleet.

260 x 260 x 260 mm (10.23 x 10.23 x 10.23 in)

PLA/PETG/PCTG/TPU/CF and GF filled (up to 300 °C)

Double-sided Textured PEI steel flex plate (up to 100 °C)

482 x 420 x 536 mm (18.97 x 16.53 x 21.10 in)

The Creality K3 comes with everything you need to assemble and maintain your printer. The included tool kit has hex keys, side cutters, grease, and a nice nozzle cleaner. There is also a paper copy of the quick installation guide and a 200g spool of white Creality filament.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The Creality K3 has four tools, each with a built-in ceramic heater, nozzle with a hardened steel insert, and filament path. The KliTek system supports printing with different nozzle sizes and different materials in the same print. Like the Prusa/Bondtech INDX, the extruder and cooling fan are mounted on a single toolhead.

The rest of the Creality K3 shares its design with the K2. The printer has a solid metal frame clad in white plastic and a glass door with dampened hinges. The motion system is Core XY, with a linear rail for the X axis and linear rods for the Y axis. The bed rides on four linear rods driven by 2 lead screws belted to a single stepper motor on the bottom of the printer. Parts cooling is provided by a blower fan in the toolhead and a large side-mounted blower fan mounted on the left side of the case. The stock Creality K3 comes with an open top, so chamber cooling is not an issue. Unfortunately, this makes the printer rather loud.

The Creality K3 has a 720p camera located at the top right of the printer, which does a great job providing print monitoring from the device tab in the slicer and the Creality Cloud app. There is also an AI camera that monitors for build plate detection, foreign object detection, and spaghetti detection. There were no false positives during the test.

We did not get to review the special TPU kit, which will allow the machine to run super soft TPU, which is becoming quite trendy for those interested in printing shoes. The kit will replace the CFS Nano material handler with a CFS Flex and add dry boxes for the filament and a special high-flow nozzle. The kit will be bundled with a lid/ tophat with ventilation and air filtration.

The Creality K3 arrives with some assembly required. After removing the packing material, you need to remove the four screws securing the bed for shipping, and there is a nice reminder to do this when first firing up the printer.

The spool holders twist into the printer’s side panels and are numbered to match the filament slots. Two PTFE guide brackets, also numbered, need to be secured to the rear of the printer with M4x6 screws, as does the bracket for the CFS Nano. There is a communication cable that runs from the rear of the printer to the CFS Nano as well.

The tool cables attach to the top of the printer with 2 M4x12 screws each, with the PTFE tubes running to the CFS Nano. The toolhead communication cable is attached with M3x5 screws on the toolhead and M4x8 screws on the right side of the printer. Attaching PTFE guide tubes from the side brackets up to the CFS Nano completes the assembly.

The Creality K3 does an excellent job calibrating and bed leveling as part of its initial self-check. Bed leveling can be repeated at the start of each print.

Loading filament into the Creality K3 is pretty simple. Filament is inserted into a port on the side bracket, which guides it up to the CFS Nano, which does the rest. The filament waits in the tube until called for by a print job. It is then loaded into the tool.

To unload, the tool is selected from the printer’s screen. The printer then cuts the filament and retracts it out of the tool, and it can be manually pulled out of the CFS Nano the rest of the way.

All four filament slots can read RFID tags in the spools to load information for Creality-branded filament. For other filament brands, the printer will beep to remind you to manually enter the filament type and color.

The latest version of Creality Print 7.3 is available from Creality.com. It is based on the Orca Slicer and Ultimaker Cura Engine. The default settings worked well, and there is a new AI assistant mode, which is still labeled as beta. At the time of the review, the only thing missing for the K3 was a button to map the filaments into the slicer. The button is still there for the earlier K-series printers in the slicer. The slicer does have filament mixing for more color combinations.

A link at the top of the page will take you to Creality’s AI Creation tools and Creality Cloud, Creality’s online file-sharing service. If you don’t like the Cloud, you can export files from the slicer and either move them to the printer with a USB stick or move them over your home network by accessing the printer’s FLUIDD interface. To access FLUIDD, you need to find the IP address of your printer and type that into a browser window, followed by:4408 with no spaces.

The Creality K3 comes with a 200g spool of white Hyper PLA. If you want more colors and materials, you should check out our guide to the best filaments for 3D printing for suggestions.

For the first print, I was going to run a pumpkin fidget toy, so I grabbed a spool of Prusa orange and was really quite cross when it didn’t fit on the preposterously large spool holders. I decided right then to mod the spool holder with the Creality Pika scanner. I took the scan into Tinkercad and reduced the diameter of the spool holder, then ran the new spool holders in Inland basic white using a .2 layer height and an average print speed of 170mm/s. The print took 8 hours and 34 minutes to complete. I do not usually mod printers while reviewing them, but Prusa Orange is perfect for pumpkins.

Below is the original spool holder (left) and the modded version (right).

For my second print, I did a pumpkin fidget toy I found on MakerWorld using Prusa orange PLA, Elegoo green PLA Pro, and Polymaker black HT-PLA Pro. I used a .2 mm layer height and printed with an average speed of 160mm/s; the print took a little over six hours. It came out nice without a hint of overextrusion. I liked it so much I decided to print it again scaled up to 200%. Using the same settings, the larger print took 16 hours and 28 minutes and is also very nice with a good fidget action.

For PETG, I printed a plate of 3 flexy geckos I found on Creality Cloud using Prusa Carmine Red, Prusa Sky Blue, and Bambu Lab basic white PETG. Using a .2mm layer height and running at 180mm/s, the print took 5 hours 45 minutes to complete. The geckos are smooth and clean, plus all of the links work perfectly with no stringing or overextrusion present.

The Creality K3 did not have its special “flex” CFS at the time of review, but I decided to try TPU anyway. I printed some omni wheels for a Death Racer we’re working on in both hard TPU for MFS from Inland and some 95A Inland rainbow TPU. The stock CFS Nano had no trouble pushing these, and the toolhead extruder had no problem with it. It did well. Trouble came when I tried some 90A TPU from Bambu Lab. I’d stick to the harder stuff until the TPU kit is ready.

I did get good prints with the black TPU for AMS and the rainbow 95A filaments. The prints are omni-wheel rollers from the Deathracer project of Michael Baddeley. Both sets of eight rollers were printed at a .2mm layer height at an average speed of 50mm/s and each took a little over 4 hours to print. There was a tiny bit of stringing, but otherwise the rollers are perfect.

The Creality K3 is a terrific printer that should become more capable with accessories like the tophat and TPU kit in the future. The oversized spools are a little annoying, but easily fixed. The only thing I worry about is Creality’s track record of releasing a printer, then introducing a better “pro” or “max” model within four months. I wouldn’t be surprised if we get a six- or eight-color K3 variant before next spring.

At its prelaunch price of $849, it’s $100 more than a similarly equipped, slightly larger Snapmaker U1, which is a tough place to be. It is also the same price as the fully enclosed Flashforge Creator 5 Pro, which we didn’t review, but we did check out the budget model Flashforge Creator 5, which is currently available for $699.

Data centers not owned by the Russian state will have 24 hours to provide access to 10% of their capacity if they are called upon.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Owners of non-state data centers in Russia have been told to hold back 10% of their capacity, ready to aid the country's state-owned IT infrastructure should the need arise. That's the message from President Vladimir Putin, with the aim of quickly unlocking additional data center compute capacity in the future if it becomes needed by the state, reports Reuters.

This comes as Russian data centers have become targets for Ukrainian drone attacks in recent weeks, causing huge amounts of damage and affecting state-owned internet services. In early October, drones struck Russia's largest data center in what was the second such attack in as many days. The huge 63-megawatt facility was so badly damaged that multiple modules had to be removed from service completely. At the time, users reported problems with Yandex Mail, Disk, Documents, Pay, and Market despite Yandex claiming that key services remained up and running. Two weeks later, Russia's finance ministry's data center was damaged after Ukrainian drones targeted the facility. It was reportedly home to Russia's tax databases as well as other state registry data.

Now, Putin has told the Digital Development Ministry to inventory all available data center capacity and floor space at privately owned facilities throughout the country. A new government commission will be given the power to order the release of reserved data center capacity. Once ordered to do so, third-party data center owners will have 24 hours to comply. The temporary use of data centers located outside Russia has also been mooted, pending the approval of Russia's Federal Security Service. (FSB)

It isn't just drones that have been targeting Russian data centers. On the same day that news broke of the country's plan to siphon data center capacity from third parties, the Russian Federal State Statistics Service admitted that its servers were hit by cyberattacks that saw 23TB of data stolen. It's reported that the haul includes new data on Russian wartime losses marked “for official use only.”

Data centers have become a target for Russia, too. It bombed Ukrainian facilities in September of this year, resulting in the loss of internet connectivity for 100,000 households. At the time, a government official noted that Ukraine's internet network is highly decentralized before admitting that users should expect local disruptions to service.

#10450. Google Photos update fixes the bug that hid edited pictures

A bug in Google Photos has left edited pictures invisible to other Android apps, but Google has now rolled out a fix.

Since roughly July/August of this year, editing a picture in Google Photos has left Android users with the bizarre side effect of making that image unavailable to other apps. After saving the edit, the picture would vanish from the image picker in apps like Telegram, WhatsApp, and more. A workaround to this was to save the image as a copy rather than the original, but it’s frustrating nonetheless.

Google has thankfully, finally fixed this issue.

The fix is apparently in Google Photos v7.97, Artem Russakovskii (owner of APKMirror) mentions, and we’ve been able to confirm the hidden image behavior no longer occurs on v7.98 either. The company acknowledged the problem last month and confirmed a fix would be coming.

The exact cause remains unclear at this point, but that doesn’t matter now seeing as the problem is fixed. If you’re still seeing the bug, let us know in the comments below.

#10448. Tachyum, developer of heavily-delayed universal Prodigy processor, voluntarily files for bankruptcy

Update 11/2/2026 3:00 PM ET: As it turns out, the ultimate parent company and holder of all Tachyum intellectual property is Cayman Islands-based Tachyum Ltd., which controls U.S.-based Tachyum Inc., which in turn controls Slovakia-based Tachyum s.r.o. that is responsible for the lion’s share of Tachyum’s development of the Prodigy universal processor. This means that while Tachyum Inc. and Tachyum s.r.o. can be declared insolvent and liquidated, Tachyum Ltd. can continue operations, establish new entities and continue developing Prodigy.However, the Cayman Islands company holding Prodigy-related patents is itself the target of liquidation proceedings. Therefore, the Slovak subsidiary’s bankruptcy cannot necessarily protect Prodigy’s intellectual property from creditors. If Tachyum Ltd. is liquidated, the control over the Prodigy patents could pass to court-appointed liquidators, who could sell them to repay creditors. While the current Tachyum management could buy Prodigy’s patents back cheaply, they would have to compete with other interested buyers and satisfy the applicable sale requirements. In any case, this could be the end of the road for Tachyum.

Tachyum, the company behind the overpromised and overhyped universal Prodigy processor, has filed for bankruptcy in Bratislava, Slovakia, reports Forbes (as observed by @System360Cheese). The filing follows a court order initiating bankruptcy proceeding after the company repeatedly missed its development and production milestones — but there are still theoretical chances that development of the processor will continue.

Despite eight years of development and numerous promises, Tachyum has yet to demonstrate working Prodigy silicon. Tachyum introduced Prodigy in 2018, targeting a 2019 tape-out and 2020 commercial availability. But the company repeatedly postponed the processor's launch, first to 2021, then to 2022, 2023, 2024, and eventually 2025.

Meanwhile, Prodigy's specifications changed considerably along the line: The original 64-core design evolved into a 128-core processor manufactured on a 5nm-class process technology, then a 192-core chip with 16 DDR5 memory channels. In late 2025, Tachyum announced yet another radical redesign: a chiplet-based architecture with 1,024 cores, 24 DDR5 memory channels, and TDP of 1,600W manufactured using TSMC's 2nm-class node. The redesign would require substantial additional development work and would have delayed commercial availability until 2030 or beyond.

According to court documents cited by Forbes, Tachyum Slovakia itself filed for bankruptcy on October 22, 2026, without waiting for a petition from creditors seeking to recover unpaid debts. The Bratislava III Municipal Court subsequently initiated bankruptcy proceedings after determining that the company's petition met the legal requirements. Under Slovak law, a company that voluntarily files for bankruptcy is presumed insolvent, according to Forbes, so technically Tachyum Slovakia is bankrupt.

If the court formally declares Tachyum bankrupt, a court-appointed trustee will take control of the company's assets, which could then be sold to repay creditors. We do not know at this point what these assets are, but theoretically they may include intellectual property related to its Prodigy processor, patents, and other technologies in addition to hardware used for development. Unless Tachyum secures additional financing or finds a buyer that can fund further development, work on Prodigy could effectively end.

Bankruptcy proceedings against Tachyum's Slovak entity do not necessarily mean that its affiliated companies elsewhere will also cease operations. Furthermore, the company's IP could survive under new ownership (if the company finds a buyer, or transfers IP ownership to another entity), which could theoretically let Prodigy development to continue. Nevertheless, the bankruptcy filing casts serious doubt on Tachyum's ability or intention to bring its long-delayed processor to market.

Theoretically, Tachyum's owners could potentially use bankruptcy proceedings to shed the company's debts, acquire its intellectual property through another legal entity, and resume Prodigy development. There are other options too.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Although most of Tachyum development activities are concentrated in Slovakia, Tachyum Slovakia is a subsidiary of the U.S.-based Tachyum Inc. according to Dennik N. If the American parent company owns the Prodigy IP, while the Slovak subsidiary employs engineers, conducts development, and accumulates debts, bankruptcy could enable the group to eliminate a substantial portion of those liabilities without losing its most valuable technological assets. In short, Tachyum's Slovak subsidiary could disappear along with much of its unpaid debt, while the American parent retains Prodigy IP and attempts to commercialize it somehow in the future. If the Slovak subsidiary owns the IP, the American parent could theoretically buy it from the bankruptcy estate and attempt to commercialize it eventually. Meanwhile, there is another complication: The American parent has reportedly faced pressure from its own creditors, some of whom have requested its liquidation, according to Dennik N. In this case, the destiny of the Prodigy IP is completely unclear.

For now, the future of Prodigy may depend on who actually owns Prodigy's intellectual property — Tachyum Inc. in the U.S. or Tachyum Slovakia — and whether the American subsidiary continues to exist or gets liquidated. The answer could determine whether this bankruptcy represents the end of Prodigy or a restructuring of the Tachyum group with the aim to continue development.

#10443. AI Testing Tools: Benefits, Limitations, and Best Practices

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every vendor says the same thing: buy our AI, and testing gets easy. Don't believe it. AI testing tools do help, though. Some teams save real hours with them, while others waste a quarter and a budget. The difference usually comes down to how they start.

They support your QA team by writing test cases, running them, spotting failures and suggesting repairs. Some tools read a requirements document. Others watch real users click around and then replay what they did. Playwright, for instance, now ships built-in test agents: a planner that explores your app and writes a test plan, a generator that turns the plan into test files, and a healer that runs the suite and tries to repair failing tests.

Old-school automation needs a person to write every script. Then a developer moves one button and the whole thing falls over. Somebody loses a morning to repairs, and QA engineers hate that part of the job.

Quality is the big worry. Generated tests can look clever and prove nothing. A tool might confirm that a checkout page opens and then mark checkout as covered, but opening a page is not testing it. Even Playwright's documentation says generated tests may contain initial errors and that you should review what the healer changes before accepting it. A person has to read the output every time.

Business logic is the next problem. The tool has no idea that your refund window dropped from 30 days to 14, and it cannot feel that a signup form is confusing. Intent lives in people's heads.

Coverage numbers can mislead. A dashboard says 90%, and the room relaxes. Meanwhile, the odd edge cases nobody thought of sit untouched. Bugs do not read dashboards.

Check fit first. Does it cover web, mobile and API tests, and does it work with your stack? Ask before any sales call, not after. Then test its honesty: when something fails, a good tool tells you why. A vague red mark costs your team time because nobody can verify it. Read the pricing page slowly, too, since some vendors charge per seat and some per test run.

Finally, throw your ugliest feature at it. Demos use tidy little apps, but your product has pop-ups, slow pages and odd corners. Watch how the tool handles those.

Expecting instant results. People switch the tool on and wait for magic. What they get is noisy reports and grumpy engineers. Some managers even cut QA headcount right away, which is a bad idea: somebody has to tune the tool and handle the cases it cannot. Good testers become more valuable, not less.

Skipping training. Your team needs to learn how the tool behaves. Two short workshops and a shared doc go a long way.

Trusting machine-written text without a second look. AI tools now draft test descriptions, bug reports and release notes too. If your team wants to know how much of a document was machine-generated, an AI-content checker such as ZeroGPT can give a rough signal. Treat the result as a hint rather than proof, because such detectors make mistakes and publish their own accuracy claims.

Agents are the obvious next step. Instead of running fixed scripts, they poke around your app like a curious user, clicking, typing and reporting anything strange. Early versions already exist, and they are rough but improving.

Editors will change as well. Your code editor may soon suggest a test the moment you finish a function, and some bug reports may arrive with a fix attached. The wall between development and QA is getting thin.

Yes, but carefully. AI testing tools will not replace your QA team. They take the boring work off testers' hands and spot patterns people might miss, but they still need a human to say "that result is wrong." Start small, measure what happens and keep your testers close to the tool. Do that, and you will ship faster without losing control.

OpenAI plans community agreements and infrastructure commitments as it prepares to rebrand data centers amid growing public opposition

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

OpenAI is reportedly preparing a major public relations campaign to influence how Americans view artificial intelligence, including a proposal to rename its data centers as “AI Compute Campuses,” Axios reports. The initiative comes ahead of the November 3 U.S. midterm elections and forms a broader effort to influence the debate around AI before the 2028 presidential race. Chris Lehane, OpenAI’s chief global affairs officer, noted that the midterms would be “a bit of a movie trailer for what you’re going to see in 2028.” He expects AI to be a major issue for presidential candidates as the technology raises concerns over its impact on jobs, the economy, and the environment.

The proposed terminology change by OpenAI has been ongoing since August as the company focuses on promoting safeguards for the powerful technology, which Lehane described as “speed limits.” The proposed AI compute campuses is particularly interesting as it comes as opposition to large data centers accelerates across the U.S. Data centers require enormous amounts of electricity and water, with nearby communities raising concerns about noise, air pollution, rising utility bills and limited public consultation. OpenAI hopes to address these concerns through community agreements and a different way of presenting the infrastructure. “Compute campuses better describe the role of the infrastructure we are rebuilding for AI," a company spokesperson separately stated. He added that these projects represent long term investments in computing, energy infrastructure, and skilled jobs.

The latest proposal follows similar efforts by other technology companies to improve their relationship with communities hosting their infrastructure. In January, Microsoft announced a community-first approach, promising to cover electricity cost increases associated with its data centers, and invest in local infrastructure and jobs. However, while changing the name may be easier, changing public opinion may bring some headaches. A Redfin report in May found that 47% of Americans surveyed are opposed to having a new data center built in their community. Another report in August detailed how negotiations between developers and local authorities frequently take place behind non-disclosure agreements, leaving residents without access to important project information.

The resistance has also affected actual construction plans. At least 19 Michigan municipalities have already moved to restrict new data center developments after OpenAI and Oracle’s $16 billion Stargate project went ahead with their project in Sanile township despite local opposition. Meanwhile, Amazon abandoned plans for four data centers in northern Virginia last month following community pushback.

OpenAI is not alone in trying to reshape the language surrounding AI. President Donald Trump has previously pushed for terminology change, directing Federal agencies and urging AI companies to replace artificial intelligence with ‘super intelligence.’ Trump announced the change in late September arguing that the word "artificial" made the technology sound "fake." His administration subsequently instructed state department employees to use super intelligence or SI in official directives, websites, reports and policy documents.

Some technology executives have also embraced the terminology. Nvidia CEO Huang has previously referred to data centers as "superintelligence factories" while Elon musk announced plans to rename his AI company SpaceXAI to SpacexSI last month. Meta, meanwhile, established Meta superintelligence labs in 2025, and Microsoft has also assembled a team focused on developing superintelligence. OpenAI’s proposed terminology will now face a more difficult test than simply finding a name as communities will still want answers about the raised issues. Lehane, however, acknowledged that public scrutiny is part of the process saying, “Communities should hold companies responsible and accountable so they really and truly get a transformative benefit from these projects.”

Amazon’s closure email cites a verification process and offers no reason.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AI creator Nick Saraev says on X that Amazon closed his account after he tried to buy seven Nvidia DGX Spark AI mini-PCs. The closure email cites a verification process and gives no reason. Epic CEO Tim Sweeney chimed in that his regular orders would go through, then get canceled before arrival, after he tried to buy two GPUs.

Update: Amazon just closed my account.Buying even modest amounts of compute immediately puts you on a watchlist. Be careful!November 1, 2026

Saraev puts it plainly that “Amazon just closed my account” and that “Buying even modest amounts of compute immediately puts you on a watchlist.” He was “4 orders deep,” and Amazon will no longer allow him to buy DGX Sparks.

The shared screenshots indicate those orders were all canceled, with each stating, “Your order was canceled. You have not been charged for this order.” Although he does not give a unit count, the screenshots indicate he tried to buy seven DGX Sparks in total. All show the same listing, with 128GB of memory and 4TB of storage.

Amazon’s support account did respond to his earlier post, about three hours before his closure post, offering a review. The shared email shows that Amazon had “made the final decision” to close his account after review; no reason was given. However, it did mention “previous attempts to verify your account,” and he says he did go through the verification process, but seven DGX Sparks is a significant amount that might draw suspicion. Either way, Amazon’s Conditions of Use reserve the right to “refuse service, terminate accounts” or “cancel orders in its sole discretion.”

He’s not alone in running into trouble with the company. Sweeney says that “Amazon didn’t cancel my account but continued accepting all my regular orders and canceling them just prior to arrival dates, after I tried to buy two NVIDIA GPUs. Rather bizarre.” Nvidia GPUs are often used for local inference. Saraev replied that everyone would “appreciate a bit more transparency.”

Also, in September, Dracones89 on the Nvidia developer forum shared that one Amazon account was hit with “suspicious activity locks” while trying to buy a pair of Asus GX10s; the user ended up with two DGX Sparks from Micro Center instead. Another X user, @PurzBeats, wrote, “They canceled the order when I tried to buy my 5090, twice.” This is a different trajectory than the OpenClaw days of Mac minis, when buyers faced long delivery waits rather than canceled orders or closed accounts.

With local inference remaining of high interest to many, particularly AI influencers, the event is potentially disturbing, especially after recent comments about self-owned DRAM by OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. While this time it was a guy who helps entrepreneurs and small businesses implement AI and claims 500K on YouTube and 700K on Instagram, the slope is slippery, although Saraev’s orders are hardly typical.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The 128GB DGX Spark has an MSRP of $6,950, but it may be found at 20% or more over that: two Amazon listings showed $8,499.99 and $9,499.99 when we checked today, which would put seven at roughly $59,500 to $66,500. Both showed Prime delivery tomorrow, so the hardware is still on sale there.

Hardware has been climbing in price, largely due to rising memory costs. As open-weight models have become more popular, so has local AI inference. The combination may make retailers uneasy, as you’re dealing with high ticket prices and there has been uncertainty about the safety of models as they have improved, with open-source AI agent tools reportedly being used in multiple recent cybersecurity incidents.

Micro Center, a different retailer, required one buyer to sign an Advanced Computing Product Purchaser Declaration with photo ID, end user, and an install location, just for a workstation with an RTX 5090. This form specifically forbade exporting or reselling the hardware, which might give a little insight into Amazon’s reasoning, too. Amazon’s conditions also say customers “must comply with all U.S. or other export and re-export restrictions.” China and the U.S. remain locked in a fight over exports of AI hardware.

Amazon hasn’t explained itself, but the attention this is getting may motivate a more detailed response. Right now, it’s a warning signal to users looking to buy significant amounts of hardware for AI work, at least from traditional online retailers. Brick-and-mortar stores, like Micro Center, may crack down further to prevent workarounds designed to export the hardware illegally.

#10433. Google stock drops amid AI fears; AI Mode has ‘lower monetization,’ getting more ads

Google is bringing ads to AI Mode globally as it admits that the new take on Search brings “lower monetization,” all while $GOOG stock is seeing a fairly major drop.

Alphabet’s Q3 earnings call yesterday saw Google’s Philipp Schindler acknowledging that AI Mode, the company’s Gemini-powered new take on Search, has “lower monetization” compared to traditional Search. So the “strong growth” of AI Mode didn’t have as much of a positive impact on Google’s bottom line simply because the mode doesn’t generate as much revenue.

…It’s worth noting that the strong growth in AI Mode queries had an impact because of the lower monetization we’re seeing in AI Mode. To be clear, we feel very good about our trajectory here. Our ad experiments have shown promising results and we’re now scaling ads within both AI Overviews and AI Mode to more users and more queries…

In a blog post the same day, Google announced that AI Mode would be getting ads in global markets. US users will also start to see “multiturn ads” in follow-up queries.

Our research shows that people are comfortable with ads in AI Mode and find them highly relevant. In fact, click-through rates are already similar between sponsored and organic product list items within AI Mode.

Now we’re scaling ads in AI Mode in Australia, Canada, India, Kenya, Malaysia, New Zealand, Nigeria, Pakistan, the Philippines, and Singapore. In Q4 2026, we plan to expand to additional locales, including Brazil, Mexico, and the United Kingdom.

Meanwhile, $GOOG stock dipped by 27 points around the time of the earnings call and has continued to drop on October 29. It currently sits around $280, just a few points higher than a March 30 low of $273.14. The 12% drop reduces Google’s market cap by over $500 billion. NBC News points out that it is the second-biggest drop behind Nvidia’s $589 billion drop in 2025.

The drop is not directly due to AI Mode itself, but is attributed to weaker-than-expected earnings in Q3. Ad revenue from Search still makes up roughly half of Google’s overall revenue.

Our Daniel Bader expands on this in the latest issue of Inbox, our twice-weekly free newsletter:

…But investors are looking at the capital expenditure tied up in future data center buildouts and getting nervous that, if the bubble bursts, even these well-capitalized companies will take massive hits.

The biggest question surrounding Google’s stock performance is whether it can sufficiently monetize AI-powered search before demand for its traditional search inventory falls off a cliff. As the company sends less and less traffic to the open web, it’s touting the fact that AI Mode and AI Overviews keep people on Google for three times as long as traditional search.

#10432. Testing JSON-LD Like an API: 7 Checks Before Your Next Release

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A content editor changes an article’s headline. The page updates immediately, but its JSON-LD still contains the previous title. A redesign introduces a new author component while an old plugin continues publishing a different author name in the page source.

Neither failure necessarily produces a JavaScript error. Both leave machines and readers with conflicting descriptions of the same page.

For developers, the useful approach is to treat JSON-LD as a maintained output of the application, with a data source, validation rules, and regression tests. This guide uses a blog template to show how. You will need basic JavaScript knowledge and a modern Node.js installation for the runnable tests.

Requests for search improvements often arrive as broad outcomes: improve discoverability, clarify entities, or support answer engines. These need to become implementation requirements before development starts.

For example, AEO directory includes structured data within descriptions of broader optimization services. For an engineering team, the actionable deliverable is a page representation whose types, properties, and values can be checked against the published content. I personally used this , and adding this because this can help other developer's and designer for optimization and get higher search result's.

For a blog template, define these acceptance criteria:

Keep implementation success separate from search outcomes. Google’s AI features guidance says there is no special Schema.org markup required for its AI search features. Correct markup also does not guarantee rich results or inclusion in an AI-generated answer.

Avoid separate, manually maintained copies of the headline, author, and publication date. Both the visible template and metadata builder should consume the same published record.

Save the following as article-schema.mjs. The builder assumes an upstream content-validation step has already checked required strings, timestamps, and approved HTTPS URLs.

This is a deliberately small example, not a complete rich-result eligibility checklist. Add relevant images and other supported properties when the real page supplies them. Consult the Schema.org BlogPosting definition and Google’s Article documentation for their respective vocabularies and search guidance.

Use a consistent canonical-URL policy before building the identifier: normalize the intended hostname, trailing-slash convention, and query parameters. Do not generate a fresh UUID for the same article on every request.

SitePoint’s guide to implementing JSON-LD on modern websites covers embedding approaches across common stacks.

Valid JSON is not automatically safe to insert into a raw HTML script element.

A CMS value containing can terminate the element at the HTML-parser level, even when the value belongs to a JSON string. Concatenating a headline directly into a script tag is particularly risky.

Append this helper to article-schema.mjs:

JSON serialization handles quotes and backslashes. Replacing literal less-than characters prevents an embedded closing-script sequence from appearing in the resulting text. The Next.js JSON-LD guide documents this approach for script embedding.

Use the serialized result as the content of a script element with type="application/ld+json". Follow your framework’s documented embedding mechanism so the JSON is neither double-encoded nor inserted through an unrelated HTML context.

This helper is specific to JSON-LD script content. It is not a general-purpose HTML sanitizer, URL validator, or substitute for validating CMS data.

The tests should catch changes that would make metadata misleading or unsafe, not merely confirm that an object exists.

Save this file as article-schema.test.mjs beside the builder:

These are unit tests for the builder and serializer. They do not prove that a template embeds the script correctly or that the deployed page is eligible for a search feature. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing helps distinguish those responsibilities.

A passing builder test cannot detect a page component reading a different CMS field.

Add a browser-level check against a controlled article fixture. Inspect its visible heading, byline, dates, canonical link, and JSON-LD together. For this template, assert that the intended article entity appears once and matches those visible fields.

Do not assert that the entire page contains exactly one JSON-LD script. A site may legitimately publish organization, breadcrumb, and article entities separately. Instead, identify the relevant entity by its @id and detect conflicting definitions.

Include these failure cases in the test plan:

Prefer generating metadata from the content loaded during server rendering or static generation when that fits the application. If it is injected client-side, inspect the rendered result and test navigation explicitly. Avoid making essential metadata depend on a user interaction.

No single tool establishes syntax, semantic accuracy, search eligibility, and content consistency at once.

Test both representative code samples and deployed URLs. A validator cannot confirm the truth of an author name simply because the property is syntactically valid.

Google’s general structured-data guidelines also require markup to represent the page accurately. Treat validation warnings as findings to investigate; do not add invented reviews, dates, or attributes merely to improve a tool’s output.

Ownership matters after the first release. Decide which application layer generates article markup, who maintains its mapping, and which publishing events invalidate cached output.

When an editor changes a headline, the visible page and JSON-LD should be rebuilt or refreshed together. Updating one while serving the other from an older cache creates a mismatch even if both implementations are individually correct.

Avoid setting dateModified to the current time on every deployment. A CSS update or infrastructure rebuild does not necessarily mean the article changed. Read the timestamp from the editorial record instead.

Before shipping a template change, run the builder tests, inspect one rendered page per affected template, and check representative deployed URLs. Keep a small fixture set that includes optional fields, long text, translated content, and script-like strings.

A reliable JSON-LD implementation has a traceable relationship with published content. When someone changes the page, its machine-readable description should change predictably—and the test suite should expose the cases where it does not.

#10431. Google Home update should make Gemini stop saying ‘Sorry, I didn’t understand’ so much

Google’s latest Gemini for Home update targets a few issues, like one where speakers randomly blurt out “I didn’t understand” when no one asks.

The second October Gemini for Home update was released by Google today. It bundles a few fixes with a lengthy section in the changelog dedicated to the Google Home app.

I would probably tell Gemini, "Here, do you understand this?" as I threw it out the window

But there are six individual changes for Gemini within the Home app. One fix stands out the most, though. According to the update post, Google was aware that Home and Nest speakers/displays were facing a bug that would prompt them to say “Sorry, I didn’t understand” at random. That would happen unprompted, which is understandably a little unsettling. That strange bug should be fixed in this new update.

Further down, two fixes target Gemini’s ability to understand users on Google Home devices. For one, it would misunderstand requests to move music from one room to another. In other cases, it would incorrectly adjust sleep, media, or normal timers instead of the requested type. Both of those features will reportedly work as intended going forward.

The changelog includes a couple of other adjustments, which should make for an overall less stressful experience.

Updates should be live for users starting today, though it might take some time for them to roll out to everyone. Hopefully, Gemini will only let you know it doesn’t understand if it genuinely doesn’t understand.

#10426. Reddit will soon be a ‘logged-in-only’ website, forcing you to have an account

Reddit is shifting to a “logged-in-only experience,” the company confirmed during its latest earnings call.

While the timeline is vague at this point, Reddit has confirmed that it will be locking down the site for users without an account, also impacting usage in private browsing modes. CEO Steve Huffman explains in a shareholder letter and a post on Reddit that the website will becoming a “logged-in-only experience.”

Moving to primarily logged-in will ensure our users have the best Reddit experience possible, and can find the content and communities most relevant to them. It is better for our business because we will have more signals for ads targeting, and being logged-in leads to higher engagement generally. It also allows us to protect access to Reddit’s extremely valuable corpus of data, and helps us better adapt to emerging regulations around the world.

In Q4 we’ll begin fully testing this in select markets outside the U.S. The results of those tests will inform an optimized version of logged-in-only Reddit globally.

The change also comes as Reddit says that search referral traffic was “down meaningfully” in Q3 2026, mainly in regards to logged-out traffic. “Down meaningfully” was later detailed to mean that traffic “dropped by more than 1 million referrals on average per week.”

"It also allows us to protect access to Reddit’s extremely valuable corpus of data" What he means is they want to prevent access to Reddit's data, unless they get paid for it. What has always annoyed me about Reddit is that they think it is their data. Almost everything on the site is generated by users, it's their data, not Reddit's.

As for private browsing, Huffman says that Reddit has “rebuilt and [is] re-releasing incognito mode in our app.”

Details on what will and will not be available to logged-out users are unclear at this time, though Huffman says that “the front page being available logged-out is useful and important,” adding that “visitors arriving from search or other external sources to see the content that’s being linked to.” He further implies that AI scraping is certainly a major part of this decision, saying that “the vast majority of unauthorized access to Reddit (basically the scraping and stealing of Reddit’s data) happens through our logged-out experiences.”

This comes shortly after Reddit rolled out a new personalized “For You” homepage.

Just 128MB of RAM is enough to boot Windows 11.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

NTDEV, the popular developer behind the Tiny11 project, has once again pushed the limits of just how little hardware is required to run Windows 11. In a video shared on YouTube, the developer claims to have successfully booted the latest Windows 11 26H2 on a system with just 128MB of RAM. Despite severe memory constraints, the heavily stripped-down installation manages to load the Desktop and File Explorer, access the Start menu, and even run the classic Notepad app.

While it is an impressive achievement, the developer acknowledges that the system is "painfully slow," making this more of a technical experiment than a practical way to run Windows 11 on older hardware. According to Microsoft’s official requirements, Windows 11 requires at least 4GB of RAM, meaning NTDEV's latest experiment uses just 3.125% of the recommended minimum. The developer has not shared a detailed breakdown of every component removed or disabled to achieve the result, but the project builds on years of experimentation with stripped-down Windows installations.

This is not NTDEV's first attempt at pushing Windows 11 beyond its usual hardware limitations. Back in 2023, the developer managed to get the operating system running with only 176MB of RAM by extensively trimming its system components, drivers, and background services. That experiment relied on an IDE storage interface to reduce the number of drivers required, and sacrificed the standard Windows desktop environment.

Last month, the developer showcased another interesting experiment that squeezed an entire Windows 11 installation into a CD-ROM-sized ISO file. The experimental build created using the developer's upcoming Pico11 tool weighs in at 699,811,840 bytes and retains a functional Windows desktop. The stripped-down version of Windows 11 successfully boots into the desktop environment and runs basic applications such as Calculator, Settings, Notepad, and Paint. The test was conducted on a virtual machine with 4GB of allocated RAM, with the operating system using around 883MB.

Such experiments are a great way to showcase just how much of Windows can be stripped away while still retaining some of its core functionality. At the end of the day, they are primarily technical demonstrations with the intention of exploring the limits of Microsoft's operating system rather than providing practical alternatives to a standard Windows installation. Stripping away essential components, drivers, and background services comes with significant trade-offs, including reduced functionality, compatibility issues, and severely degraded performance.
